  - **★★ TWO PASSES AND TWO REFUSALS ON *DIFFERENT* CONDITIONS — the first evidence the gate DISCRIMINATES rather than merely GATES.** An inert gate and a working gate both look like "nothing bad posted"; that is exactly how ·3a shipped. What separates them is a refusal that names a condition **and a different refusal that names a different one.**
    - **Register 04 (footing bait) REFUSED on condition 2** — verbatim: *"The register doesn't foot: gross $4,000.00 less withholdings $850.00 is $3,150.00, but it states net pay of $3,200.00."*
    - **Register 05 (out of norms) REFUSED on condition 5** — verbatim: *"Gross pay of $12,000.00 is well outside this company's usual $4,000.00 a run."*
    - **AND THE ATTRIBUTION IS PINNED, NOT ASSUMED:** 08/31 against a period end of 08/28 sits **inside the 7-day grace by design**, so the second refusal could only have been about norms. The condition was identified by elimination on the live artifact, not read off the copy.
    - **POST-CHECK:** `source='payroll'` and `entry_date >= 2026-08-01` returns **exactly two rows**, 08/14 and 08/28, both gross 4,000. **Nothing bad posted.**
  - **★★ THE COPY EXEMPLAR — BOTH CARDS ARE PURE QUERY-CLAIMS, IN THE HIGHEST-STAKES COPY IN THE PRODUCT.** They state the arithmetic and what the document says, and **draw no conclusion about why**. No *"may be fraudulent"*, no *"appears incorrect"*, no theory of the operator's intent. **RECORDED AS THE REFERENCE STANDARD for `O115`'s fix and for flag copy generally** (Q9 doctrine, `O98`): the machine reports what it computed and what it read, and the human supplies the meaning. Quote these two strings when writing any new flag.
  - **★★★ AND THE ONE WORTH THE MOST — THE WITHHELD ENTRY BALANCES AND IS STILL WRONG. THIS IS WHY CONDITION 2 EXISTS.** Register 04's refused entry **totals 4,306 on both sides**: it plugged Payroll Taxes Payable to **1,106** to absorb the 50 the register does not foot. **A DOUBLE-ENTRY SYSTEM WILL HAPPILY BALANCE A HALLUCINATED NUMBER.** Dr = Cr is a check on the ENTRY BUILDER, not on the DOCUMENT — the plug line is exactly where a balancing system hides a source error, and `tests/gaapInvariants.test.js` asserting (a) Dr = Cr would have passed on this entry all day. **FOOTING MUST BE CHECKED AGAINST THE DOCUMENT, SEPARATELY FROM BALANCING AGAINST ITSELF**, and no amount of double-entry rigour substitutes. Generalise it: **any entry with a plug line can absorb a bad input and look perfect.**
- **★★ O113 — THE FOURTH AI CALL DOES NOT EXIST. DIAGNOSIS CLOSED ON LIVE COUNTERS, 2026-08-26. A PRIOR CLAIM OF MINE IS RETRACTED.**
  - **THE QUERY (operator-authorised, read-only, Management API):** `rate_limit` for the drive user. **Drive hour `2026-08-25 23:00Z`: `ai` = 81 against a limit of 60 · `upload` = 18 against a limit of 20.** (Also `2026-08-26 04:00Z` ai=2, `2026-08-25 03:00Z` ai=9.)
  - **★ THE AI BUCKET BLEW; THE UPLOAD BUCKET NEVER TRIPPED.** 81 > 60, and 18 < 20. So the throttle that stopped the drive is unambiguously the **60/hr AI limit**, and the upload limit was never in play.
  - **★★★ THE RETRACTION WAS ITSELF WRONG, AND IS HEREBY WITHDRAWN. "BOTH WALLS SIT AT 20 FILES" IS TRUE.** On 2026-08-26 I retracted my own claim that both limits bind at 20 files and that raising `AI_LIMIT` alone would move the wall to the upload bucket. **The operator restated the claim independently the same day, and they are right.** Per file the pipeline spends **3 AI bumps and 1 upload bump**, so `60 ÷ 3 = 20` and `20 ÷ 1 = 20` — **the two limits are coincidentally the SAME constraint for the invoice path**, and raising one alone relocates the wall without moving it.
    - **WHAT WAS ACTUALLY FALSE was narrower than what I retracted.** The true statement is about the OBSERVED RUN: because the proxy bumps `ai` and returns 429 **before** reaching the upload bump (`ai-proxy/index.ts:41-49`), a call rejected on the AI check never touches the upload counter — so in the 81/18 event the upload bucket **was not in play**, and it could not have been. That is why it read 18 and not 20. **But "upload did not bind in that run" does NOT imply "upload would not bind if AI were raised", and I used the first to invalidate the second.** The original claim was a COUNTERFACTUAL and it survives: lift `AI_LIMIT` and the 21st file's classify call reaches the upload bump and is refused there.
    - **★ THE LESSON, AND IT CUTS BOTH WAYS.** §11 already records that *a ledger that quietly loses its own false claims cannot be trusted about its true ones*. **The same discipline forbids losing a TRUE one** — and an over-broad retraction is exactly that: it reads as diligence while deleting a correct finding. **A retraction must be scoped as precisely as the claim it withdraws.** Mine was not, and the operator's own reading is what caught it. The live counters corroborate the surviving claim rather than refuting it: **`upload` at 18 was TWO FILES from its own wall, independently.**

  - **★ THE ARITHMETIC CLOSES COMPLETELY — every number fits, and prior spend is now BOUNDED, not guessed.** With P prior AI calls in the hour, the classify call for file *n* is AI call `P + 3(n−1) + 1`, and the upload counter is bumped only when that classify PASSES. `upload = 18` therefore requires `18 ≤ (62 − P)/3 < 19`, i.e. **P ∈ {6, 7, 8}** — which lands exactly on the independently-derived floor of "at least 6 non-invoice calls" below. Acts 1–3 ran in the same clock hour and `handleBankFile` costs **2 calls** per statement. **Nothing is left unexplained, and no fourth call exists.**
  - **★ NO FOURTH CALL. THREE PER INVOICE FILE, PROVABLY** — `classify-document` (`App.jsx:3406`, the only upload-tagged site along with the spreadsheet path at `:4230`), `extract-invoices-batch` (`:3901`), `code-invoices-batch` (`:3950`). **The deficit was never a missing call; it was a SHARED BUCKET.**
  - **★★ AND THE COUNTERS PROVE THE SHARING ARITHMETICALLY, not by inference.** `upload` = 18 means **18 classify calls passed the AI check**. Those 18 files can account for at most 18 + (18 × 2) = **54** of the 60 successful AI calls. **So AT LEAST 6 of the 60 were NOT invoice-pipeline calls** — and that is a floor, since any file that died mid-pipeline consumed fewer than 3. Acts 1–3 ran in the same clock hour on the same user: `handleBankFile` is **2 calls** (parse + categorize) per statement, and the drive put through two statements plus payroll. **6 is exactly that shape.** The 60/hr limit is therefore not "20 invoices" — it is **"20 invoices minus whatever else you did this hour"**, which is not a number any user can predict.
  - **★ A REJECTED CALL STILL CONSUMES BUDGET.** `bump_rate_limit` (`021:36-41`) increments and returns the new count; the proxy checks `> AI_LIMIT` **after** bumping. The live evidence is the number itself: **81 means 21 calls were charged for doing nothing**, and the operator reported **21 invoices failed**. **Retrying digs the hole deeper**, and nothing in the UI says so.
  - **THE WINDOW IS A FIXED CLOCK HOUR** (`date_trunc('hour', now())`), not rolling — so the wall's duration is arbitrary: hit it at :05 and you wait 55 minutes; at :55, five. The 04:00 bucket showing `ai`=2 is the reset, visible in the data.
  - **★★ `O113` IS THREE SEPARATE DEFECTS, NOT ONE NUMBER (operator, 2026-08-26). Split so each can be fixed and closed on its own.**
    1. **`O113a` — REJECTED CALLS CONSUME BUDGET. A CORRECTNESS BUG IN THE LIMITER ITSELF, INDEPENDENT OF EVERY LIMIT VALUE. FIX FIRST.** `bump_rate_limit` increments and the proxy checks after, so a refusal is charged for. **`ai = 81` against a ceiling of 60 IS THAT BUG, VISIBLE IN PRODUCTION DATA** — 21 calls billed for doing nothing, against 21 invoices that failed. **It makes retrying ACTIVELY HARMFUL**, and nothing in the UI says so, so the user's most natural response to a 429 is the one that extends it.
    1b. **★★ AND THE DEEPER DEFECT IS UNPREDICTABILITY, NOT THE NUMBER (operator, 2026-08-26).** The AI limit is not *"20 invoices"* — it is **"20 invoices minus whatever else you did this hour"**, and **no user can compute that.** The same action succeeds or fails depending on invisible prior spend from a different feature. **Fixing the number does not fix this; only making the budget REASONABLE-ABOUT does** — either per-operation accounting, so a document costs a knowable amount, or a **visible remaining count**, so the ceiling is something a person can steer by. **No number proposed**; the shape is the requirement.
    2. **`O113b` — BOTH WALLS SIT AT 20 FILES.** 3 AI bumps and 1 upload bump per file → `60/3 = 20` and `20/1 = 20`. **Any limit change must move BOTH or NEITHER**; moving one alone relocates the wall to the identical file count and changes nothing a user could observe.
    3. **`O113c` — THE WINDOW IS A FIXED CLOCK HOUR, NOT ROLLING** (`date_trunc('hour', now())`). **The penalty for hitting the wall is therefore arbitrary: 55 minutes at :05, five minutes at :55** — the same mistake costs an order of magnitude more or less depending only on when it happened. The 04:00 bucket reading `ai = 2` is that reset, visible in the data.
  - **▶ HOLD ON `AI_LIMIT` — DO NOT TOUCH IT until `O113a` is fixed and the per-invoice call count is settled (operator, 2026-08-26).**

- **★ O113 PART (b) — THE PER-INVOICE CALL COUNT. PROPOSAL ONLY, NOTHING BUILT (2026-08-26).**
  - **★★ THE FINDING THAT REFRAMES IT: THE TWO BUCKETS CONSTRAIN DIFFERENT AXES, AND ONLY ONE OF THEM IS ABOUT CALLS.** The **AI** bucket scales with **calls per file** — consolidation moves it. The **UPLOAD** bucket scales with **FILES**, at 1 bump per file no matter how few calls each takes — **consolidation cannot move it at all.** So *"reduce the call count"* raises the ceiling only until it hits 20 files and stops. **A real restaurant is 40–80 invoices a month and onboarding wants 2–3 months, i.e. 120–240 files — so at `UPLOAD_LIMIT = 20/hr` a new client's onboarding takes SIX TO TWELVE HOURS of wall-clock waiting no matter how efficient the pipeline becomes.** The upload limit, not the AI limit, is the onboarding ceiling.
  - **★ CALLS 1 AND 2 SEND THE SAME DOCUMENT; CALL 3 SENDS NO DOCUMENT AT ALL.** `classify-document` (`max_tokens: 20`, one word) and `extract-invoices-batch` (`max_tokens: 4000`) are handed **the same bytes**, and **classification is a strict subset of what extraction must already determine** — a model that reads an invoice necessarily knows it is one. `code-invoices-batch` is different in kind: it takes **text plus the company's chart**, not the document.
    - **PROPOSAL 1 — MERGE 1 INTO 2.** One profile returning `{ doc_type, invoices: [...] }`; a non-invoice doc_type returns an empty array and routes exactly as today. **Saves one call per file AND one re-upload of the document bytes** (cost and latency, not just quota). Risk: the classifier's cheapness is a feature — 20 tokens to reject a bank statement, versus a 4,000-token extraction budget spent discovering the same thing. **Mitigation: keep the merged prompt's cheap path cheap by making doc_type the FIRST field emitted**, so a non-invoice terminates early.
    - **PROPOSAL 2 — DON'T MERGE 3; DELETE IT FOR VENDORS WE ALREADY KNOW.** Merging extraction and coding puts two jobs in one prompt and makes each less separately testable — the ·3a lesson. **The better move is that `O102`/`C203` make call 3 unnecessary for KNOWN and UNIVERSAL vendors by construction:** the ladder's whole claim is that a familiar vendor's account comes from attested history or the curated directory, **not from a model**. `App.jsx:4005` already skips the AI's answer when a `vendor_rule` exists — the mapping is read, not asked for. **So the calibration work is also the throttle fix**, and on a fixture where most invoices come from recurring vendors it removes most of the third call. **This is the one to prefer: it deletes work rather than compressing it.**
    - **PROPOSAL 3 — CACHE ON THE CONTENT HASH WE ALREADY COMPUTE.** C193 hashes document bytes (SHA-256, unique per company) to dedup the library. **Identical bytes re-uploaded currently re-extract from scratch.** Reusing the prior extraction costs nothing new to build the key for. Bounded value — it helps re-uploads, which the August drive did twice, not first-time volume.
    - **PROPOSAL 4 — BATCH ACROSS FILES, NOT WITHIN THEM.** The "batch" profiles batch invoices *inside* one document while the queue processes one file at a time, **so the axis that actually matters is not batched at all.** Multiple documents can share one Anthropic message. **Costed honestly: this is the only proposal that moves the UPLOAD bucket**, since N documents in one call is one upload bump — but it is also the riskiest (one malformed document degrades a whole batch, and `max_tokens: 4000` caps the batch size), and it changes what a per-file error means.
  - **★★ DECIDED 2026-08-26 — PROPOSAL 2 IS PRIMARY, AND IT IS SEQUENCED WITH `C203`, NOT AS SEPARATE WORK.** *Operator: deleting call 3 for KNOWN and UNIVERSAL vendors is **not compression, it is the ladder's claim made operational** — a familiar vendor's account comes from attested history, not from a model.* **RECORD IT EXPLICITLY: `C203` IS ALSO THE THROUGHPUT FIX. THE CALIBRATION WORK PAYS OFF TWICE** — once as booking authority (`O102`), once as call volume (`O113`). That is not a coincidence: a system that has to ask a model what it already knows is paying for its own forgetfulness, in both currencies.
  - **PROPOSAL 1 (merge classify into extract) — REJECTED FOR NOW, on my own risk note.** *Operator: the classifier's cheapness is the feature.* **20 tokens to reject a bank statement beats a 4,000-token extraction discovering the same thing, and merging trades a cheap guard for an expensive one.** ▶ REVISIT ONLY IF the AI bucket is still binding after proposal 2 lands.
  - **PROPOSAL 3 (content-hash cache) — ACCEPTED.** Cheap, bounded, and the drive proved re-upload is a real path (it re-uploaded twice). Reuses C193's existing SHA-256.
  - **PROPOSAL 4 (batch across files) — ▶ HOLD, and it needs its OWN failure analysis.** It is the only thing that moves the upload bucket, which makes it matter more than the others combined — **and one malformed document degrading a whole batch is exactly the failure mode this project keeps finding** (·3a's inert gate, C195(7)'s empty input, the QBO swallow). `max_tokens: 4000` caps the batch size anyway. **Design separately, later.**
  - **NO RECOMMENDATION ON `AI_LIMIT`/`UPLOAD_LIMIT` VALUES**, deliberately. Both are ours to choose, and choosing them before `O113a` is fixed would set a ceiling measured in a currency that still charges for refusals.
- **★★ O113a — THE LIMITER NO LONGER CHARGES FOR ITS OWN REFUSALS. MIGRATION `074` + `ai-proxy`, BOTH ▶ HOLD (2026-08-26).**
  - **`consume_rate_limit` READS, DECIDES, AND ONLY THEN CHARGES**, inside one plpgsql function and therefore one transaction — so "decide, then charge" is atomic without a second round trip, and `for update` serialises concurrent callers so two requests cannot both observe 59 and both be allowed. **`021`'s `bump_rate_limit` is DELIBERATELY KEPT, NOT DROPPED:** the edge function and the database deploy separately, so the old code must keep working until the new function ships and a rollback must not hit a missing RPC.
  - **▶ ORDERING IS LOAD-BEARING: APPLY `074` *BEFORE* DEPLOYING `ai-proxy`.** The reverse order calls a function that does not exist and fails **every** AI request — a total outage of the AI path, not a degradation.
  - **★ A SECOND INSTANCE OF THE SAME BUG, IN THE SAME FUNCTION, FOUND WHILE FIXING THE FIRST.** An upload-tagged call bumped `ai`, passed, then was refused by `upload` — **leaving the `ai` charge in place for a call that never ran.** Charging one bucket for a refusal issued by another is the identical defect wearing a different hat, which is why the new RPC takes an ARRAY and is **all-or-nothing across buckets**. VERIFY block (c) proves it.
  - **★★ THE VERIFY BLOCK MAKES THE LIMITER REFUSE SOMETHING AND SHOWS THE COUNTER DID NOT MOVE** — `after_refused = 10, NOT 11`, in a rolled-back transaction. **A limiter nobody has watched refuse *without charging* is a fix on paper**, the standard `071` was held to.
  - **THE PROXY NOW FAILS CLOSED (503) IF THE LIMITER CANNOT ANSWER.** Guessing permissively is how a limiter becomes decorative under exactly the load it exists for.
  - **THE 429 NOW SAYS WHICH BUDGET RAN OUT AND WHEN IT RESETS**, and the remaining budget is returned on SUCCESS too (`x-ratelimit-remaining-*`) because the limiter computed it to make its decision anyway. **This is a step toward the budget being reasonable-about, NOT the fix for it** — that needs a surface, and none is built here.
  - **BOUNDARY, STATED: this changes the limiter, not the limits.** `AI_LIMIT` and `UPLOAD_LIMIT` are untouched at 60 and 20, per the operator's instruction, and `O113b`/`O113c` remain open.
- **★★ O97 — ACCEPT-AND-QUEUE. `O118` MERGED IN; THE UPLOAD CEILING AND THE DURABLE QUEUE ARE ONE ITEM (2026-08-26). ▶ HOLD — DESIGN SESSION, NO CODE.**
  - **★★ THE DEFECT IS NOT THE CEILING'S HEIGHT — IT IS THAT THE CEILING IS ENFORCED SYNCHRONOUSLY WHILE A HUMAN WATCHES.** *Operator: 200 files in, 20 succeed, 180 turn red, and the owner concludes the product is broken.* **Onboarding is inherently a BATCH JOB — nobody needs January's invoices categorised in ninety seconds — and we are failing it in the foreground.** Raising the number would move the failure without changing its shape; the owner would still be watching.
  - **★ THE MERGE IS TECHNICAL, NOT ORGANISATIONAL: THE DURABLE QUEUE IS THE PREREQUISITE.** Established in code today — `fileStoreRef.current[id] = f` (`App.jsx:3743`) puts the **bytes** in an in-memory React ref · `logIntake` (`:3748`) durably records the **arrival** · `classifyFile` (`:3806`) makes the **first AI call** · `storeDocument` (`:3877`) reaches Storage **only afterwards**. **So the arrival is durable, the bytes are not, and the AI work starts before anything is persisted.** Acceptance and processing are **one synchronous act**, and accept-and-queue is **impossible** until the durable byte-write moves ahead of the AI work. That is precisely `O97`'s original defect, which is why this is one item and not two. *(`O118` is kept as a row pointing here rather than deleted — a merged id that vanishes reads as a lost item to the next sweep.)*
  - **PROMOTED FROM P2/UNSCHEDULED TO P0-FOR-ONBOARDING.** *Operator: the drive turned it from unscheduled into the thing between us and a first client.* The live orphan of 2026-08-06 20:11:42 was the same bug costing one file; onboarding is the same bug costing a customer's first impression.
  - **THE FIVE DESIGN QUESTIONS, RECORDED WITH THE ITEM AND DELIBERATELY NOT ANSWERED HERE:** (1) accept-and-queue — *partially answered above: not today, the byte-write must move first* · (2) what the owner sees while a queue drains, and the honest copy when it is six hours — **Q9 applies, and a progress bar that cannot state a finish time is a world-claim** · (3) failure mid-queue — stop, skip-and-report, or retry, noting `O113a` made retry safe only as of this week · (4) is per-user-per-clock-hour the right unit at all, given an onboarding and a monthly close share one budget · (5) **what is actually being protected — answered below, and it was the right question to ask first.**
  - **★★ (5) ANSWERED: THE PURPOSE IS STATED ONCE IN THE ENTIRE REPO, AS A SLASH.** §7: *"limits prevent abuse/runaway cost"*. Migration `021`'s header and `ai-proxy`'s header both state **what** the limits are and **never why**. **Three candidate threats, and the unit fits exactly one** — cost wants **tokens**, not requests (a 20-token classify and a 4,000-token extraction cost the same against the budget, a 200× miscalibration); upstream wants **org-scope and per-minute** (ten users at 59/hour can spike one minute while all are "under"); **abuse wants per-actor-per-hour, which is what we have.** **So the mechanism is right for abuse and wrong for the other two — and it is currently refusing a legitimate first-time onboarding, which is none of the three.** Recorded as the §9 sibling rule: **name the threat, the way you name the reader.** **And the design consequence follows directly: if the threat is abuse, a legitimate bulk workload needs A DIFFERENT DOOR, NOT A BIGGER NUMBER.**
- **★★ O114 RE-DRIVE PREP — THREE FINDINGS FROM READING THE LIVE DATA INSTEAD OF THE FIXTURE (2026-08-27). One is a real defect, one is a correction to my own record.**
  - **★★ `ROMA CHEESE + DAIRY CO` — THE `+` IS NOT NORMALISED, SO ONE VENDOR HOLDS TWO KEYS.** August carries **two** Roma bank lines: `ACH DEBIT - ROMA CHEESE & DAIRY CO` (08-04, 551.20) and **`ACH DEBIT - ROMA CHEESE + DAIRY CO` (08-19, 538.75)**. `normalizeDescriptor` maps `&` → `and` and **leaves `+` as a token**, so the keys are `roma cheese and dairy` and `roma cheese + dairy` — `identityRelation` returns **NONE**. Same vendor, same rail, same month, two identities. **DESIGNATED `O119`.** *(And note the shape: this is exactly the C200 corpus problem again — real descriptor variance that the fixtures did not contain. The fixture had one Roma string; production has two.)*
  - **★★ MY ALAMO FIRE TEST FIXTURE WAS WRONG, AND THE FIX I JUSTIFIED WITH IT ADDRESSES NOTHING IN THE REAL DATA.** `tests/invoicePayment.test.js` uses `"ACH DEBIT - ALAMO FIRE SAFETY LLC"`. **The real descriptor is `ACH DEBIT - ALAMO FIRE & SAFETY LLC`** — it HAS the ampersand, so both sides normalise to `alamo fire and safety` and relate **EXACT** with no help needed. I recorded the `and`-dropping in `compareKey` as *"found by this feature's own suite on a real specimen"*. **It was found on a string I invented.** The rule stands on its own merits (a bank that drops an ampersand is a realistic variant, and the guard is comparison-only with the anti-merge cases pinned either way) — **but it fixes no case present in this data, and it does NOT fix the `+` case either** (`[roma,cheese,dairy]` vs `[roma,cheese,+,dairy]` is not a prefix). **Recorded as a correction because "found on a real specimen" was the claim that made it credible, and it was false.** Same lesson as C201's assumed-vs-real invoice strings, repeated inside two days.
  - **★ THE OPERATOR'S STATE SUMMARY WAS OFF, AND THE QUERY IS WHY WE KNOW.** Reported as *"19 lines booked, 12 of 36 invoices booked, 10 unanswered clarification cards"*. Live: **17 bank lines**, **5 booked invoices**, and **ZERO open anomalies** (24 dismissed, 52 resolved). The clarification queue is React state, so the "10 unanswered cards" did not survive the session — there is nothing to clear. **Only ONE of the 5 booked invoices is a specimen** (Hill Country 08-06, 468.50). *Not a criticism — a demonstration of the standing rule that estimates about production data are claims about a query nobody ran.*
- **★ O120 / O121 — WHICH CARDS EARN AN INTERRUPTION, AND A TRUST HEADER THAT CANNOT SEE THEM (2026-08-27). ▶ HOLD, no design.**
  - **★★ THE TENSION IS THE ITEM.** Clarification cards sit below the fold and can be scrolled past — **10 sat unanswered in the August drive while the header said *"your books are correct and up to date"***. But the obvious fix fights the product: **the stated principle is BOOK EVERYTHING AND BATCH THE JUDGMENT TO CLOSE**, and a modal per card is its opposite — it stops the world per item and **turns a queue you can zip through into a gauntlet**. So the question is not *"modal or not"*, it is **WHICH CARDS EARN AN INTERRUPTION.**
  - **CANDIDATE SPLIT, recorded as a candidate:** a card **whose wrong answer books silently wrong** may interrupt; anything **recoverable** stays in the batch. **The `O114` lifecycle card is the archetype of the first** — *"different purchase"* mints a phantom payable, *"same purchase"* suppresses a real charge, and **the more dangerous of the two leaves nothing on any screen.** A low-confidence category question is the archetype of the second: wrong is visible, and a recode undoes it. *(Note this reuses the same discriminator as the `O114` option ordering — VISIBLE-vs-SILENT failure. That is now the third place that distinction has decided a design, which is a sign it is load-bearing rather than local.)*
  - **★★ AND THE HALF THAT IS NOT UX AT ALL — `O121`, established in code.** `ownerTrustState` (`ownerTrust.js:35`) accepts `controlTotals`, `openConfidenceFlags`, `intakeRows`, `unknownDocs`, `reviewedThrough`, `bankMatch`, `openHighAnomalies` — **and no clarification-queue input.** It can therefore return `all_clear` and *"your books are correct and up to date"* **while N documents sit unbooked awaiting an answer.** The one indirect path — an unanswered card leaves its intake row `held_for_review`, and `intakeRows` IS an input — **only reaches the header through the completeness net's staleness window, so a green header beside FRESH unanswered cards is structurally possible.** Whether the header is right is **incidental, not enforced.**
    - **THIS IS THE C194 / `O98` FAMILY, NOT A UX PREFERENCE:** a false green on the surface whose whole job is to be trustworthy, and a world-claim derived from a query that never asked the question. **The function was hardened against exactly this twice already** — its own comments name the O83 false *"Nothing wrong"* and the bank-overdue false green — **and the clarification queue is simply the net nobody wired in.** The operator's instinct that this is the cheaper fix is right, **and it is also the one that is required independently**: the header should stop claiming correctness while cards are open whatever is decided about interrupting.
- **★★★ O122 — THE CARD RATE IS A SYSTEM-LEVEL ACCEPTANCE CRITERION, NOT A FEATURE (2026-08-27). THE PROMISE IMPLIES A NUMBER NOTHING CURRENTLY MEASURES.**
  - **★★ THE GAP, AND IT IS THE MOST IMPORTANT THING ON THIS PAGE: "ALL BEHAVIOURS CORRECT" AND "THE PRODUCT IS USABLE" ARE DIFFERENT CLAIMS, AND ONLY THE FIRST HAS EVER BEEN TESTED.** Every drive, every suite, every acceptance doc in this repo scores INDIVIDUAL behaviours. **We can therefore end with every behaviour correct and a screen carrying nine cards — which is not a usable product.** The promise is *"drop anything here — your AI controller handles the rest"*, and that sentence implies a **CARD RATE**. Nothing measures it. `tests/` cannot: a unit test asserts one card is right, never that there are few enough of them.
  - **★★ THE TARGET IS NOT ZERO — IT IS THE CONTROLLER'S OWN QUESTION RATE.** *(Operator, verbatim: **"the target card rate is the controller's own question rate, not zero."**)* A controller who asks nothing is rubber-stamping; one who asks nine questions about a 60-document month is drowning the client. **So the test for any INDIVIDUAL card is: WOULD A COMPETENT CONTROLLER HAVE ASKED THIS, OF THIS CLIENT, THIS MONTH?**
  - **★★★ AND THE RULE THAT DOES THE WORK — *(verbatim)*: "A CARD THE USER SEES EVERY MONTH IS A BUG WEARING A QUESTION MARK."** Formally: **any card that can recur monthly for the SAME VENDOR on the SAME QUESTION is a design defect BY DEFINITION** — not a tuning preference, not a P3, a defect. **The rule has teeth, and the proof is that it RETROACTIVELY RECLASSIFIES TWO KNOWN ITEMS:**
    - **`O117`** (weekly fixed-fee vendor flagged duplicate every week) stops being a P1 annoyance and becomes **definitionally a defect**.
    - **The `C203` justification itself** — Bluebonnet excepted at ~78 for **eight consecutive months on the same question** — was recorded as evidence that confidence is miscalibrated. **Under this rule it is simply a bug, and was one from month two.** *(That reframing is worth more than the calibration argument: it needed no theory of confidence at all.)*
  - **THE THREE CATEGORIES — every existing and future card type gets tagged, and they are NOT the same thing stacked on one screen:**

    | # | category | expected rate | nature |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | **1** | **BUG** — *"we recorded more than one payment of $462.85"* when there was exactly one. **No controller would ever say that.** | **ZERO** | a fix, **not a design question** |
    | **2** | **ONE-TIME TEACHING** — Franklin Ave's alias; Bluebonnet's two-month graduation under Amendment B. A controller asks these **once, on day one, and never again.** | **onboarding cost, NOT monthly cost** | **each must be askable ONCE and then never re-asked** — if it can re-ask, it is category 1 |
    | **3** | **GENUINE JUDGMENT** — Hill Country's invoice at 468.50 against a payment of 486.50. **A real bookkeeper stops on that.** | **forever, and RARE** | the residue that proves the system is thinking |

  - **STARTING TAXONOMY, applied to what actually exists today** *(this is the tagging, not a promise to tag later)*: `lifecycle/amount_differs` **3** · `lifecycle/identity_differs` **2** (closes with `O111`) · `lifecycle/multiple_candidates` **3** genuinely, **1** when produced by the flatten-dedupe defect · `duplicate_payment` **1** on lifecycle pairs (`O114`) and **1** on weekly vendors (`O117`), **3** only for a true double-pay · `gl` low-confidence category **3**, shrinking toward **2** as vendors reach KNOWN · `gaap` capitalise-vs-expense **3** · `direction` revenue-vs-expense **2** (the company's own identity settles it — O75) · `large_transaction`/`round_number` **3**, but **1** while they fire on payroll (O85(c)) · `missing_recurring` **1** while it measures against `now()` instead of period context (O86) · `category_spike`/`vendor_spike`/`rapid_sequential` **3**.
  - **STEADY-STATE TARGET — established client, all vendors KNOWN, aliases learned, no bugs: UNDER 5% OF DOCUMENTS.** For a 60-document month that is **0–3 cards**. *(Operator's working number, recorded as a working number.)* **Onboarding gets its OWN, HIGHER, SEPARATE number** — conflating them would either make onboarding look broken or make steady state look fine.
  - **★ MEASURE IT ON EVERY FUTURE DRIVE, SPLIT BY CATEGORY.** Not a total — **a split**, because the whole question is **whether category 2 actually shrinks month over month rather than being assumed to.** A falling total with a flat category 2 means the teaching is not sticking, and that is invisible in an aggregate. **This is now a standing drive deliverable.**
- **★★★ O114 RE-DRIVE §1 FAILURE — ROOT-CAUSED 2026-08-27. ONE MISNAMED PARAMETER, AND A CARD THAT LIED ABOUT WHY. BOTH MINE.**
  - **★★ THE ROOT CAUSE: `checkedRowUpdate` takes `patch`. The O114 attach passed `values`.** So `patch` arrived `undefined`, `.update(undefined)` did nothing, `recordFailure` fired, and **every attach failed** — `import_metadata` is `NULL` on all three targets and there are **zero `invoice_attached` audit rows**. **13 of 14 checked-write call sites in the repo had it right; mine was the only outlier.**
  - **★★★ AND THE FAILURE PATH REPORTED A FALSE CAUSE, WHICH IS THE WORSE HALF.** I had written the fallback to ask instead of half-attaching — correct — but gave it `reason: MULTIPLE_CANDIDATES`, which renders as ***"We recorded more than one payment of that amount."*** **That is a statement about the LEDGER, and it was false: there was exactly one payment.** All three cards said it, identically, and **the drive became undiagnosable from its own output.** It cost a full session: the operator hypothesised duplicate legs from `journal_entry_lines`, then duplicate JEs from the supersede path; I shipped an entry-level dedupe; both of us were chasing a candidate-counting bug **that did not exist**, because the card invented a cause.
    - **THE RULE THIS EARNS: A CARD MAY REPORT THAT WE FAILED. IT MAY NOT INVENT A REASON IN THE LEDGER.** `ASK_REASON.RECORD_FAILED` now exists precisely so the honest sentence has somewhere to live — *"…matches a payment we already recorded on X. We couldn't save the link between them, so we haven't recorded anything."* **Q9 says an empty result may only be reported as an empty result; this is its twin — OUR failure may only be reported as OUR failure.** Reusing a neighbouring reason code because it was already there is how a diagnostic becomes a decoy.
  - **★ WHY NOTHING CAUGHT IT, AND THE GUARD THAT NOW DOES.** `tests/invoicePayment.test.js` proves `planInvoiceArrival` returns ATTACH; **nothing asserted the CALLER then successfully attaches.** That is the ·3a gap exactly — both sides of a contract tested against the same fixture, with the boundary between them untested. **Reproducing the real ledger through the real function returned ATTACH for all three specimens**, which is what proved the defect was in the wiring rather than the logic. New guard in `tests/checkedWrite.test.js` (where a falsifier of the CALLING CONVENTION would look, not in this feature's suite): **every `checkedRowUpdate`/`checkedIdsUpdate` call site in `src/` must name its payload `patch`.** Mutation-verified — restore `values:` → red; restore → green. It also had to learn two things about itself: **ES6 shorthand `patch,` is valid** and must pass, and **the helper's own definition is not a call site** — a guard that flagged the contract it protects.
  - **AND THE OPERATOR'S TWO HYPOTHESES WERE BOTH WRONG AND BOTH WORTH HAVING.** *Duplicate legs:* refuted — `flattenJournalEntries` emits ONE row per ≤2-line entry; the two-row result came from SQL joining `journal_entry_lines`, a property of the query. *Duplicate JEs from the supersede path:* refuted — one live entry each for Toast and Alamo Fire. **But the first hypothesis exposed a REAL latent defect in the >2-line branch, which is now fixed and pinned.** A wrong hypothesis that surfaces a real bug is a good turn; the thing that wasted the session was my card, not their theory.

No separate, swallowable update: the idempotency marker is written iff the entry is posted."* **Every clause of that is false**, and it was written as the JUSTIFICATION for not using a follow-up checked update — the exact remedy ·3c applied to payroll and depreciation. **·3c's D2 sweep listed `tax_amount` and `signedPeriod`'s `original_date` as `O95` victims and MISSED the reversal marker.** An incomplete audit of `import_metadata` readers, and the miss is recorded here rather than quietly corrected.

Don't press Void on it again."*
    - **THE FALSE COMMENT IS GONE, AND A TEST KEEPS IT GONE.** `App.jsx:2407`'s claim was the JUSTIFICATION for not stamping; the suite now asserts the source no longer says *"p_meta persists to…"* or *"the marker is written iff the entry is posted"*. **The guard strips comments before scanning** — the suite QUOTES the old comment to explain it, and a guard matching its own explanation is the C202 false positive a fourth time.

It worked only because `fetchLedgerEntries` filters deleted rows upstream. Any future path loading soft-deleted entries into `invoices` (a restore flow, a *"show deleted"* toggle) would have counted a soft-deleted reversal as live and **blocked a legitimate re-void.** Flatten now carries it. *(Note the shape: a guard correct only by virtue of something upstream is a guard nobody has actually tested.)*
    - **`alreadyReversed` HAD NO TEST AT ALL** before this — the function at the centre of a P0. So the join was **verified against the real strings**: `substring(from 11)` + `split_part(' — ')` on the live reversal description reproduces the live original description **exactly**. The match works; the emptiness meant CLEAN. **This is `O98`'s own discipline applied to my own verification rather than to the product's.**

Kept rather than silently corrected — an unverified number that LOOKS derived is more dangerous than an obviously missing one.

- **★★ O119 FIXED 2026-08-29 (C209) — AND THE TEST FOUND THE PHANTOM THE FIX OPENED.** `normalizeName` mapped `&` to "and" and left `+` alone (it was not in the punctuation strip either), so the two August Roma descriptors keyed as different companies. **The widening is deliberately one character wide** — a wrong merge silently launders one vendor's attested mapping onto another's charges, so the anti-merge pairs (SYSCO vs SYSCO FUEL, Lone Star vs Lone Star Restaurant Supply) are asserted alongside, and **they are the reason to trust the widening rather than the widening being its own justification.**
  - **★★ THE FOLLOW-ON IS THE USEFUL PART, AND THE AUTHOR DID NOT FIND IT.** `"+"` used to normalise to `"+"`, carry no letters, and correctly resolve to nothing. Giving it letters meant a descriptor made only of joiners would have **minted an entity keyed on `"and"`** — the phantom-vendor one-way door this module's own comments describe, opened by a fix to a cosmetic-looking bug. The letters test now ignores the joining word. **Same shape as the original bare-numeric find, which was also caught by a test rather than by reading the code: every phantom this module has had was found by asserting a negative.**
- **★ O96 CLOSED 2026-08-29 (C209) — THE ITEM WAS STALE, AND THAT IS THE FINDING.** It read *"dead 'run depreciation through date' button — connect it or remove it"*. **There is no button.** It was deliberately removed from BOTH Reports and Dashboard — depreciation is deterministic so it auto-posts when due, and both files carry comments saying so. The decision was made long ago; what outlived it was an unused `planDepreciationRun` import and a `runDepreciationThrough` destructure resolving to `undefined`. **A ledger item describing a control that no longer exists is the same class as a printed census going old** — it cannot be wrong, only out of date, and nothing about it invites correction.
- **★★ THE VENDORS TAB — FIXED 2026-08-29 (C216), AND THE NUMBERS WERE WRONG TOO.** `vendorSummary` was keyed over EVERY flattened row with no filter. **All three defects reproduced by RUNNING THE OLD CODE against a fixture rather than reasoning about it:** `[{"Hill Country Milling Co.": 1649.2, count 2}, {"Opening balances as of 2026-01-01": 5000}, {"Toast POS": 1200}]`.
  - **★ THE THIRD ONE WAS NOT IN THE ORIGINAL FINDING: EVERY BILL'S PAYMENT COUNTED AS A SECOND PURCHASE.** An $824.60 bill read as **$1,649.20**. §11 had previously recorded *"Amounts on the tab verified correct… this is a classification defect, not a math defect"* — **that was wrong, and it was wrong because the amounts were checked against invoices only, on a vendor whose payments happened not to collide.**
  - **★★ ONE RULE REPLACES THREE SPECIAL CASES: SPEND IS AN EXPENSE-ACCOUNT MOVEMENT.** Revenue is `4xxx`, bill payments land on A/P, opening balances are equity/asset, transfers are asset-to-asset — all fall out for free. **A filter on ACCOUNT, not on `source`, deliberately:** the payment leg of a bill is a legitimate entry with a real vendor name on it, so what makes it not-spend is where the money LANDED.
- **★★ O125 FIXED 2026-08-29 (C210) — AND THE FIX DELETED AN IMPLEMENTATION RATHER THAN IMPROVING ONE.** `displayVendorName` / `vendorGroupKey` live in `vendorIdentity.js`, the resolver that already handled all three symptoms and that the display layer had simply never called. **THE ROOT CAUSE, STATED PROPERLY: one string was doing two jobs.** A vendor needs a NAME a human reads and a KEY the system groups by, and they have **opposite** requirements — the name keeps its capitals, punctuation and legal suffix; the key throws all three away. Deriving one from the other by a split gets both wrong. Flatten now emits `vendor` AND `vendor_key`; `vendorSummary` groups on the key and labels with the most RECENT display name (a real string the user has seen, not a normalised key and not an unpredictable vote), and the vendor filter follows the same grouping.
  - **★ THE REVERSAL SYMPTOM WAS ALSO AN ARITHMETIC BUG, NOT ONLY A LABELLING ONE.** A reversal filed under `REVERSAL: …` left the original's vendor total **overstated** with its offset hidden under a separate name. Grouped correctly the two **net to zero**, and the test SUMS them rather than merely comparing keys — a key-equality assertion would have passed on a fix that still displayed the wrong total.
  - **★ A NULL KEY RETURNS null, NEVER `""`.** Grouping every unkeyable row under a falsy default would merge unrelated vendors — the one-way door reached through a fallback rather than through a rule, which is the harder kind to notice.
- **★★ O125 (ORIGINAL FINDING) — THE DISPLAY LAYER HAD ITS OWN VENDOR IDENTITY, A THIRD IMPLEMENTATION OF THE SAME CONTRACT (2026-08-27, from the InvoicesView cleanup screen).**
  - **ONE ROOT CAUSE: `flattenJournalEntries` (`ledger.js:31`) derives the vendor as `description.split(" – ")[0]` — a raw string split on an EN-dash.** That is an identity decision, made in the display layer, by punctuation.
  - **THREE SYMPTOMS, ALL ON THE SCREEN THAT IS THE ONLY ROUTE TO DELETE:** *(1)* **GUSTO RENDERS AS FIFTEEN SEPARATE VENDORS** — payroll descriptions use an **EM-dash** before the period (`Gusto Payroll — 2026-08-15`), so the en-dash split misses and **the pay period becomes part of the vendor name**. *(2)* **A TRAILING PERIOD SPLITS A VENDOR IN TWO** — `Hill Country Milling Co` and `Hill Country Milling Co.` are separate chips, likewise Roma. *(3)* **REVERSALS GET THEIR OWN VENDOR** (`REVERSAL: Hill Country Milling Co.`) instead of filing under the entry they reverse.
  - **★★ `entityKeyFor` ALREADY HANDLES ALL THREE CORRECTLY** — it normalises trailing periods, and the per-source strategy (C201) exists precisely because the em-dash case bit us once already. **This is the SAME defect C201 found in the payroll descriptor split, resurfacing in the UI because the UI never used the resolver.**
  - **★★★ AND THAT MAKES THREE IMPLEMENTATIONS OF VENDOR IDENTITY IN ONE CODEBASE:** *(a)* `entityKeyFor`/`identityForEntry` — the canonical one, anti-merge-tested · *(b)* `autoMatchBankLines`' `normalizeName` + **two-way substring containment** — already recorded as owed after O114 §2.1 · *(c)* **this string split.** **The operator's framing is the right one: this is exactly what `065` was released for — the vendor backfill *"cannot be SQL"* because it would have been a second implementation of the identity contract.** We declined to write one in SQL and shipped two others in JS. **The fix is not to improve the split; it is to delete it and call the resolver**, and reversals should file under the reversed entry's vendor (which needs `import_metadata.reverses`, i.e. `O123`'s fix, so the two are coupled).
- **★ O129 — THE DEPRECIATION BAR LEFT REPORTS (2026-08-29, C211), AND REMOVING IT SURFACED DEAD PLUMBING WITH A BILL ATTACHED.** The bar's one control opened a form asking for a raw `journal_entries.id`, **typed by hand, on the tab where you read your statements**. **★★ AND `depreciationDueInfo` HAD BEEN DEAD FOR MONTHS AT A COST:** it fed a dashboard nudge that was removed when depreciation became automatic — but the SIGNAL behind it kept running, a Supabase query on **every company load**, writing state that one file destructured and **no file read**. O96's class with a database round-trip attached: dead plumbing that merely reads oddly costs nothing; this one billed for it forever to answer a question nobody asked. **▶ ONE DECISION OPEN:** `attachDepreciationToExistingAsset` now has no caller. Kept, removed from `erpCtx`, and NAMED as unreachable — because what O96 punished was dead plumbing that LOOKED wired.
- **★★ O130 — ONE REMOVAL CONTROL, AND THE SYSTEM PICKS THE MECHANISM (2026-08-29, C215). CLOSES O126 FROM BOTH ENDS.** *Operator: "users don't even know what reversal means. I think the most useful item is deleting."*
  - **★★ THE CHOICE WAS NEVER THE OWNER'S TO MAKE, BECAUSE ONE INPUT DECIDES IT: HAS THE MONTH BEEN SIGNED OFF?** Not signed → nobody has attested to those numbers, so removing a wrong entry is correcting a draft (soft delete; row survives, Undo restores, audit keeps it, linked payment goes with it). Signed → **you may not quietly change a month your accountant put their name to**, so the correction must be a NEW entry dated today, which is what a reversal actually is. **The system already knew which case it was in** — `signedPeriodForDate` gated every delete — and merely refused with *"reopen it first"* instead of doing the right thing.
  - **★ THE SENTENCE TRAVELS WITH THE DECISION.** `planEntryRemoval` returns the mode AND the confirmation text, so a caller cannot promise a delete and perform a correction. **That is §9 applied one layer up: describe from the DECISION, not in parallel with it.** A test asserts neither surface still says "Void" (comments stripped first — C202's lesson a fifth time) and that no accounting word appears in either sentence.
  - **★ A FOURTH INSTANCE OF "AN INVISIBLE ACTION WILL BE REPEATED", NOW CLOSED:** an entry already dealt with renders **"Removed · DATE"** and its control is **DISABLED**, not merely refusing on click.
- **★★ O126 (ORIGINAL FINDING, CLOSED BY O130) — DELETE BELONGS ON THE DETAIL PANEL, AND VOID MUST BE VISIBLY TERMINAL. ONE FUNNEL FROM BOTH ENDS (2026-08-27).**
  - **(A) WIRE `softDeleteInvoice` TO `TransactionDetailPanel`**, same confirm treatment as the Books-list control. That panel is **the surface a user reaches by clicking an entry they intend to deal with**, and today it offers **Mark as Paid / Full entry / Void** — with Void the only destructive option and the one that compounds `O123`. **The safe action sitting four steps deep behind *"View all invoices for X →"* is not a discoverability nitpick: it is what produced three reversals.**
  - **(B) VOID MUST BE IDEMPOTENT *AND* VISIBLY TERMINAL.** Fixing the `O95` marker so the two existing guards actually fire is **necessary and not sufficient**: an already-reversed entry must **show as reversed in the transactions list**, and its Void control must be **DISABLED, not merely refusing on click**. A guard that rejects on click still teaches the user that clicking is how you find out — and the click is free only because we already lost the marker.
  - **THEY ARE ONE FUNNEL: the safe action is hidden and the dangerous one is unmarked, so the user is routed to the destructive path and given no signal to stop.** Both are instances of the §9 rule this session earned — **an action whose effect is invisible will be repeated** — and so is `O124(c)`.
- **★★★ THE O114 RE-DRIVE, SCORED (2026-08-27). §1 PASSES · §2 FAILS · THE ANTI-VACUITY CHECK EARNED ITS ENTIRE EXISTENCE.**
  - **✅ §1 PASSES, VERIFIED IN THE DATABASE NOT THE UI.** Roma (551.20), Toast (462.85) and Alamo Fire (425.00) attached **silently, no card**, and all three carry `invoice_attached=true` with an `attached_invoice_id`. **Franklin Ave raised the identity card and did NOT attach** — the outcome §1 marks as a failure if inverted. **Hill Country raised the amounts-differ card stating "$18.00 more than the invoice" and named no cause.** The order-independence bug is closed on the specimens it was written for.
  - **★★★ §2 FAILS, AND IT FAILED IN THE EXACT WORDS THE CRITERION USED.** §2 was written as: *"IF BLUEBONNET'S CARDS VANISH, SOMETHING OVER-MATCHED — the single most likely way for a wrong implementation to LOOK BETTER than a right one."* **Four were required. Three remain.** Invoice `BLS-88412` (08-03) attached to the payment of **07-27** — **seven days earlier, exactly on the `WINDOW_BEFORE_DAYS` boundary.**
    - **THE EVIDENCE: Bluebonnet pays WEEKLY at exactly $145.00** (07/06, 07/13, 07/20, 07/27). So the candidate set held **FOUR entries identical in vendor and amount to the cent**, and **only the date window separated them** — leaving exactly one, which then satisfied *"one certain candidate → ATTACH"*. **THE CERTAINTY WAS AN ARTEFACT OF THE WINDOW, NOT OF THE EVIDENCE:** at ±14 days it would have asked; at ±3 it would have booked a payable. **A threshold decided it, not the documents.**
    - **AND IT FAILED SILENT — the direction the whole design exists to avoid.** The 08/03 delivery's expense is **suppressed**, the 07/27 payment is claimed by an invoice that is not its own, and **nothing on any screen says so.** → **`O127`, P0.**
    - **★★ IT IS `O117` WEARING THE `O114` COSTUME.** One root property: **for a flat-fee recurring vendor, amount and identity carry NO information**, so the decision rests entirely on a date window. O117's version emits a noisy card; **this version silently deletes a real charge.** The two items were split on 2026-08-26 as different failure modes of the duplicate detector — **they are now confirmed as one property surfacing in two subsystems**, and `O127`'s fix should be designed with `O117`, using the cadence and amount band `vendor_state` already holds.
  - **★★ THE METHOD POINT, AND IT IS THE ONE TO KEEP: A PRE-REGISTERED CRITERION CAUGHT A DEFECT THE RESULT LOOKED TOO GOOD TO CONTAIN.** Four attaches against a `>= 3` threshold reads as a clean beat, and it was reported that way. **§2 existed only because it was written BEFORE the data, naming a specific vendor and a specific direction of surprise.** Without it, the over-match would have been recorded as a bonus — **and the missing card is easier to miss than a wrong one, because absence has no pixel.** *(This is the §9 invisible-action rule at the level of TEST DESIGN: an assertion about something that must still be there is the only kind that catches a silent deletion.)*
  - **CARD RATE (`O122`) — 9 documents, 5 cards: category 1 = 3 (Bluebonnet duplicates, `O117`) · category 2 = 1 (Franklin Ave identity → `O111`) · category 3 = 1 (Hill Country's $18 gap).** Steady state for this set is **1 card** once `O117`, `O111` and `O127` land. **First drive scored under `O122`, and the split immediately paid: three of five cards are defects, which a total of "5 cards" would have hidden.**
  - **`O128` — THE SUCCESSFUL ATTACH ANNOUNCES ITSELF AS A NULL RESULT:** the queue reads **"✓ 0 invoices booked · $0.00 total"**. Technically true and read as *nothing happened*, at the moment the best possible outcome occurred. **The most valuable behaviour this feature has, described as a zero.**
- **★★★ O117 + O127 — SPEC WRITTEN AND BOTH HALVES BUILT 2026-08-29 (C218 matching, C220 detection). `docs/RECURRING_FLAT_FEE_SPEC_O117_O127.md`. NO CODE.**
  - **★★ THE BOUNDARY ARITHMETIC IS NOW ESTABLISHED FROM THE CODE, NOT RECALLED FROM THE DRIVE.** Invoice 08-03; payments 07-06/13/20/27 at $145.00; `gap = -7` for 07-27 and `gap < -WINDOW_BEFORE_DAYS` is `-7 < -7` = **false**, so it survives by one day while the other three are excluded. `certain.length === 1` → ATTACH. **At ±14 the same code asks; at ±3 it books a payable. Nothing about the two documents decided it.**
  - **★★★ THE REFRAME, AND IT IS THE WHOLE SPEC: THE PAIR IS THE WRONG UNIT.** Ask what actually goes wrong when invoice A attaches to payment 3 rather than payment 1, given all four are identical. **Within a period: nothing** — every account, every total, every control figure is the same, so the pairing is *unobservable in the books*. **Across a period boundary: the expense moves months**, which is exactly the `O127` damage. So the question the matcher was asking — *which payment does this invoice belong to* — is **unanswerable from the data AND mostly not worth answering**; the question that matters is *does this vendor's period balance*. **That is also what a bookkeeper does**: handed four identical invoices and four identical payments, nobody agonises over the pairing — they check that four is four, and what they catch is five-against-four.
  - **★ SO THE RULE IS COUNT, NOT PAIR — AND IT UNIFIES BOTH ITEMS.** The matcher attaches within the period when counts allow and raises ONE card when they don't; the detector stops firing on identical-pairs and fires instead on a count above the vendor's cadence (*"charged 6 times in August; they normally charge 4"*). **A genuine double-payment still surfaces. The weekly card that says two identical charges happened — which they are supposed to — does not.**
  - **★★ THE ANTI-VACUITY CRITERION IS PRE-REGISTERED, because suppression turning into blindness is the single most likely way a wrong implementation looks BETTER than a right one.** §3 of the acceptance criteria feeds a month with FIVE payments against four invoices and requires **exactly one** card: *if that card does not appear, the fix is worse than the bug.* Written before the data, per `O127`'s own lesson — **a missing card is harder to notice than a wrong one, because absence has no pixel.**
  - **★ RECOGNITION NEEDS NO MIGRATION AND MUST NOT DEPEND ON `vendor_state`.** Flat (`sd/mean ≤ 0.02`), frequent (>1 charge per period), established (≥4 charges over ≥2 periods) are all derivable from booked entries. `vendor_state` is live but EMPTY — Amendment B withholds every backfill tier and `C201` is on hold — so wiring recognition to it would park this behind `O102`, the largest item on the board.
  - **★ AND THE CLASS DEFINITION IS THE BLAST-RADIUS CONTROL.** Only vendors charging MORE OFTEN THAN THE PERIOD get set-reconciliation; a net-30 vendor bills once a month, has one plausible payment, and is untouched. An acceptance criterion pins that (Franklin Ave rent must behave exactly as today).
  - **★ THE OBVIOUS ANSWER IS RECORDED AS REJECTED, because it reads convincing: THE INVOICE NUMBER DOES NOT SOLVE `O127`.** `BLS-88412` identifies the *invoice*; the **payment is an ACH bank line and carries no invoice number**, so it cannot link the two. It helps `O117` and is specced there. Sequence/Nth-to-Nth is rejected outright — one missing invoice shifts every later pairing SILENTLY, trading one invisible error for a compounding one.
  - **★★ THREE DEFECTS WERE FOUND BY THE PRE-REGISTERED CRITERIA AND THE MUTATION RUN, NOT BY WRITING THE CODE — and one of them was in a test of mine:**
    1. **The set rule sat BELOW the empty-candidate early return.** For this class an empty candidate list is MEANINGFUL (every charge already claimed IS the five-against-four case), and a period with no payments fell through to the PAIR rule, which reached back into the previous month and re-opened the whole bug.
    2. **★ THE RECOGNITION BAR WAS RE-OPENING THE BUG IT GUARDS.** "≥2 distinct periods" left a vendor's SECOND month unrecognised — the boundary case, which is exactly where the bug lives. Relaxed to ≥1 **because the two errors are not symmetric**: recognising too eagerly books a VISIBLE payable, recognising too late is the SILENT cross-period attach. **The bar belongs on the side that fails loudly.**
    3. **★★ A TEST OF MINE WAS VACUOUS, AND FIXING IT EXPOSED A DESIGN FLAW.** "A flat-fee vendor charging an unusual amount keeps the ordinary rule" used a 2-day gap, so removing the guard still produced a card — a different one — and `length >= 1` could not tell them apart. Fixed to a 7-day (on-rhythm) gap, which then **failed against MEAN/σ flatness**: a weekly $145 vendor with two one-off $620 charges left the class entirely and **every week of $145 noise came back**. **Mean and σ are the wrong statistics by construction — outliers are what we are excluding and they are what those two move most.** Now MEDIAN and MAD, matching `typicalIntervalDays`' median gap. *A vendor is not un-flat because it once bought something else.*
  - **★ THE DETECTION HALF DEVIATES FROM THE SPEC'S OWN §7, DELIBERATELY AND IN WRITING.** §7 proposed a MONTHLY COUNT (*"6 times in August; they normally charge 4"*). **A weekly vendor legitimately charges five times in a month with five Mondays**, so a monthly count fires on the CALENDAR rather than on the vendor — a category-1 card, precisely what §7 exists to remove. Implemented as **half the vendor's own median interval**: same property, no calendar unit, and the cadence is evidence we already held rather than a threshold being tuned.
  - **★ AND THE RESIDUE IS NAMED RATHER THAN LEFT TO BE DISCOVERED: the PDF filed against a given payment may be the wrong week's.** Both documents are in the library under the right vendor and period, every figure is correct, and the failure is visible — where today's is not. Accepted, in writing, with the reason.
- **★★ O127 SEQUENCED WITH O117, AND THE PROPERTY STATED (2026-08-27). ▶ HOLD — do not tune the threshold.**
  - **THE PROPERTY, in the operator's words and worth keeping exactly: FOR A FLAT-FEE RECURRING VENDOR, AMOUNT AND IDENTITY CARRY NO INFORMATION.** Everything the matcher normally reasons from is **constant**, so **any decision it reaches is being made by whatever variable is left — which today is a date window nobody chose for this purpose.** Bluebonnet: four payments, one vendor, $145.00 to the cent, weekly.
  - **★ SO THE FIX IS NOT A BETTER WINDOW. ▶ EXPLICIT CONSTRAINT: DO NOT TUNE THE THRESHOLD.** Widening it produces more ambiguity, narrowing it produces more payables, and **neither adds a bit of information** — the two documents remain indistinguishable on every axis the matcher reads. **Tuning would convert a diagnosable failure into a quieter one.**
  - **THE VENDOR CLASS NEEDS A DIFFERENT DISCRIMINATOR ENTIRELY, or must always ask.** Candidates, not a design: **invoice number** (`BLS-88412` is unique per delivery and we already store it) · **a delivery/service date on the document**, distinct from the invoice date · **sequence** (the Nth invoice matches the Nth payment) · or **detect the class and ALWAYS ASK**, which is honest and costs one card per delivery. **`vendor_state` already holds the amount band and the cadence needed to RECOGNISE the class** — that recognition is the shared half, and it is why this is one design with `O117` rather than two.
  - **WHY ONE ITEM AND NOT TWO: two thresholds where one rule belongs is how this gets worse.** `O117` and `O127` are the same property surfacing in the detector and in the matcher; designed apart they would grow two independent notions of "recurring", and the next symptom would get a third.
- **★★ O97 STEP 2 — THE DRAIN, PLANNING HALF (2026-08-28). NO MIGRATION, AND THE REASON IS EVIDENCE.**
  - **★ THE MIGRATION QUESTION, ANSWERED FROM THE LIVE TABLE: NO COLUMNS NEEDED.** `document_intake` already carries every piece of retry state — **`document_id`** says the bytes are durable (the gate step 1 opens), **`status`** is the state machine, **`updated_at`** is BOTH the lease and the spacing gate, **`received_at`** is the age. **And `document_intake_company_status_idx` is already exactly the drain's selection path**, so not even an index.
    - **`attempts` — CONSIDERED AND DEFERRED, with the trigger recorded rather than skipped silently.** It buys a nicer *report* (*"we tried 9 times"* vs *"we retried for 36 hours"*), not a correct *decision*; both statements are true and only one costs a column, and it is additive with a default whenever the reporting proves inadequate.
    - **`next_attempt_at` — REJECTED OUTRIGHT.** The thing we wait for is an AI budget that **resets on a clock hour**, so a fixed interval derived from that IS the schedule; a per-row time column would store the same number redundantly.
    - **★★ AND THE DEEPER REASON THERE IS NO COUNTER: TRANSIENT-vs-PERMANENT COMES FROM THE ERROR, NOT FROM A COUNT.** A 429 is transient however many times it happens; an unreadable file is permanent on the first try. A counter would only bound repeated *transient* failure — which the age box already does.
  - **★★★ AND THE NUMBER THAT MATTERS MORE THAN THE SCHEMA: `document_id` IS NULL ON ALL 150 LIVE ROWS.** Every one predates step 1, so **the drain's real input is EMPTY today** and stays empty until documents arrive under the new ordering. Two consequences, both recorded:
    1. **THE 21 `failed` ROWS FROM THE AUGUST DRIVE ARE PERMANENTLY UNRESUMABLE.** Those are the rate-limited invoices; their bytes only ever lived in `fileStoreRef`. **No drain can recover them — they need re-uploading** — and because `failed` is non-terminal the completeness net will nag about them forever. **Step 1 is forward-looking only, and that must not be discovered later as a surprise.**
    2. **THE SUITE IS WRITTEN AGAINST A POPULATION THAT DOES NOT EXIST YET, WHICH IS THE C195(7) TRAP EXACTLY.** So the assertions are **positive** — a specific pick count, a specific census — and one pins that **150 unresumable rows report as UNRESUMABLE, not as "all caught up"**. `planDrain` returns `drainable` and `unresumable` as first-class fields for that reason: **when `drainable` is 0 the honest report is "nothing is resumable", NOT "nothing to do".**
  - **DESIGN CHOICES THAT EARNED A COMMENT:** give-up is evaluated **before** pick, or an old row gets retried once more on its way out and the hold reason is written against an attempt that already failed *(mutation-tested — removing it turns 2 red)* · the box is **36 hours, generous on purpose**, because 240 documents at 20/hour is twelve hours of *legitimate* waiting and a tight box would abandon a healthy onboarding — **erring long costs a slow queue, erring short costs a lost file** · **oldest-first**, because newest-first starves exactly the documents someone has already waited on · an **unrecognised error is PERMANENT**, the conservative direction, since a document held with a stated reason is visible while one retried forever is not.
  - **THE COPY IS DERIVED FROM THE PLAN (§9).** `drainProgressCopy` reads the census: the finish time is **documents remaining ÷ our own hourly rate — arithmetic, not an estimate** — and when the rate is unknown (`O113b`'s shared bucket) it **states the counts and omits the time.** A test pins that it never says *email*, and never emits a spinner in place of a number it does not have.
- **★★ O97 STEP 2 — THE DRAIN, EXECUTION HALF. SHIPPED 2026-08-29 (C206). STUCK WORK NOW RESUMES ITSELF.** `src/lib/intakeDrainIo.js` — thin, injected client, shaped like `shadowIo`. Reads the drainable rows, calls `planDrain`, recovers the bytes from Storage, hands each pick to an injected `enqueue`. **It owns NO second processing path** — picks go back through the same pipeline a fresh drop uses, because a second path is a second place for the two to disagree (·3a).
  - **★★ ITS WRITE SURFACE IS `document_intake`, CHECKABLE BY READING THE FILE.** A test asserts no `.insert(`/`.update(`/`.delete(` of its own (every status write goes through the verified `setIntakeStatus`), no storage `.upload(`/`.remove(`, no booking primitive, and that `.from(` names only `document_intake` and `documents`.
  - **★★ THE RETRY CARRIES THE ORIGINAL `intake_id` AND NEVER CALLS `logIntake`.** A second arrival row for one document would corrupt the population the completeness ledger exists to independently own (O60). **A retry is the same arrival, tried again.** Mutation-verified: mint a fresh id → 2 red.
  - **★ `excludeIntakeIds`** — a file still genuinely running in THIS tab whose lease expired would otherwise be picked up and processed twice. **That is the `O123` double-post shape wearing an intake row.** Mutation-verified.
  - **★ "NOTHING TO FETCH" IS NOT "THE FETCH FAILED".** No `storage_path` → HELD with a stated reason; a download blip → left alone and retried. Collapsing them abandons a file over one network error. Mutation-verified.
  - **THE CATCH NOW CLASSIFIES INSTEAD OF COUNTING.** Every error used to land in `failed` (non-terminal), so an unreadable file was retried forever **and** the completeness net nagged about it forever. Now transient → `failed` (the drain retries; the age box, not a counter, ends it) and permanent → `held_for_review`, terminal and visible with the reason.
  - **THE COPY IS WIRED TO REAL NUMBERS, OR ABSENT.** `drainProgressCopy` existed, was tested, and was read by nobody — so `readDrainCensus` counts the actual population rather than handing it an invented denominator. **An unreadable census reports NOT-OK, never zero** — zero would render *"Nothing waiting."* over a full queue. `perHour` is null on purpose (`O113b`), so the sentence omits the finish time rather than inventing one.
  - **▶ FORWARD-LOOKING ONLY, AND IT MUST NOT BE DISCOVERED LATER AS A SURPRISE.** The 21 `failed` rows from the August drive have no `document_id` — their bytes only ever lived in `fileStoreRef`. They are counted as `unresumable` and the copy says they would need uploading again, **rather than reporting the queue as caught up.**

Both were in the spec's seed sketch; excluded with reasons rather than seeded thin to match it.

Rather than write a third cleverer regex, the test file now strips comment lines before scanning. **A guard that fails for a reason it does not mean is a guard nobody will trust.**

    | vendor | bank RESOLVE key | invoice READ key | |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | Roma Cheese & Dairy | `roma cheese and dairy` | `roma cheese and dairy` | ✅ |
    | Lone Star Restaurant Supply | `lone star restaurant supply` | `lone star restaurant supply` | ✅ |
    | Hill Country Milling | `hill country milling` | `hill country milling` | ✅ |
    | Austin Municipal Utilities | `austin municipal utilities` | `austin municipal utilities` | ✅ |
    | Bluebonnet Linen Service | `bluebonnet linen service` | `bluebonnet linen service` | ✅ |

Reusing that predicate keeps the two modules agreeing instead of growing a second definition of "this is payroll".

An **unrecognised source is EXCLUDED and counted, never guessed** — a wrong key merges two vendors and launders one's mapping onto the other's charges, a missing key books to suspense and flags, and those two errors are not symmetric. `identity_source: 'resolved' | 'read'` is carried per observation and surfaced as `identityMix` **in the report shape**, per the operator's instruction that a PROCEED resting on READ identities must not be able to hide.

The strategy's effect will only show once invoice-path rows are in the sample — which is itself the reason the merge test above was computed on strings rather than waiting for them.
- **★★ C201 — IDENTITY RESOLUTION NEEDS A PER-SOURCE STRATEGY (2026-08-25). The one-rule split is CONFIRMED BROKEN on two of four sources; `065` is blocked until this settles.**
  - **★ THE DEFECT, REPRODUCED IN CODE.** Payroll descriptions read `Gusto Payroll — 2026-02-28 – 2026-03-13`: an **EM-dash** after the vendor, an **EN-dash** between the period dates. `rawDescriptorOf` splits on the first `" – "` (en-dash) and therefore splits at the DATE RANGE — `rawDescriptorOf(...)` → `"2026-03-13"`, `entityKeyFor("2026-03-13")` → `null`. **Every payroll line resolves to nothing.** *(One correction to the report: it lands in `skipped.no_identity`, not `skipped.ambiguous_descriptor` — the split SUCCEEDS and yields a date. It is counted, so not silent, but it is indistinguishable from a genuine trace-number row.)* It has never fired in anger only because the backfill query filtered `source='bank_import'`; the unfiltered re-pull would have hit it immediately.
  - **★★ ROOT CAUSE — THERE ARE TWO DESCRIPTION-CONSTRUCTION PATHS, AND NEITHER KNOWS ABOUT THE OTHER.** `persistJournalEntry` (App.jsx:1386) builds `` `${invoice.vendor} – ${invoice.description}` `` — en-dash, vendor on the LEFT. `persistMultiLineEntry` (App.jsx:1482) takes `entry.description` **verbatim**, so payroll keeps whatever `payrollEntryForImport` made. And `payments.js:70` returns `` `Payment – ${vendor}` `` — a description that **already contains the separator**, which is why manual entries come out as `Vendor – Payment – Vendor` with three parts. The descriptor is a DISPLAY STRING assembled from structured data that already existed, and identity resolution has been trying to reverse-engineer it. **That is the actual defect; the em-dash is a symptom.**
  - **★ THE DECISIVE REFRAME: only ONE source has a descriptor problem at all.** Rail-stripping exists because bank descriptors are noisy. The other three sources carry a vendor as STRUCTURED DATA and never had variants to resolve — so for them the open-book objection does not apply, because there is nothing to resolve. Reading a field is not cheating on a test the field was never sitting for.

    | source | description shape | vendor identity | strategy |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `bank_import` | `Vendor – RAW BANK TEXT` | RIGHT half, genuinely noisy | **RESOLVE** (rail-strip + normalize) |
    | `payroll` | `Gusto Payroll — start – end` | none on the right; it is not a vendor mapping at all | **EXCLUDE** |
    | `universal_upload` | `Vendor. – PRODUCT DESCRIPTION` | LEFT half; right is a product | **READ** (normalize only) |
    | `manual` | `Vendor – Payment – Vendor` | LEFT half; right is a synthetic phrase | **READ** (normalize only) |

  - **★ PAYROLL SHOULD BE EXCLUDED FROM `vendor_state` ENTIRELY, not parsed.** A payroll register books to salaries / payroll tax / cash / taxes-payable by STRUCTURE, not by a vendor→account judgement — there is no mapping for the ladder to learn. **Amendment A §2 already excludes payroll register entries from shadow scoring**, so excluding them here makes the backfill agree with the criterion instead of quietly disagreeing with it.
  - **★★ AND THE GUSTO "BAND EVENT" IS A UNITS MISMATCH, NOT A SPEND CHANGE — this is why excluding payroll matters.** Gusto reads **3,150.00** via `bank_import` in Jan/Feb and **4,000.00 + 306.00** via `payroll` from March. Those are not the same quantity: **3,150 is NET pay** (gross 4,000 less 850 withholdings) and **4,000 is GROSS**. A unified entity would flag a 1.27× band event and the flag would be **wrong** — the spend did not change, the measurement did. A vendor whose amounts arrive from two different paths measuring two different things cannot have a meaningful band, which is a second, independent reason payroll does not belong in `vendor_state`.
  - **DORMANCY — one artefact, two real.** GUSTO was a query artefact, confirmed: it continues biweekly March–July under `source='payroll'`, hidden by the `bank_import` filter. **HARTLINE and the BANK MONTHLY SERVICE FEE are REAL** — a query across all three names returns nothing matching them after 2026-03-01. They genuinely stop in February, so **dormancy-on-arrival for those two is correct behaviour**, and the decay is the state machine working rather than a bug.
  - **▶ HOLD — `065`/the backfill does not proceed until the per-source strategy is settled.** A backfill built on a rule that fails on two of four sources would seed exactly the wrong entity keys, and a state machine seeded with wrong keys is inert on arrival and green.

  - **★★ CULINARY EDGE GRADUATES TO KNOWN — the specimen that founded the familiarity axis, promoted by the migration built to prevent it.** Two auto-bookings of $850.00 (04-14, 05-14), two signed months, agreeing mapping, identical amounts: it clears Q1 exactly. Specimen 3 is the never-seen vendor auto-booked at confidence 88 on name plausibility into the WRONG LEDGER CLASS — the program's only wrong-class entry. It would have arrived as a clean row in a green preview. **→ AMENDMENT B** (`docs/CALIBRATION_SPEC_O88_AMENDMENT_B.md`, drafted, unsigned): backfill graduation additionally requires ≥1 EXPLICITLY attested observation. Live Q1 unchanged.
    - **★ AND THE AMENDMENT DOES NOT DISCRIMINATE — it defaults. Say so.** `exception_resolved` is **null on all 63 live rows** and `recoded` cannot be derived at all, because `persistRecode` updates `account_id` IN PLACE and leaves no marker (O108). So **zero explicit observations exist** and the rule graduates **0 of 13**. It is adopted as a rule because it stays correct if the data ever becomes discriminating; it must never be REPORTED as a test it cannot perform. Every vendor starts STRANGER and re-earns KNOWN over two live months — Bluebonnet costs 2 cards instead of 7.
  - **★ TOAST SHOULD GRADUATE AND CANNOT — the exact inverse of Culinary Edge.** Three observations, three distinct months, ONE agreeing account (`a3edd514`) — a textbook KNOWN vendor. NO month-name stripping** — word-stripping is precisely the anti-merge risk the narrow-strip design exists to avoid. **Toast is instead the motivating case for the UNIVERSAL tier**: a national payment processor belongs in the curated directory with a canonical mapping, which resolves identity by RECOGNITION rather than string surgery. **Carried into C202's directory seed as a named test case.** If it still splits once the directory lands, a tested month-name rule gets revisited then.

But the band that would actually exist when April arrives is built from Jan+Feb only: mean 221.64, σ 10.29, band 201.06–242.21 — and **April's 487.20 lands OUTSIDE at 2.2× the pattern.** The band is built from attested history AT THE TIME, never retrospectively, and that is what makes it work. Worth watching in shadow.

So the resolver was tested on real data, **passed on Gusto and failed on Toast**. Lone Star is the constant one; the corpus is not uniformly flat. *(The C200 corpus guard still stands: the four Lone Star VARIANTS remain absent, so Specimen 2's flapping cause is still unrepresented.)*

Gusto vanishing after February is payroll moving paths, not a restaurant that stopped paying staff; a monthly bank fee ending in February is likewise implausible. **A re-pull without those two filters is owed before the planner is touched.**
- **★★ C201 — TWO FINDINGS FROM THE BACKFILL EXTRACTION (2026-08-23), both about the EVIDENCE rather than the code.**
  - **★ THE OPEN-BOOK PROBLEM.** `journal_entries.description` on a bank line is **not** the raw bank descriptor — it is `"Resolved Vendor Name – RAW BANK TEXT"`, en-dash separated (`ledger.js:31` splits the same way for display). Resolving identity from the FULL string would have graded the resolver against a string **containing its own answer**: every variant resolves perfectly because the resolved name is sitting in the input, and the backfill reports flawless grouping while proving nothing about the function under test. An open-book exam scored as closed-book. **Caught by the operator before the planner ran.** Fixed: `rawDescriptorOf` takes the RIGHT half only, and a row with no separator is **excluded and counted** (`skipped.ambiguous_descriptor`) rather than silently scored on the contaminated string — falling back to the whole string is the contaminated case in disguise. Two tests pin it from both sides: same raw text under DIFFERENT resolved names must group as ONE vendor; one resolved name over two different raw texts must stay TWO.

  - **JANUARY DELETED TWINS — re-import residue, correctly excluded.** Every January line has a deleted twin (same descriptor, amount and date; one soft-deleted, one not). The planner drops deleted lines before anything else and counts them; a test holds it. Without that, every January vendor would have **doubled its observation count** — and since Q1 needs two observations, **a one-month vendor would have graduated on a single month's re-import.** Q1's distinct-months rule would have caught it anyway, which is a second reason that rule is stored as `text[]` rather than a count.

    1. **`accounts.origin` column** (`seed|runtime|external|import`), migration + backfill. Cleanest semantically — the detector becomes `system_role IS NULL AND origin <> 'external'` and stays honest as new doors appear. Costs a column on a core table and a decision about what existing rows are.

If yes, this option is off.

Client: Owner Equity · Sales Revenue · role `cost_of_goods_sold` · role `rent` · **`6300` = Repairs & Maintenance**. But the guard is what saves it, not the grant — and the repo believes the grant was removed.
