  · **(a)** evidence column + 7-column `anomaly_comments` ✓ · **(b)** RLS on, **1 SELECT / 1 INSERT / 0 mutating policies** — a comment cannot be edited or removed ✓ · **(e)** the live `with_check` reads back exactly as written ✓.
  · **★ (c) PASSED AND THE `[ran as: authenticated]` TAG IS WHAT MAKES IT MEAN ANYTHING:** *"PASS - refused: new row violates row-level security policy"*. **The probe reports the role it actually ran as because the Supabase editor runs as a superuser, which BYPASSES RLS** — the first draft of this probe ran a bare UPDATE and would have reported **`FAIL - a dismissal with NO REASON was accepted` against a perfectly correct migration.** A false FAIL on a correct guard is the mirror of a false PASS on a broken one; both come from testing something other than the thing you named.
  · **★★ AND (d) AS FIRST WRITTEN COULD PASS VACUOUSLY — FOUND BY READING IT, NOT BY RUNNING IT.** Under RLS an UPDATE whose `USING` clause hides the row touches **zero rows and raises nothing**, so *"no exception"* would have been recorded as a pass by a probe that never reached the thing it was testing. It now `get diagnostics row_count` and reports **INCONCLUSIVE on zero**, never PASS. Same shape as C195(7)'s guard whose input was always empty.
  · **★★★ (f) RETURNED `PASS - a non-reviewer (owner) was refused` — THE REVIEWER GATE IS PROVEN END TO END, AND THE TECHNIQUE MATTERS MORE THAN THE RESULT.** (d) returned PASS with `row_count = 1`, so the housekeeping was genuinely exercised.
  · **★★★ TWO OF THE THREE PASSED LIVE 2026-08-30, RUN AS `authenticated`.** **TIER 1 #8's DATABASE HALF IS NOW PROVED** — *"PASS - a non-reviewer (owner) was refused: new row violates row-level security policy for table `period_signoffs`"*, the check the operator's own login structurally could not perform. And **`066` VERIFY (d) is closed**: the one genuinely cross-tenant table in the product refused a normal member's write.
  · **★★ AND s4 CAME BACK `INCONCLUSIVE - saw 0 of their rows AND 0 of my own`, WHICH IS THE ANTI-VACUITY GUARD EARNING ITS PLACE ON ITS FIRST RUN.** The only non-platform-admin member owns a company with no entries. **Without the `mine = 0` branch it would have printed PASS and s4 would have been closed on a probe that proved nothing** — the exact failure the guard was written against, occurring immediately. The probe now SEEDS a row in the borrower's own company inside the transaction it rolls back, so `mine > 0` means *RLS let this user see a row that genuinely exists in their company* — the real counterpart of `theirs = 0`. **Re-run: `PASS - saw 1 of my own rows and 0 of theirs [ran as: authenticated]`. ALL THREE PROBES PASS.**
  · **▶ WHAT s4 STILL OWES, STATED PRECISELY: the rung asks for an AUTOMATED test, and this is a REPRODUCIBLE SCRIPT.** That is a real advance over *"the memory of an afternoon"* — it has a verdict, it refuses to pass vacuously, and anyone can re-run it — **but a script nobody runs decays exactly like a memory.** Automating it needs live credentials in CI, which is its own decision; until that is made, s4 stays open and the evidence is dated.
  · **★★★ THE UNLOCK: THREE ITEMS PARKED ON "WE NEED A NON-PLATFORM-ADMIN TEST USER" WERE NEVER BLOCKED ON THE ACCOUNT — THEY WERE BLOCKED ON THE TECHNIQUE.** Borrowing another member's uid via `request.jwt.claims` + `set_config('role','authenticated')` makes the policies evaluate for THEM, from the SQL editor, today. `supabase/verify/nonreviewer_rls_probes.sql` now carries all three: **TIER 1 #8**'s sign-off DB-layer rejection (never proved, because the operator's own login *cannot* fail it), **`066` VERIFY (d)**'s cross-tenant directory write, and **s4**'s cross-tenant read — which had been *"the memory of an afternoon"* since 2026-08-09.
  · **★★ AND THE FIRST DRAFT OF THOSE PROBES WOULD HAVE REPORTED A TYPO AS THE WALL HOLDING.** They caught `when others` and called it PASS — **and probe (2)'s insert named three columns that do not exist on `universal_vendor_directory`**, so it would have "passed" without the policy being consulted at all. Only `insufficient_privilege` (42501) counts; anything else is INCONCLUSIVE. **This is `076`'s recorded rule — any other error is reported as ITSELF — and I had to be taught it twice in one file.**
  · **★★ THE REVIEWER HALF IS PROVABLE TODAY, AND I HAD RECORDED THE OPPOSITE. `is_platform_admin()` RESOLVES THE EMAIL FROM `auth.uid()`, SO THE BYPASS IS KEYED ON WHICH USER THE PROBE BORROWS — NOT ON WHO IS SITTING AT THE EDITOR.** Borrow a member who is neither the platform admin nor admin/accountant (an OWNER of their own company qualifies — `is_company_reviewer` is admin-or-accountant only) and give the dismissal a **perfectly good reason**; the only thing left that can refuse it is the reviewer test. Probe (f). **The original claim — "the reviewer half cannot be proved from that account at all" — was true of the OPERATOR's uid and I generalised it to the session.** Recorded rather than quietly corrected: it is the same over-broad move as the `O113` retraction, one layer down.
  · **THE DISMISSAL GATE ITSELF WAS A REAL FIND, NOT A FEATURE.** `056` states in its own header that reviewer-only dismissal was UI-enforced and the DB boundary was membership. Dismissal clears a flag **and** feeds `priorDismissalFor`, which downgrades later flags for the same vendor and amount — **so a viewer could lower the guard against a repeat of the thing they cleared.** Same class as `061` and `078`.
  · **THE POLICY DISCRIMINATES BY THE RESULTING ROW (the `079` lesson, applied deliberately this time):** auto-resolve, sign-off expiry, reopen and `last_seen` bumps all run as whoever is logged in — **including an OWNER, who is not a reviewer** — so a blanket requirement would have switched off anomaly housekeeping for every owner-run company. Only a row that ends up `dismissed` needs a reviewer. **(d) is the check that proves that, which is exactly why it is not being written off as a formality while it is unrun.**

- **★★★ `O35` — AUDIT RUN LIVE 2026-08-30, AND IT CLOSES THE ITEM. THE THING `O35` WAS ABOUT DOES NOT EXIST ANY MORE.**
  - **★★★ QUERY 2 RETURNED NO ROWS AND QUERY 4 CONFIRMS IT: `roleless_ours = 0`.** Every account this application created carries a role. **`O35`'s "safe half" — v1-era accounts with NULL roles, invisible to `getAccountByRole` — is EMPTY.** The 36 role-less accounts are the `O110` foreign-chart set, exactly as predicted and already excluded by design.
  - **★★ AND THE ONE VARIANT IS HARMLESS BY CONSTRUCTION, WHICH IS THE ROLE SCHEME EARNING ITS KEEP.** `payroll_tax` sits on **`5101` on some companies and `6010` on others, across 11** — the v1/v2 split the item itself named. **Both carry the role, so `getAccountByRole('payroll_tax')` resolves on either**, and companies are separate tenants so cross-company code comparability buys nothing. **§4's whole argument was "users renumber, so never hardcode a code" — this is that argument being VINDICATED by a live audit rather than asserted.** Renumbering would re-point journal lines to fix something that is not broken.
  - **★★ THE REAL FINDING IS IN QUERY 1, AND IT IS A DIFFERENT ITEM: `opening_balance_equity` IS MISSING ON SEVEN OF ELEVEN COMPANIES.** That is `O108` finding 3 confirmed live — it is absent from the seed and reaches the chart only when someone posts an opening balance, via `ensureAccount`. **It works by accident, through the materialise-mid-flight path `C223`/`C254` exist to avoid.** Added to the universal template list on that evidence — **and then checked how far that actually reaches, which was less far than it sounded.**
  - **★★ `applyCoaTemplate` RUNS FROM EXACTLY ONE PLACE: saving the business profile.** So the template change covers a NEW company that completes onboarding, and **nothing else** — the nine existing companies would not have got it, and neither would a company that never sets a business type. **Stating that rather than letting "added to the template" imply "fixed everywhere".**
  - **MIGRATION `083` ▶ HOLD closes the existing-company half:** additive only, skipping on **the role OR the code** (a chart may hold it under a different number — the very reason the app resolves by role — or hold `3400` under a different name; either way inserting would create a duplicate `byRole` then picks between silently). Labelled **`origin = 'seed'`**, because `070` defaults to `'runtime'` deliberately and **an unlabelled backfill would arrive in the `O108` detector as accounts the system appears to have invented on its own.** Its VERIFY checks for **duplicates, not presence** — *"at least one"* is not the check when duplication is the plausible failure.
  - **★★★ APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30: (a) 11 companies, fewest 1, most 1 · (b) 0 lines on the new accounts · (d) idempotent. AND (c) RETURNED A FALSE FAIL, WHICH WAS MY CHECK BEING WRONG.** It asked whether EVERY opening-balance account is labelled `seed` — but pre-existing ones carry whatever origin they were created with, and **one is `runtime` BY A RECORDED DECISION**: `073` deliberately left Franklin Ave's that way, because *"origin records HOW A ROW GOT HERE"* and relabelling would erase the only durable evidence the materialisation path ever fired on a real client's chart. **The check asserted a property a deliberate decision violates.**
  - **★★ AND A NUMBER I STATED REPEATEDLY WAS WRONG, CORRECTED BY THE MIGRATION'S OWN OUTPUT.** I said **"seven of eleven"** companies lacked the account — in the report, in `CLAUDE.md`, in `ROADMAP.md` and in the migration's header. **`083` created NINE.** Counting the audit's rows properly: three companies missing 31 roles plus six missing three, all including `opening_balance_equity`; Northwind was missing only two and not that one. **I counted ten rows by eye and got the subset wrong** — §11's own rule, *"estimates about production data are claims about a query nobody ran"*, applied to me, on the same day I wrote a query specifically to avoid it. *(Precedent kept deliberately: `·3c`'s "14 payroll runs" was an estimate carried forward through a spec and a report until the backfill said 10.)*
  - **★★ AND VERIFY (b) TWO LINES ABOVE IT SCOPED CORRECTLY — same file, same migration, one check scoped to the rows it created and one not.** The unscoped one failed correct data. **A false FAIL on a correct migration is the mirror of a false PASS on a broken one**, and this is the second time today the same shape appeared (the `080` probes that would have reported a superuser bypass as the guard failing). Corrected to scope by `created_at`, and a test now pins that the origin check is scoped.
  - **▶ AND THE HALF IT DOES NOT CLOSE IS NAMED: `seed_company_accounts` STILL LACKS `3400`.** A company that never saves a business profile still relies on the create-on-demand door. Editing that function requires starting from `pg_get_functiondef` (§6, five definitions in the repo and the newest never applied) — **its own task with its own verification, and bundling it into a backfill is how `063` nearly dropped 17 accounts.**
  - **`merchant_processing_fees` and `bank_service_charges` missing on EIGHT of eleven** is `O112` confirmed on live data — already fixed by `C254`, and the audit is the evidence that the fix was aimed at something real.

- **★★★ C264 — `O120`: WHICH QUESTIONS DESERVE TO STOP YOU (2026-08-30). ▶ THE HOLD SAID "no design"; THE CALL IS MADE HERE AND SAID SO.**
  - **The old banner read *"⚠ N invoices need your input before booking — scroll down to review"*: a bare count and an instruction to scroll.** Ten cards sat unanswered through the August drive while the header said the books were correct.
  - **★★★ THE QUESTION WAS NEVER "MODAL OR NOT", IT IS *WHICH CARDS EARN AN INTERRUPTION* — and the discriminator was already recorded: a card whose wrong answer books SILENTLY wrong may stop you; one whose wrong answer is visible and undoable waits its turn.** The `O114` lifecycle card is the archetype of the first — *"different purchase"* mints a phantom payable, *"same purchase"* suppresses a real charge, **and the more dangerous of the two leaves nothing on any screen.** A low-confidence category question is the archetype of the second: wrong is visible in the account and a recode undoes it.
  - **★★ AND IT IS ORDERING, NOT INTERRUPTING. NOTHING OPENS A MODAL — a test asserts the module contains no modal, dialog or confirm at all.** A pop-up per card **turns a sitting into a gauntlet**, which is the opposite of *"book everything and batch the judgement to close"*. The dangerous ones simply come FIRST and are counted by name, so **they cannot be buried behind the harmless ones. "Louder" was never the fix.**
  - **★★★ AN UNCLASSIFIED CARD *WAITS*, AND THE DEFAULT DIRECTION IS THE WHOLE SAFETY OF THIS.** Drifting into "stops" rebuilds the gauntlet the batch exists to avoid, and an unknown card is far likelier to be an ordinary category question. **Adding a kind to the stopping set is a decision someone makes; arriving there is not.**
  - **★ KEYED ON WHAT A WRONG ANSWER *DOES*, NOT ON SEVERITY OR AMOUNT.** A $40 lifecycle card stops you and a $40,000 category question waits — **the question is whether you could TELL, not how much is at stake.**
  - **★ THE BANNER NAMES THE KINDS**, because a bare count makes ten harmless questions look like ten problems, **which is how a person learns to ignore the number entirely** — and when nothing is dangerous it no longer sounds like an alarm.
  - **★ FIFTH COMMENT-GUARD FALSE POSITIVE:** the no-modal test matched the file's own explanation of why there is no modal. Comments stripped, as every previous instance ended up doing.

- **★★ C263 — REUSING WHAT WE ALREADY READ FROM A DOCUMENT (2026-08-30). `O113` proposal 3, accepted 2026-08-26. **Migration `084` APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30 — two checks, two passes:** both columns present · **91 documents, 0 carrying a stored reading** (additive as designed — **a stored answer nobody produced is an answer nobody can trust**, so a non-zero count would have been a FAILURE rather than a head start) · the partial index is on `(company_id, content_hash) WHERE content_hash IS NOT NULL`, the same pair C193's dedupe already uses. Ledger: through `061`, plus `063`, `064`, `066`, `068`–`084`.**
  - Identical bytes re-uploaded re-ran the whole pipeline. **C193 already hashes every document to dedupe the library, so the key costs nothing new** — and the August drive re-uploaded twice, so this is a path people take rather than a hypothetical.
  - **★★★ THE WHOLE DESIGN IS IN *WHICH* ANSWERS MAY BE REUSED, NOT IN THE REUSING.** What KIND of document it is, and what was ON it, are **properties of the bytes** — safe. **WHICH ACCOUNT it belongs to is a property of the COMPANY'S CHART, which changes** (two migrations added accounts today, learned mappings move, accounts get renamed). **Caching it would pin a booking decision to a chart that no longer exists — the same document re-uploaded after a chart change would book to yesterday's answer, silently, and look identical to a correct one.** Never cached, and `extractionToStore` **drops a coding if it is handed one**, so the omission is structural rather than a convention a caller can forget.
  - **★★ `EXTRACTION_VERSION` IS WHAT STOPS THE CACHE OUTLIVING THE THING IT CACHED.** If the extraction prompt changes, every stored answer came from a model that no longer exists here — **reusing it would make a prompt fix invisible on exactly the documents most likely to be re-uploaded.** It is a **hand-set constant, deliberately not derived**: a hash of the prompt file would invalidate on a typo fix and keep on a semantic change, and **only a person can tell those apart.**
  - **★ AN ENTRY THAT CANNOT ANSWER IS A MISS, NOT A HIT.** An empty extraction would otherwise short-circuit into *"we read this and found nothing"* — the `O98` shape with an AI call's cost saved and the answer thrown away. **Each miss names its kind** (`none` / `stale_version` / `unreadable` / `empty`), so *"the cache never hits"* has somewhere to look.
  - **★ AND A REUSE IS SAID OUT LOUD**, because a re-upload that finishes instantly and silently looks like it did nothing at all — the `O128` shape, where the best available outcome read as a null result.

- **★★★ C261 — THE HIGHEST-VOLUME BOOKING PATH WAS NOT IN THE GAAP GUARDRAIL (2026-08-30).**
  - **§12 says every economic event gets a pure builder with a test asserting exact Dr/Cr, validated against `gaapInvariants`. `buildBankLineEntry` had the builder and not the validation.** It was tested for its SHAPE in five files while **no test ever ran it through Dr=Cr, the accounting equation, or the net-income rules** — and **statements are how most transactions enter these books.** Every drive to date has been statement-shaped. **A builder that produces most of the ledger belongs in the file that checks the ledger.** Both directions added (money out, money in), expanded the same way `persistJournalEntry` posts them so the invariants run on **what actually reaches the books**.
  - **★★ AND THE INTERESTING PART IS A HAZARD THAT IS CURRENTLY HARMLESS.** The builder decides debit-or-credit from **`txn.type === "revenue"`** — the denormalized-flag derivation §9 names as the root cause of the whole `O73` bank-matching class. **It is safe today ONLY BY VIRTUE OF SOMETHING UPSTREAM:** `bank_statement_lines` has a `direction` column and **no `type` column**, so the pipeline's `l.type || (l.direction === "in" ? …)` always falls through to the statement's own answer.
  - **★★★ "CORRECT BECAUSE OF A SCHEMA NOBODY IS THINKING ABOUT" IS THE SAME SHAPE AS THE REVERSAL GUARD THAT WORKED ONLY BECAUSE A QUERY UPSTREAM FILTERED DELETED ROWS.** So the invariant is pinned directly: **the direction the books get is the direction the STATEMENT reported**, and a test reads `058` and fails if a `type` column ever appears on those lines. **If that day comes, the flag would take precedence over the statement and book money the wrong way round — silently.**
  - **★ AND THE NO-DIRECTION DEFAULT IS PINNED AS "EXPENSE", WITH THE REASON: booking an unknown line as an expense understates income and surfaces in review; booking it as revenue inflates income and looks like good news, which is the direction nobody checks.**

- **★★★ C260 — `O109`: THE ASSISTANT MAY RECOGNISE AN ACCOUNT, NEVER INVENT ONE (2026-08-30).**
  - **`resolveAccountId` IS REACHED FROM THE AI ACTION PATH** (`add_rule` / `set_contact_rule` / `add_recurring`), so **a number the model produced could mint a PERMANENT account on a client's chart** — and the client asked a question in chat, not for their chart to be reorganised. It created whatever it was handed: **audibly in the audit log, silently on screen.**
  - **★★ THE SPLIT IS BETWEEN RECOGNISING AND INVENTING, AND "ALWAYS REFUSE" WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG.** A code in the canonical chart that this company merely **predates** is recognisable — **exactly what the `O35` audit found for merchant fees on eight of eleven companies.** Refusing it would break *"make a rule for Marketing"* on a company lacking `6300`: **real work, refused for a bookkeeping reason the person cannot see.** A code in NEITHER chart is a number produced from nothing, and **there is no reading under which inventing a general-ledger account on someone's books is the right answer to a chat message.** That one is refused.
  - **★ THE RECOGNISABLE CASE IS NOW SAID OUT LOUD** — *"Added 6520 Merchant Processing Fees to your chart of accounts — you didn't have one."* §9: **an action whose effect is invisible will be repeated**, and a person who cannot see that their chart changed has no way to disagree with it. The `account_materialized` audit action is **kept unchanged**, because the `O108` detector reads it and renaming it would blind an instrument that took a migration and a live diagnosis to restore.
  - **★ THE REFUSAL NAMES THE TWO THINGS THEY CAN ACTUALLY DO** — add it in Settings, or name an existing account. *"I won't do that"* with no way forward is a dead end, and this is a chat surface where the next thing they type depends on what we just said.
  - **★★ AND A TEST OF MINE FAILED ON CORRECT CODE BECAUSE ITS WINDOW WAS A CHARACTER COUNT.** It sliced 400 characters looking for a `return null` that sits ~450 in, behind two long strings. **A count is a guess about how long code is; the closing brace is where the block actually ends.** Sixth mechanical slip of the day, same family, caught the same way.

- **★★ C257 — `O35`: THE PER-COMPANY ROLE AUDIT (2026-08-30). `supabase/verify/O35_role_audit.sql`. A REPORT, AND DELIBERATELY NOT A FIX.**
  - **THE DEFECT IS THAT AN ACCOUNT CAN BE ON THE BALANCE SHEET AND INVISIBLE TO THE CODE.** Companies were seeded by different chart versions, every role-resolved feature looks up `system_role`, and a v1-era account has NULL there. It surfaced through payroll first, and **fixing payroll's roles alone did not close it** — which is why the item asks for an audit across ALL roles.
  - **★★ THE SPLIT BETWEEN SAFE AND UNSAFE IS THE POINT.** Setting a role on an existing account is safe: nothing moves. **Renumbering a code is not** — journal lines point at accounts, and moving a code without re-pointing them **silently re-files history**. The two must never be done by the same script, so the audit lists them separately, proposes neither, and **a test asserts the file contains no `update`, `insert`, `delete` or `alter` at all.**
  - **★ THE SAFE LIST EXCLUDES `O110`'s 36 FOREIGN-CHART ACCOUNTS.** Giving them our roles would collide — **two accounts claiming `utilities` on one company, with `byRole` silently keeping whichever it saw last.** A booking hazard introduced to tidy a report.
  - **★★★ AND THE AUDIT CARRIES A COPY OF THE 59-ROLE CANONICAL LIST, WHICH IS THE FAILURE MODE THIS FILE KEEPS PAYING FOR — a printed census cannot be wrong, only old.** So it is GENERATED from `constants.js` and a test regenerates it and compares, in both directions: **a role added to the chart and not to the audit fails**, and so does **a role in the audit that the chart does not have** (which would report false gaps on every company). Without that, adding a role would quietly stop the audit asking about it, and **every company would look complete.**

- **★★★ C256 — 1099 ELIGIBILITY IS DERIVED NOW, NOT TICKED (2026-08-30). `src/lib/form1099.js`. **WIRED THE SAME DAY, DELIBERATELY** — a careful derivation nothing consults is exactly the `O95` shape this file spends pages on, and *"logic only, a screen later"* is how a field with no reader gets written.**
  - **★★ THE TAX PAGE NOW COUNTS FROM THE DERIVATION, and the old flag count is GONE rather than merely unused.** Payments are grouped on the **same key the vendor list uses (`O111`)**, so a supplier known by two names is one supplier here too — otherwise their payments split and **both halves fall under the threshold, which is a wrong answer that looks tidy.**
  - **★ AND AN EXISTING TEST REQUIRED THE TAX PAGE TO READ THE FLAG.** It pinned the `is1099` / `is_1099` naming fix using *"TaxView mentions `is1099`"* as its evidence — so **not reading the flag at all failed it.** Rewritten to name the property (**no component touches the raw snake_case column**; only the load mapping and the save payload may). **A test that names a reader blocks the reader from improving.**
  - **THE STAKE IS NOT TIDINESS: A 1099 IS FILED WITH THE IRS UNDER THE ACCOUNTANT'S NAME.** The live finding that opened this: on one company **nearly every supplier carried the flag** — food, equipment, a utility — **none of them 1099-NEC reportable**, because the badge was effectively defaulted on. **Wrong flags become wrong filings.**
  - **★★★ THE RULE THE FILE EXISTS FOR: AN UNKNOWN ENTITY TYPE IS NOT "NOT A CORPORATION".** Treating unknown as not-exempt **files 1099s for corporations**; treating it as exempt **misses real ones**. Neither is defensible, so it is neither — `NEEDS_INFO` is a first-class verdict. **`O98` applied where it costs money.**
  - **THREE INPUTS, ALL DERIVED:** *what they were paid* (from the LEDGER, with a correcting credit REDUCING the total, and voided rows not counting) · *what for* (from the ACCOUNT's `system_role`, never the supplier's name — §9's "the account fixes the category") · *what they are* (stated `business_type` first, the name only as weak evidence).
  - **★★ AND THE RULE MOST OFTEN MISSED IS ENCODED: LEGAL AND MEDICAL PAYMENTS ARE REPORTABLE EVEN TO A CORPORATION.** A law firm that is a corporation still gets one.
  - **★ GOODS ARE A DIFFERENT ANSWER FROM "UNDER THE FLOOR", AND IT SAYS WHICH.** One changes next year when they are paid more; the other never does. Reporting them identically is how a supplier gets re-examined every January for no reason.
  - **★★ `utilities` IS DELIBERATELY ABSENT FROM THE ROLE MAP, and a test pins that.** Guessing it is *exactly* how the badge ended up on a utility company. An unclassifiable payment above the floor becomes `NEEDS_INFO` rather than a quiet *"below the floor"* — which would be a claim about a query.
  - **★★ THE HEADLINE COUNTS THE UNSURE ONES TOO.** A proposal reporting only what it is certain of understates the work, and **the unsure ones are precisely the ones a person has to act on.** *(Five mutations, five red — including "treat unknown as reportable", which is the failure mode that costs money.)*

- **★★ C255 — `O129`: THE REPAIR TOOL THAT HAD NO BUTTON (2026-08-30).** `attachDepreciationToExistingAsset` builds a depreciation schedule for equipment already in the books — real, idempotent, owner/admin-gated, **and called by nothing**, because the control it sat behind was removed for a good reason: **it asked you to type a raw journal-entry id, on the page where you read your financial statements.**
  - **★★ THE ANSWER WAS NEITHER "DELETE IT" NOR "PUT THE BOX BACK".** The capability is legitimate — equipment reaches the books through paths that never create a schedule (a QuickBooks import, a hand-entered correction, anything predating the feature) and **without one it sits on the balance sheet at full cost forever.** What was wrong was WHERE it lived. **A repair tool with no button is invisible; a repair tool that asks for a database id is unusable.** It now sits on the entry that needs repairing, where the id is already known and **nobody types anything** — pinned by a test that fails if an id input ever reappears.
  - **★ AND IT OFFERS ITSELF ONLY WHERE IT COULD APPLY: a DEBIT to a fixed-asset account.** A credit is a disposal, and scheduling depreciation against one would be **depreciating a removal**. Anywhere else it would be a control that exists to be declined, which is how a screen teaches you to ignore it.
  - **★ THE VALIDATOR CATCHES SALVAGE ABOVE COST — depreciation that ADDS value** — at the point where the message can be about the number the person typed, rather than in the schedule builder where it would be about an internal total they never see.
  - **★★★ AND A SURVIVING MUTATION TURNED OUT TO INDICT THE CODE, NOT THE TEST.** Deleting an explicit *"did we resolve a fixed-asset account?"* guard changed nothing — an empty set matches nothing, so the membership test already returned false. **The line read as a check and could not affect the outcome: the `if (error)` shape in a different costume.** Removed, with the reasoning kept in both files. **A surviving mutation is a question, not a verdict — sometimes the answer is that the line could never have mattered.**

- **★★ C254 — `O112` WAS NOT A DESIGN QUESTION (2026-08-30).** The item asked: we recognise Toast, Square and Stripe by name, but ten of eleven companies have no card-processing account — **create it, ask first, or leave it parked?**
  - **★★ `6520` AND `6530` ARE ALREADY IN THE DEFAULT CHART.** `068` blessed them after a CPA created them by hand on a real client, so **every company created since gets them** and the ten that don't are simply **older than that decision**. The question is a BACKFILL wearing a design question's clothes.
  - **★★★ AND THE ANSWER TURNS ON *WHEN*, NOT *WHETHER*. Materialising an account MID-BOOKING is the `O108`/`O109` hazard — the system inventing a line on a client's chart while nobody is looking. Adding one as a deliberate, audited SETUP step is what `C223`'s template path already does safely: additive, never renaming, never touching an account that carries transactions. Same account, same code — the difference is entirely that a person is present.**
  - **THEY ARE UNIVERSAL, NOT INDUSTRY-SPECIFIC**, so they apply to every business type **including `Other`** — whose industry list stays deliberately empty. *"A business we cannot name is one whose chart we should not be opinionated about"* is about INDUSTRY opinions; **every business has a bank account.**
  - **★ THE ROLES GO WITH THEM.** A code with no `system_role` is invisible to every `getAccountByRole` lookup (the `O110` finding) — adding the account without the role would have looked like a fix and been one only by coincidence of numbering.
  - **★★ AND AN EXISTING TEST NAMED *"does NOT re-add merchant fees — the generic chart already has them"* NOW READS AS A REJECTION OF THIS AND IS NOT ONE.** It runs against a company holding the full default chart, so the dedupe correctly skips. **Renamed to say "TO A COMPANY THAT ALREADY HAS THEM" and extended to assert the planner DOES add them to a company without them** — the difference is the company, not the rule, and a test whose title states the general case would have blocked this work for the next reader.

- **★★ C253 — `O122`: THE CARD RATE IS MEASURED NOW (2026-08-30). `src/lib/cardRate.js`.**
  - **THE ITEM SAID *"from now on we count them separately every time we test"* AND NOTHING COUNTED ANYTHING.** Every drive and suite here scores INDIVIDUAL behaviours, so **we can end with every behaviour correct and a screen carrying nine cards** — and unit tests structurally cannot catch that: one asserts a card is RIGHT, never that there are few enough of them.
  - **★★★ CATEGORY 1 IS A DEFECT COUNT AND IS NEVER AVERAGED INTO THE RATE.** **A single category-1 card fails the run however small a share of the documents it is.** The test builds a deliberately flattering denominator so the assertion has something to overrule, and **the numbers in it are a FIXTURE, not a measurement** — first written as *"a 1% rate"*, which the operator correctly read as a claim that one in a hundred records fails to save. **It is not true of anything:** `record_failed` has been observed exactly once, firing on **THREE OF THREE** attempts from the `patch`/`values` parameter bug, and never since that fix. The real history is *broken, then fixed* — never a low background rate. **A percentage in an example reads as an observed frequency, and this one did within the hour.** It fails the run because *"a card the user sees every month is a bug wearing a question mark"* and a bug's expected value is zero. **Averaging is how a defect becomes "within tolerance".**
  - **★★ AN UNRECOGNISED CARD IS REPORTED, NEVER DEFAULTED.** Bucketing an unknown kind into "judgement" would flatter the number **by exactly the amount we do not understand** — and a new card kind nobody classified is the most likely place for a category-1 defect to hide. The report says the rate is unreliable while any remain, and **a test asserts every anomaly type and every `ASK_REASON` the product can actually emit is in the taxonomy**, so a new detector cannot arrive unmeasured.
  - **★ THE SPLIT IS THE DELIVERABLE, NOT THE TOTAL, AND A TEST DEMONSTRATES WHY:** a month whose total FALLS while category 2 stays flat is the teaching not sticking — invisible in an aggregate, obvious in the split.
  - **★ ONBOARDING HAS NO TARGET RATHER THAN BORROWING STEADY STATE'S.** Conflating them would either make onboarding look broken or make steady state look fine; *"no target set for this mode yet"* is the honest output.
  - **★★ AND THE TAXONOMY RECORDS WHAT CHANGED CATEGORY.** `duplicate_payment`, `large_transaction`, `round_number` and `missing_recurring` were all category 1 **while a bug was live** — payroll tripping the large-charge detector, staleness measured against wall-clock, duplicates on lifecycle pairs. All four are fixed, all four are now category 3, and **each entry says it used to be a defect**, because a taxonomy that silently re-grades itself hides the improvement it exists to show.

- **★★★ C252 — `O116`: PAYROLL IS HANDLED, NOT HANDED OVER (2026-08-30). A REFACTOR BEFORE IT WAS A FEATURE.**
  - **THE BLOCKER WAS STRUCTURAL, NOT BEHAVIOURAL: the whole payroll pipeline (148 lines) LIVED INSIDE `PayrollView`.** A file dropped on Home could therefore only ever be ROUTED to that screen — and on the **CLIENT seat it was stashed UNPARSED** with *"we've saved that for your accountant"*, so **dropping a register did nothing at all** until somebody else opened a different screen, possibly days later. The home promise is *"drop anything here and we'll handle the rest"*; payroll was the one thing that answered *"go to this other page"*.
  - **★★ MOVED, NOT COPIED — the ·3a rule, applied as the reason the work took this shape.** Two implementations of one contract is how the payroll gate shipped inert: both halves look tested and drift apart. The drop zone, the Home queue and the manual Post button now run **the same code** and cannot disagree about what a register does. A test asserts the view holds **no** poster, gate or request-builder of its own.
  - **★ IT RUNS IN PLACE ON BOTH SEATS BECAUSE THE GATE ALREADY DECIDES THE HARD PART.** A standard register that proves itself POSTS (·3a); anything else is held with the failing reasons attached. So a client gets an OUTCOME — their payroll is in the books — or an honest hold. **THE OTHER THREE STILL ROUTE, DELIBERATELY:** a bank statement opens a reconciliation a person drives, a contract needs terms confirmed, QuickBooks is a mapping exercise. **Payroll is the one whose clean path is fully decided by a gate we already trust.**
  - **★★ THE HELD CASE HAD TO BECOME AN OUTCOME TOO, OR THE FIX WOULD HAVE MADE THINGS WORSE.** The gate refusing is CORRECT for a register that doesn't foot — but without a word, a client who dropped payroll on Home would see the file accepted and then **nothing, forever**, which is precisely what "handled" must not mean. The sentence reads `gate.reasons`, so it cannot describe a hold that did not happen (§9).
  - **★★★ AND SEVEN EXISTING TESTS FAILED, EVERY ONE OF THEM CORRECTLY.** They pin where the payroll pipeline lives, and it moved. **Repointing them was the risky part, and two attempts were wrong in the informative direction:** the *"exactly ONE builder and ONE write call"* guard, naively pointed at `App.jsx`, demanded one `persistMultiLineEntry` in a file that legitimately has eight — **a repointed test that now asserts something else is worse than a failing one** — so it is scoped to the payroll region instead. And `payrollPdf` had to be SPLIT: the pipeline moved, the file PICKER did not, and pointing both at `App.jsx` made the accept-list assertion read a file with no picker in it — **it would have gone green the moment someone deleted the picker entirely.**
  - **★★ ONE TEST WAS REWRITTEN TO NAME ITS PROPERTY RATHER THAN ITS MECHANISM.** `iaCollapse` asserted that bank, payroll and QBO are all STASHED for a client. The property it protects — *a client's file is accounted for and never throws them onto a reviewer screen* — is fully honoured by processing payroll in place; **stashing was one way to achieve it, not the thing itself.** It now also pins that the payroll branch runs BEFORE the seat check, so the client path cannot fall through to a `setView`.

- **★★ C251 — `O111`: TELLING US TWO NAMES ARE ONE SUPPLIER. **Migration `082` APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30 — three checks, three passes:** `ARRAY / NO / '{}'::text[]` · **42 contacts, 0 carrying an alias** (additive as designed — a guessed alias is exactly the silent merge the column exists to prevent, so a non-zero count would have been a FAILURE, not a head start) · GIN index present. **Ledger: through `061`, plus `063`, `064`, `066`, `068`–`082`.****
  - **THE CASE, UNCHANGED SINCE C201 FOUND IT:** the bank line reads `ACH DEBIT - FRANKLIN AVE PROPERTIES LP RENT`, the invoice reads `Franklin Ave Properties`. Rail-stripping removes transport noise and legal suffixes, but the descriptor also carries a **PURPOSE word** — `RENT` — saying what the payment was FOR, not who it was TO. One landlord, two vendors.
  - **★★ THE DESIGN DECISION IS THE REFUSAL TO DO STRING SURGERY.** Stripping trailing RENT / FEES / SERVICES would also eat **"Lone Star Restaurant SUPPLY"** and **"Bluebonnet Linen SERVICE"** — and §11 already recorded that reasoning for the Toast month-name split, where the answer was recognition rather than surgery. **A wrong merge is a ONE-WAY DOOR: it launders one vendor's attested mapping onto another's charges, silently.** So the merge is asserted by a person and applied by a **Map lookup** — no scoring, no fuzzy match, no *"did you mean"*. **A test asserts the module contains no similarity code at all**, because once `O106` (suggesting matches) exists the difference between the two is invisible from outside.
  - **★ THREE REFUSALS THE VALIDATOR MAKES, EACH FOR A DIFFERENT DANGER:** a name with **no letters** (the phantom-vendor door, C209) · the contact's **own** name · **a name another supplier already answers to — and it NAMES them**, because *"Lone Star already goes by that name"* is actionable where *"invalid"* is not.
  - **★★ AND A CONFLICT RESOLVES TO NEITHER.** Two contacts claiming one alias leaves the vendor **split — visible** — rather than merged onto whichever row was inserted first. Same rule as the directory's `directoryConflicts()`: **two claimants is worse than no entry.**
  - **STORED ON `contacts`, NOT A NEW TABLE:** a contact IS the vendor record, an alias is another name for that same row, and a join table would invite the question *"which contact does this point at"* that the column answers by construction. Mirrors `companies.aliases` (O75).
  - **★★ IT REACHES BOTH DECISIONS, PINNED BY TESTS (§9 "name the reader"):** the **vendor list** groups Franklin Ave as one row with the totals joined, and the **invoice/payment matcher** now ATTACHES the case that `O114`'s re-drive left open. *(The pre-registered test in `invoicePayment.test.js` used a descriptor WITHOUT the purpose word, so it passed on the string rules alone — the new test uses the real one, where only the asserted alias closes it.)*

- **★ THE `O98` READ-SIDE SWEEP — ONE REAL INSTANCE, ONE FALSE POSITIVE, AND THE DISCRIMINATOR IS THE USEFUL PART (2026-08-30). NO CODE BEYOND C250.**
  - **57 reads in `src/` destructure `{ data }` and drop `error`** — the read-side twin of the unchecked write. **Fixing all 57 would be churn:** a failed read usually yields an empty list on a screen the user can simply reload, and nothing is claimed about it.
  - **★★ IT MATTERS ONLY WHERE THE EMPTY RESULT ITSELF BECOMES A SENTENCE.** That is the whole of `O98`, stated as a test you can apply: *does anything on screen assert something about the world because this came back empty?* **`TeamView` did — "nobody is on this team" — and it is fixed (C250).** `MonthlyReportsPanel` looked identical to the sweep and **is not**: its empty state is derived from the LEDGER (`invoices`), not from the query whose error is dropped; that query only fetches stored AI narratives, and the figures recompute live either way.
  - **RECORDED AS A FALSE POSITIVE ON PURPOSE.** A sweep that reports only its hits teaches nothing about where to look next; **the discriminator is not "was the error dropped" but "is the empty result load-bearing for a claim"**, and the two cases only separate when you trace what feeds the sentence.

- **★★ C250 — THE EMPTY-CATCH SWEEP, AND THE ONE THAT MATTERED MOST WAS A PAIR OF *READS* (2026-08-30).**
  - 23 empty catches wrap a write or an RPC; **most are `localStorage`, Sentry calls, or the notification markers already excused** (a failed marker leaves the badge *visibly* lit). **Three were real.**
  - **★★★ THE TEAM SCREEN FAILED INTO AN EMPTY LIST.** Both queries — members and pending invites — swallowed their error, so a failed load rendered as **"nobody is on this team"** and **"no invites outstanding"** on **the one screen whose entire job is to tell you who can reach your books.** *"We couldn't ask"* and *"there is nobody"* are different answers and **a silently empty list is the most reassuring possible way to be wrong.** This is `O98` on a security surface. It now says the list may be incomplete **and that it is not a confirmation that nobody else has access.**
  - **★★ NO QUICKBOOKS BATCH RECORD MEANS NO UNDO.** The undo works off the `qbo_imports` row, so an import whose batch insert failed was booked and then **could not be reversed as a group** — inside an empty catch, so the person clicking Import learned nothing. It still proceeds (the entries are correct and wanted); what changed is that **the missing safety net is announced BEFORE it is needed**, not discovered when they reach for it.
  - **★★★ AND A TEST I EXPECTED TO PASS FOUND A SECOND ASSET ROLLBACK I HAD NOT FIXED.** Two byte-identical unchecked deletes existed; I hardened one, and the assertion's `indexOf` reached **the other** and failed against what I believed was correct code. **A test failing on correct-looking code is the cheap version of this**; the expensive version is a second copy nobody ever looks at. Both paths now go through **one** checked helper, and the assertion pins that **exactly one** place removes an asset — so a third copy cannot appear either.
  - **★ MECHANICAL SLIP NUMBER FIVE, SAME FAMILY: my slice-end heuristic cut in the wrong place and left orphaned lines**, caught by the build. Bad anchor · reversed slice · no-op mutation · import inside an import · this. **All five are the same failure to verify that an edit did what it was written to do**, and the build or a test caught every one — which is the argument for making the edit and then LOOKING, not for editing more carefully.

- **★★★ C249 — THE UNCHECKED-WRITE CLASS IS CLOSED AS A CLASS (2026-08-30). `tests/uncheckedWrites.test.js`.**
  - **IN ONE DAY THIS SINGLE FACT — PostgREST reports NO error for an update that matched zero rows — PRODUCED:** a delete function whose ids every *"✓ Deleted"* message was built from · **both** Undo buttons · a payment compensation · a recode · the opening-balance supersede · the tax page and settings saves · **and a fix written that same morning carrying the gap it was fixing.** `if (error)` **reads as a check and is not one**, which is why the rule cannot be advisory.
  - **FIVE MORE FIXED FIRST, TRIAGED BY WHETHER A SILENT FAILURE CHANGES A NUMBER OR REPEATS WORK:** the **cutoff date** (§12 load-bearing — no transaction may be dated before it, so a failed save leaves the screen showing one date and every guard enforcing another) · the **bank balance adopted from a statement** · the **business profile** (`fiscal_year_end` drives the retained-earnings split, so a lost write is a wrong FIGURE later, not a missing preference) · **onboarding-complete** (a silent failure greets them again on every load, forever, with nothing they can do) · **`dismissAnomaly`** (`080`'s policy filters a non-reviewer's dismissal out with `USING`, yielding **no error AND no rows** — so the card would have said "dismissed", vanished, and returned on the next load).
  - **★★ AND THE GUARD ALLOWS EXCEPTIONS ONLY WITH A WRITTEN REASON, WHICH IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** Nine tables are excused and **each names why nobody needs to know it failed** — a failed notification marker leaves the badge *visibly* lit; a failed anomaly transition leaves the note *visibly* open; a lost learning signal is re-taught by the next correction. **A blanket allow-list would be the same as no rule.**
  - **★ A THIRD CHECK REFUSES AN UNUSED EXCUSE.** An excuse for a table nobody writes unchecked any more is **a licence sitting open for the next person who adds one** — the same reasoning that removed the five dead context destructures. Mutation-verified in all three directions: a new unchecked ledger write · a `.select()` removed · an excuse gone stale.

- **★★★ C248 — THE GATE WAS CORRECT AND ITS INPUT WAS NOT (2026-08-30). FIVE LEDGER WRITES.**
  - **`softDeleteJournalEntry` RETURNS THE IDS IT DELETED, AND EVERY CALLER GATES "✓ DELETED" ON THAT LIST** — `O124(c)` checked `allIds.length`, `C240` built its partial-refusal report from the same list. **Both were reasoning about ids that could be phantoms**: `mark()` pushed an id whenever there was no error, and PostgREST reports no error for an update matching zero rows. An id that no longer resolves, or a row RLS filters out — **the `C191` id-seam class** — came back as a successful delete.
  - **★★ SO TWO HARDENINGS I VALIDATED, ONE OF THEM THIS MORNING, WERE BUILT ON AN UNCHECKED WRITE ONE LEVEL DOWN.** Same shape as the reversal guard that was only correct by virtue of something upstream: **hardening a gate says nothing about the truthfulness of what it is handed.** The defect was never where either fix was looking.
  - **FOUR MORE, TRIAGED BY STAKES rather than swept blind** (28 unchecked row-targeted writes exist; the other 23 are notification read-markers and similar): **`persistRecode`** — a recode is the operation where *"it didn't throw"* and *"the money moved"* are most easily confused, and it matched on `journal_entry_id` + a debit/credit filter, so `.select("id")` rather than the helper · **the opening-balance supersede** — *"a failure here leaves TWO opening entries"*, the one write in the product whose silent failure **doubles the balance sheet** · **`persistAccountEdit`** — a rename or renumber that saves nothing · **`compensateCapitalization`** — the twin of `C245`'s payment compensation, keeping the books from holding a capitalized asset with no depreciation schedule.
  - **★ AND A MUTATION ESCAPED FOR THE THIRD TIME TODAY ON THE SAME BLIND SPOT: `if (false) { logAudit(…) }` satisfied a test matching the audit NAME.** A source scan cannot see reachability, so the assertion must pin the CONSUMPTION — `if (!r.ok) {` immediately preceding. Three occurrences in one day (contracts Undo · the refused-rows restore · this) is not carelessness, it is a property of source-scanning tests, and the rule is now explicit: **match the branch, never the identifier.**

- **★★ C247 — `O76` HAD NO DETAIL, SO THE CLASS WAS FOUND RATHER THAN LOOKED UP (2026-08-30).** The item read *"screens that don't refresh after a change — fix as a class, not screen by screen"*, one line, no examples. **The class, from the WRITING end: a view writes directly to the database, does not check the write landed, then paints the new value from local state.** The screen is right, the database never agreed, and the *"it didn't refresh"* complaint arrives on the NEXT load when the value silently reverts.
  - **FOUR LIVE INSTANCES, and one is a person's own typing.** **`SettingsView`** reported settings saved while checking only `error` — and then wrote the new name into the header optimistically, so a failed save looked done. **`TaxView`** failed to a `console.warn` and **nothing else**: estimated payments made and which deadlines are filed are **figures a human types**, so a silent failure means they enter their tax position, see no complaint, and find it gone. **A silent failure of the user's own typing is the worst kind — no reason to suspect it, no way to notice.**
  - **★ TWO WERE GENUINELY BEST-EFFORT AND ARE NOW EXPLICITLY SO.** The `aliases` write tolerates a missing column pre-`046`; QBO's batch counters must not fail a completed import. **But "we tolerate this failing" had become "we cannot tell whether it ever saved", which is a different thing** — both now report without blocking. And the counters are not cosmetic: **`imported_count` is what the UNDO reports**, so a lost counter makes the undo say it removed nothing — `C235`'s bug seen from the writing end.
  - **★★★ AND THE FOURTH WAS MY OWN C235 FIX FROM THIS MORNING, CARRYING THE GAP IT WAS FIXING.** C235 hardened the QBO undo to stop claiming success without checking — **and then checked only `error`**, which PostgREST does not set for an update matching zero rows. A batch that failed to be marked undone would still have said *"Import undone ✓"* — **the exact sentence C235 deleted one line above.** Found by a guard written afterwards, not by re-reading it. **The lesson is not "be careful": it is that `if (error)` reads as a check and is not one, which is why the guard has to be mechanical.**

- **★★ `O131` — A SOLO SIGNUP CAN NEVER SIGN OFF, AND THE OLD NOTE SAID THE OPPOSITE (2026-08-30). PRODUCT DECISION, NOT A DEFECT; NO FIX PROPOSED.**
  - **ESTABLISHED IN CODE AND CONFIRMED LIVE:** `create_company` gives the creator `'owner'` · `canAttestPeriod` = `admin || accountant`, with a comment saying the owner may NOT self-attest (the O83 separation fix) · `is_company_reviewer` matches it exactly · `company_users` is UNIQUE on `(company_id, user_id)`, **so one person holds one role and an owner cannot also be an accountant** · `081` closed the last accidental route (self-promotion through the API). **Probe (1) this morning watched an owner be refused on `period_signoffs`.**
  - **★★ SO THE CONSEQUENCE IS: A SOLO CLIENT'S TRUST PANEL SAYS "AWAITING YOUR ACCOUNTANT'S SIGN-OFF" PERMANENTLY, AND NOTHING EXPLAINS WHY.** Correct for the CPA-led model this product is built around; a dead end for anyone who arrives alone.

It shipped in `053`. **The note has been describing a hole that was closed, while the real consequence — the solo dead end — went unrecorded.** *(Tenth stale item this week, and the first whose staleness pointed at a live product gap rather than at finished work.)*
  - **DISCLOSURE `D2` REVERSED, NOT CLOSED.** It said *"you would be signing off your own books"*. The truth is the opposite and it is the more awkward sentence: *"you cannot sign off your own books at all."*

- **★★★ C246 — `O14` CLOSED: SCREENS ARE TESTED NOW, WITH NO NEW DEPENDENCY (2026-08-30). `tests/viewsRender.test.jsx`.**
  - **THE ITEM WAS *"a screen can crash with a green suite"*, AND IT WAS EXACTLY TRUE.** Every test here exercised LOGIC; nothing had ever rendered a view. **Three commits in this session alone renamed or moved context values that views consume** — any of them could have shipped a screen that throws on open against 2,400 passing tests.
  - **★ `renderToString` SHIPS WITH REACT AND NEEDS NO DOM.** No jsdom, no testing-library, no config — the project has deliberately stayed at six dependencies, and the render pass is exactly where a crash-on-open happens. **All 33 views + 3 top-level components render.**
  - **★★ AND THE FIRST HARNESS PRODUCED SEVEN FALSE FAILURES THAT LOOKED LIKE REAL CRASHES.** The verb regex matched `filteredInvoices` ("filter…"), `openingBalances` ("open…") and `signoffs` ("sign…"), handing views a function where they expected an array. **A camelCase verb prefix is only a verb when a capital follows it** — `(?=[A-Z])` is load-bearing and pinned by its own test.
  - **★★★ BUT A MUTATION ESCAPED IT, AND THAT ESCAPE IS THE MORE USEFUL HALF: RENAMING A CONTEXT KEY STILL RENDERED.** The fixture returns an empty array for anything it does not know, so the screen paints — **empty and wrong**. That is the harness being permissive on purpose (a strict fixture would be a second copy of a 394-key state shape, stale in a week), and it means plainly: **THE RENDER SWEEP CATCHES A SCREEN THAT CRASHES, NOT ONE THAT SILENTLY RENDERS EMPTY.**
  - **SO A SECOND CHECK ASSERTS THE CONTRACT, WITH NO FIXTURE AT ALL:** every name a component destructures from `useERP()` must actually be in `erpCtx`. **It immediately found 5 real orphans** — `getOpenAP`, `getOpenAR`, `getUnpaidInvoices`, `getUnpaidReceivables`, `setCustomCOA`, **destructured by 21 views each and existing NOWHERE in `src/`.** Dead copy-paste, harmless today — **and precisely the noise that would hide a real one.** Removed, so the check now has zero exceptions.
  - **★ AND MY PARSER'S FIRST VERSION REPORTED 173 "MISSING KEYS", FIVE OF THEM REAL.** `[\s\S]*?` matched from the first `const {` in `ClarificationFlow.jsx` to a `useERP()` hundreds of lines later, so every local variable, style property and comment word in between became a finding. **A finding list that is 97% noise is worse than no check** — it trains you to skim. Tightened to `[^{}();]*?`: a context destructure is a plain identifier list.

- **★★ C245 — `O17`'s SAFETY NET WAS ITSELF AN UNCHECKED WRITE (2026-08-30).** `markBillPaid` posts the GL movement and THEN flips the paid flag; a compensating reversal removes the movement if the second step fails, **and that reversal is the entire safety property of the two-step design.** It was a bare `.update()` inside a `console.warn` catch — and PostgREST reports no error for an update that matched nothing (C192), **so "it didn't throw" was never evidence it worked.** A silent failure left the books showing cash leaving for a bill still marked unpaid, traced only by a console line nobody reads.
  - **★★★ AND THE COPY MATTERED AS MUCH AS THE WRITE: the generic *"please try again"* invited a SECOND payment movement on top of the orphaned one — the `O123` shape, in money instead of reversals.** A failure that cannot be retried must never be described as one. It now says what is actually true of the books — *"we recorded the money leaving but couldn't finish marking it paid, and couldn't undo it either. **Don't try again** — send this to your accountant"* — and is audited **and** sent to Sentry, because a money-state inconsistency is not a UI event.
  - **▶ THE TWO STEPS REMAIN TWO STEPS.** Making them one is an RPC change (`O95`'s neighbour) and stays open; this removes the way the existing design could fail invisibly.

- **★★ C244 — `O99` CLOSED: NINETEEN HAND-ROLLED AI PARSES, NOW ONE (2026-08-30). And the sweep found two defects the item did not describe.**
  - **`JSON.parse((d.content?.find(b=>b.type==="text")?.text||"{}").replace(/```json|```/g,"").trim())` appeared VERBATIM** across upload, payroll, contracts, QBO, onboarding, screening and reconcile. It breaks the moment the model adds a sentence after the JSON — **which is what killed the payroll parse mid-drive (C188)** — and C188 fixed exactly one copy and listed the rest for "deliberate migration". **`src/` now holds ZERO raw parses of a model reply**, pinned by a test that walks the tree.
  - **★★ THE MIGRATION PRESERVES BOTH OLD BEHAVIOURS EXACTLY, WHICH IS THE WHOLE DESIGN:** *no text at all* → the caller's fallback, as `||"{}"` did (**an absent reply is not a parse failure**, and turning it into one would convert benign no-ops into user-visible errors in a dozen flows at once) · *text that will not parse* → **THROWS**, as `JSON.parse` did, so every existing try/catch still runs (**returning the fallback here would be the silent-failure trade this codebase spends most of its time undoing** — garbage would read as *"nothing found"*). **The only change is that trailing prose parses.** All three pinned, all three mutation-killed.
  - **★★ DEFECT 1, NOT IN THE ITEM — THE CLARIFICATION FLOW'S BRACE REGEX WAS GREEDY.** `/\{[\s\S]*\}/` spans from the FIRST `{` to the **LAST** `}`, so a reply containing two objects produced a string that is not JSON at all — **the account lookup failed on precisely the replies that contained an answer.**
  - **★★ DEFECT 2 — A "RECOVERY" CATCH THAT RE-RAN THE IDENTICAL CALL.** `try { JSON.parse(raw) } catch { try { JSON.parse(raw) } catch { [] } }` — the second attempt is the same call on the same string, so it **could never recover anything the first missed**. It was two ways of writing `catch { [] }` while reading like a fallback strategy. *(Sibling of C195(7): code that cannot do anything, indistinguishable from code with nothing to do.)*
  - **★ AND A SELF-INFLICTED BREAK WORTH RECORDING: my "insert after the last line starting with `import`" heuristic put an import INSIDE a multi-line import** in `ai.js`, producing invalid JS. **Caught by the build, not by a test** — and the check that found the rest was a two-line script asserting no import line follows one ending in `{`. Fourth mechanical slip today (bad anchor · reversed slice · no-op mutation · this): all four are the same failure to verify that an edit did what it was written to do.

- **★★★ C243 — `O95` IS NOW GUARDED AS A CLASS, NOT CHASED AS INSTANCES (2026-08-30). `tests/importMetadataContract.test.js`.**
  - **SIX DEFECTS, ONE FACT.** `post_journal_entry` discards every `p_meta` key outside its six named scalars, and that has produced: **the payroll gate that could never fire** (a whole release) · **payroll bank-line matching, inert** · **depreciation's idempotency marker, inert** · **the reversal marker, inert AND FAILING OPEN — one bill reversed three times** · **the sales-tax control total, INVERTED** · **"the original date is kept on file", kept nowhere and displayed nowhere.**
  - **★★ EVERY ONE WAS FOUND BY A PERSON READING CODE, MONTHS APART. NONE BY A TEST** — because a test that hands the reader its own fixture proves the reader works GIVEN the data and says nothing about whether the data arrives (`·3a`). So the guard asserts the **CONTRACT**: every key read off `import_metadata` must be written by something, the reader roster is pinned by exact equality, nothing may be added to `p_meta` outside the six the RPC keeps, and every metadata write goes through a **checked** update.
  - **▶ WHAT IT CANNOT DO, STATED: it cannot prove a write LANDS at runtime.** Only a live drive does that. It proves nobody has added a SEVENTH reader with no writer — **which is how all six above began.**
  - **★★ AND THE FIRST MUTATION AGAINST IT ESCAPED BECAUSE THE MUTATION NEVER APPLIED.** My `sed` targeted `export function isLiveEntry`; the source says `export const`. The replace was a no-op, the suite stayed green, and **that is indistinguishable from a guard doing its job.** Re-run properly it kills two checks. **A mutation must be confirmed to have CHANGED THE FILE before its survival means anything** — the same shape as a slice whose end anchor precedes its start, hit three times today.

- **★★★ C241 — `O95`'s SALES-TAX INSTANCE IS NOT INERT. IT IS INVERTED (2026-08-30).**
  - **THE MECHANISM IS THE FAMILIAR ONE:** `persistJournalEntry` puts `tax_amount` in `p_meta`, `post_journal_entry` cherry-picks six named scalars and discards the rest, so **`import_metadata.tax_amount` has never once reached the database.** The comment where it is built — *"persisted INDEPENDENT of how it was booked"* — has been describing an intention since the RPC existed.
  - **★★★ BUT THE CONSEQUENCE IS WORSE THAN THE PAYROLL AND DEPRECIATION INSTANCES, WHICH BOTH FAILED CLOSED. `sales_tax_tie` compares *tax the invoices charged* against the Sales-Tax-Payable balance, and with the charged figure stuck at 0 THE NET RUNS BACKWARDS:**
    - **tax booked CORRECTLY** → the liability is credited, charged reads 0 → **MISMATCH → an accuracy flag that BLOCKS SIGN-OFF on books that are right.**
    - **tax MIS-BOOKED INTO REVENUE — the Riverside case the whole net was built for** → liability 0, charged 0 → **TIES, and the one failure it exists to catch passes silently.**
    - **Both proved by running `computeControlTotals` on the two fixtures**, not by reading it. **A guard that fires on correct work and stays quiet on the failure it was written for is worse than an absent guard: the absent one does not train you to ignore it.**
  - **★★ AND WHY A GREEN SUITE NEVER SAID SO — `·3a`'s LESSON, IN THIS CHECK'S OWN TEST FILE.** `tests/controlTotals.test.js` hands its fixtures `import_metadata: { tax_amount: 8 }` **directly**. Reader and fixture therefore agree with each other while neither agrees with the database — **the identical shape that shipped the payroll gate unable to fire for a release.** A test that supplies the field production never writes proves the check works GIVEN the data and says **nothing** about whether the data arrives. **The new suite asserts the WRITE SIDE.**
  - **FIXED with the follow-up `checkedRowUpdate`** ·3c used for payroll and depreciation — **migration-free**, because changing the RPC's contract is its own decision and remains `O95`'s open half.
  - **★★ AND THE SECOND `O95` VICTIM ON THE SAME FUNCTION IS A BROKEN PROMISE TO THE USER, NOT AN INERT FEATURE.** An entry dated into a signed month offers three choices; one is *"record it in the current month instead"*, and the confirmation says **"the original date is kept on file"**. `rebookedIntoOpenMonth` duly builds `original_date` — **onto the invoice object, which `persistJournalEntry` never forwarded into `p_meta` at all, and which the RPC would have discarded had it done so.** The date was kept nowhere and the sentence was a claim about a write that did not happen.
  - **★★★ AND IT HAD NO READER EITHER — `grep` for `original_date` outside the writer returns NOTHING.** So fixing only the write would have produced a field that is stored, correct, and invisible. **Both halves were missing at once**, which is the rare case where "name the reader" and "the write does not land" are the same bug. The panel now renders *"Originally dated 15 Jan — moved here because that month was already signed off"*.
  - **★ `tax_amount` AND `original_date` ARE NOW GONE FROM `p_meta` ENTIRELY, and a test forbids their return.** Leaving them there "for clarity" is exactly how this looked correct for as long as the RPC has existed: **a key in `p_meta` is written nowhere, and the comment describing it becomes a description of an intention.**
  - **★ THE FAILED-STAMP PATH FAILS LOUD, DELIBERATELY.** The entry is correct either way, but a missing stamp makes the control total read 0 against a real liability and **block sign-off with a mismatch nobody can explain** — so it is audited AND said on screen, in terms of what the accountant will see: *"we couldn't record this invoice's sales tax for the monthly cross-check. Your accountant may see a sales-tax mismatch that isn't real."*

- **★★★ `081` — THE MEMBERSHIP TABLE HANDED OUT ADMIN. FOUND BY CHECKING WHETHER A LEDGER ITEM WAS STALE (2026-08-30). ▶ MIGRATION WRITTEN; PROBE FIRST.**
  - **THE ROUTE IN IS THE POINT: I WAS CHECKING `O93`** (*"the client-owner must not sign off their own books"*), **which IS stale** — it describes gating via `is_company_admin`, but `053` replaced that with `is_company_reviewer` (admin or accountant, **owner excluded**), and probe (1) confirmed live that **an owner IS refused on `period_signoffs`**. **Asking how that could be worked around is what surfaced this.**
  - **`001` GAVE `company_users` THE FOUR STANDARD POLICIES, EACH CARRYING `user_id = auth.uid()` BESIDE THE ADMIN TEST**, intended for accepting an invite. Unscoped, it says considerably more:
    1. **UPDATE — SELF-PROMOTION.** `role` is on the row a member may update. **A `viewer` can make themselves `admin`** — the entire role vocabulary C225 repaired, undone in one call. **And an OWNER can make themselves `admin`, hence a REVIEWER, hence able to sign off their own books** — reaching `O93`'s hole through legitimate admin power rather than through a missing gate.
    2. **★★ INSERT — JOINING A COMPANY YOU WERE NEVER INVITED TO.** `with check (user_id = auth.uid() or …)` **does not constrain `company_id`.** An authenticated user may insert `(company_id = <any>, user_id = self, role = 'admin', accepted_at = now())`, and **`is_company_member` asks for exactly that row** — so every tenant table opens. The only obstacle is knowing a uuid, which is **not a secret and not a boundary**.
  - **★★ WHY THE TIER 1 AUDIT DID NOT CATCH IT, AND THIS IS THE DURABLE LESSON. It asked *"is RLS enabled, and does a policy exist, for every table carrying `company_id`?"* and answered 33 of 33, correctly. IT NEVER ASKED WHAT THE POLICIES PERMIT.** A table can be fully covered and still hand out admin. **Coverage is not a property of the wall; it is a property of the checklist.** *(And `052` sits one inch away — it fixes this exact shape on `companies` and `users`, and states in its own header: "Does NOT touch … the company_users policies.")*
  - **NEITHER CLAUSE IS USED BY ANYTHING.** Every membership write goes through a SECURITY DEFINER function (`create_company`, `accept_invite`) that bypasses RLS; **`src/` never writes the table at all** — pinned by a test that walks the tree rather than by a claim.
  - **★ THE FIX IS A POLICY *AND* A TRIGGER, AND THE SPLIT IS FORCED: RLS `WITH CHECK` CANNOT SEE THE OLD ROW.** A policy can express *"the new role is admin"*; only a trigger can express *"the role CHANGED"*. Refusing self-UPDATE outright would be the `079` mistake — blocking more than we mean, surfacing when someone tries to do their job.
  - **★★ AND IT REFUSES CHANGING YOUR OWN ROLE EVEN AS AN OWNER OR ADMIN. That is `O93` itself, not belt-and-braces:** you may manage other people's roles, not your own. An admin needing their own role changed asks another admin, which is the correct amount of friction for that operation.
  - **★★★ PROBE (B) RAN LIVE 2026-08-30 AND RETURNED `FAIL - joined another company as admin and can now read 224 of its journal entries [ran as: authenticated]`. THE TENANT-ISOLATION HOLE IS DEMONSTRATED, NOT INFERRED** — one company's member reached another company's books, and the damage is stated as a COUNT rather than as a mechanism.
  - **★★ AND PROBE (A) IS A WEAKER RESULT THAN IT LOOKS — SAY SO. It returned `FAIL - a admin promoted THEMSELVES to admin`: THE SPECIMEN IT BORROWED WAS ALREADY AN ADMIN**, so the role did not change. That proves the role column is **self-writable**; it does **not** demonstrate **ESCALATION**, which is one inference further than the observation. *"The update was permitted"* and *"a viewer can become an admin"* are different claims and only the second is the finding. **(A2) added, requiring the borrowed member to be BELOW admin** so a success is a real promotion — and falling back to the OWNER case, where promotion to `admin` confers REVIEWER and is `O93` exactly. **Recorded rather than quietly re-run: this is the same over-claim shape as the `O113` retraction and the "reviewer half is unprovable" slip, both caught the same week.**
  - **★★★ (A2) RAN LIVE AND CLOSED THE GAP (A) LEFT: `FAIL - a owner promoted THEMSELVES to admin (and is now a reviewer, so can sign off their own books) [ran as: authenticated]`.** So the escalation is **observed, not inferred** — and the specimen is the OWNER case, which means **`O93`'s hole is reachable through the MEMBERSHIP table even though the SIGN-OFF gate itself is sound.** Probe (1) this morning proved the database refuses an owner on `period_signoffs`; **this proves the owner can simply stop being an owner.** *(Worth keeping as a shape: a gate can be correct and irrelevant if the ROLE it reads is writable by the person it is gating.)*
  - **★★★ `081` APPLIED & VERIFIED LIVE 2026-08-30. BOTH HOLES CLOSED, WATCHED TO REFUSE.** (A2) → *"PASS - refused: **You cannot change your own role. Ask another admin on this company to do it.**"* — the trigger's own sentence, which is what a person would actually get. (B) → *"PASS - refused: new row violates row-level security policy for table `company_users`"*. **Ledger: through `061`, plus `063`, `064`, `066`, `068`–`081`.**
  - **★★ AND (C) — THE "DID IT BLOCK TOO MUCH" CHECK — CAME BACK INCONCLUSIVE, WHICH IS NOT A PASS.** It required a company already holding an admin AND a second member; **no company has two.** So the `079` direction is **unproven**, and (A2)+(B) passing is still formally consistent with *"we broke role management entirely"*. **RE-RUN AND PASSED: *"PASS - an admin can still change someone else's role [ran as: authenticated]"* — so the guard refuses exactly the two things it names and nothing more. `081` IS FULLY VERIFIED: THREE CHECKS, THREE PASSES.** Rewritten to **create the second member itself** inside the transaction it rolls back — the seed row goes in as the superuser before the role switch, and the trigger under test fires on the UPDATE regardless. **The dependency on the data happening to be shaped right was the defect in the check, not a fact about the fix.**
  - **▶ THE PROBE RUNS FIRST AND IS EXPECTED TO SAY *FAIL*.** `supabase/verify/081_self_escalation.sql` demonstrates both holes in a rolled-back transaction before the migration is applied — **this whole finding is currently READ FROM POLICY TEXT, and this project's standard is that a claim about the database is a claim about a query somebody ran.** (B) reports **the consequence, counted** — *"can now read N of its journal entries"* — rather than the mechanism. (C) checks the `079` direction after.

- **★★ C240 — THE AUDIT TRAIL WAS RECORDING AN INTENTION AS AN EVENT (2026-08-30). Found by CHECKING TWO LEDGER ITEMS FOR STALENESS, not by looking for a bug.**
  - **BOTH ITEMS WERE INDEED STALE** — *"decide how we identify a vendor for each kind of record"* was settled and shipped in C201 (`identityForEntry`, per-source strategy), and `O124`'s *"'Deleted' still appears even when the delete didn't happen"* was fixed yesterday. **That is now FIVE stale items this week** (O96, the reconcile prefill, the recurring-charge false positives, and these two): the ledger's failure mode is not losing items, it is holding ones that quietly became true.
  - **★★ BUT READING THE CODE TO CONFIRM IT FOUND TWO DEFECTS THE ITEM DID NOT DESCRIBE.** *(1)* **`softDeleteInvoices` wrote its `invoice_deleted` audit rows BEFORE attempting the write**, so a refused delete left *"Deleted: Roma $551.20"* permanently in the audit trail **beside the `invoice_delete_failed` row that contradicts it. The audit trail is the one record an accountant is entitled to trust, and it was being told what we INTENDED.** *(2)* **`allIds.length` is a BATCH-level test**, so five entries with two refused reported *"Deleted 5 entries ✓"* while those two sat removed from the screen and present in the books.
  - **★ THE PARTIAL CASE WAS REACHABLE ONLY FROM THE AI CHAT PATH — the caller with nobody checking its work.** `BooksView` pre-splits signed months through `planEntryRemoval`; `App.jsx:6943` hands over a mixed batch unfiltered. **Yesterday's bulk-delete UI made the batch case ordinary**, which is what moved this from latent to live.
  - **★★ AND THE SAME MUTATION ESCAPED A TEST OF MINE TWICE IN ONE DAY.** Asserting `setInvoices(prev =>` APPEARS survived wrapping it in `if (false)` — identical to the contracts-Undo escape this morning. **A source scan cannot see reachability.** Fixed by pinning what the block BEGINS with, which also kills a reorder. **The general form: assert STRUCTURE or CONSUMPTION, never presence.**

- **★★ C293 — THE GATE THAT MUST NEVER SHOW A FALSE GREEN, SWEPT (2026-08-31).** `C194`: *"Your books match your bank ✓"* rendered UNGATED — a failed completion insert was `console.warn`'d and the success screen appeared anyway, reproduced live with NO row existing. §11 calls that the worst class of bug in this product.
  - **★★★ THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION IS NOT "IT REFUSED A GOOD MONTH" — that is loud and someone complains — IT IS "IT ACCEPTED A MONTH A CENT OUT", which nobody ever sees.** So the sweep asserts that over ~320 books/outstanding/unmatched combinations, a genuinely tying month completes and **a month one cent out in EITHER direction is refused**. ✅ **It holds.** Five mutations, five reds: the tolerance widened to a cent · an unverified balance no longer blocking · zero passing without confirmation · **zero-rows treated as success (the C194 bug itself)** · the completion reading React state instead of the synchronous ref (the seam that once inserted a SECOND row).
  - **★ AND THE ONE FAILURE WAS MY FIXTURE AGAIN, FOR THE SAME REASON AS THE DEPRECIATION SWEEP: I used a books balance of `33333.335`.** A GL balance is a **sum of two-decimal amounts**, so a third decimal cannot occur — the fixture invented an impossible input and then blamed the gate. Recorded because it is now the second time the same wrong instinct produced a false alarm: **money has two decimals, and a sweep that forgets it tests arithmetic nothing will ever be handed.**

- **★★★ C292 — THE RE-UPLOAD DEDUP HOLDS, AND MY FIRST TWO FIXTURES COULD NOT HAVE PROVED IT (2026-08-31).** `O83` is the worst production incident this project has had: re-uploading one statement double-booked **14 of 20 lines**, because the dedup key mixed the CLEANED vendor on the booked side with the RAW memo on the re-parse side — two strings that never collide. The key is now date + amount + direction only. **Swept: book a statement, re-parse the SAME statement, every line must come back marked.** ✅ **It holds on every shape** — ordinary, three identical same-day lines, opposing directions, awkward cents, a month boundary — and correctly does NOT mark a genuinely new line, a booking on another account, or one whose entry was voided.
  - **★★★ AND THE MUTATION THAT MATTERED: PUTTING THE DESCRIPTION BACK IN THE KEY — THE O83 BUG ITSELF — LEFT EVERY TEST GREEN.** My fixture passed the **same** description to both sides, so there was no asymmetry to expose. **A fixture that agrees with itself proves nothing** (the `·3a` shape, third time this project has recorded it). Fixed by reproducing what production actually does — the booked row carries a cleaned vendor, a rewritten memo and a re-categorised GL — and then **restoring the bug faithfully in the CALLER**, which fails 9 tests with the incident's own words: *"3 of 3 lines would be booked AGAIN"*.
  - **★★ IT ALSO CAUGHT A VACUOUS ASSERTION I HAD WRITTEN TWO COMMITS EARLIER.** `gaapSweep`'s bank-line case passed `buildBankLineEntry`'s output straight to a balance check that SKIPS anything without a `lines` array — and that function returns the flattened ROW, not a `{lines}` entry. **So the bank-line half of that sweep asserted nothing.** Now expanded exactly as the write path posts it, with a count assertion so it cannot silently skip again.
  - **★ MY FIRST MUTATION WAS ALSO WRONG, AND SAYING SO IS THE DISCIPLINE:** I added a description to `bankTxnKey`, which receives only three fields — so it was empty on both sides and changed nothing. **A mutation that cannot express the bug is not evidence the bug is guarded against.**

- **★★★ C291 — THE LARGE-CHARGE DETECTOR WAS FLAGGING THE RENT, EVERY MONTH (2026-08-30).** The detectors had only ever been tested on fixtures built to TRIP them. That proves each one CAN fire and says **nothing** about whether they fire on ordinary bookkeeping — which is the failure that costs, because a client asked the same pointless question every month stops reading the questions.
  - **★★★ THE FIND: monthly rent raised `large_transaction` EVERY MONTH** — *"If it's equipment or software lasting over a year, it may need to be capitalized"*, asked of the most predictable expense a business has. **`O122`'s rule verbatim: a card the user sees every month is a bug wearing a question mark.**
  - **★★ FIXED BY A PROPERTY, NOT A THIRD SPECIAL CASE.** `isSystemPostedEntry` was added to this same detector because PAYROLL tripped it; rent is not payroll, so it was not covered. The real property in both cases is that **a vendor with an established history at this magnitude is not a surprise** — and `vendor_spike` already owns *"this vendor charged unusually much"*, so this detector is for a large charge we have no history for. **Two priors within 25% is the bar: one is a coincidence, two is a pattern.**
  - **★★ A SURVIVING MUTATION SHOWED MY TESTS COULD NOT TELL `< 2` FROM `< 1`** — the spike fixture's amounts were nowhere near its priors, so the count was 0 either way. **An unpinned judgement drifts**, so the bar is now asserted directly (0 priors → fires · 1 → fires · 2 → silent). Pinning it needed a second correction: counting the TYPE total failed on its own noise, because the earlier priors are themselves large and raise their own cards — it counts the SUBJECT's card now.
  - **✅ A HEALTHY 8-MONTH LEDGER NOW RAISES ZERO CARDS**, including a flat weekly vendor (`O117`), twice-monthly payroll at a round number, and identical monthly rent — and the detectors still fire on a real duplicate, a real spike and a genuinely stopped recurring charge.
  - **★ TWO SCOPE LIMITS RECORDED RATHER THAN CLAIMED AS DEFECTS:** `missing_recurring` keys on 25–40 day gaps and says so in its own copy, so a WEEKLY vendor that stops is not caught; and it needs TWO charges inside a 95-day window, so a vendor that stopped four months ago raises nothing. Both are defensible; my fixture missed the window twice before I read the code properly.

- **★★★ C290 — THE DATE SWEEP, AND THE MOST USEFUL FINDING WAS ABOUT THE SWEEP ITSELF (2026-08-30).** Dates are the axis this codebase has been bitten on most — the helpers carry the scars in their comments (never `toISOString()` for a calendar date; clamp Jan 31 + 1 month to Feb 28 rather than overflowing to Mar 3). Those lessons are applied by careful reasoning at a handful of call sites; this checks them **at every value**: ~340 dates across leap and non-leap years × 0–36 months, day round-trips, every month's first and last day against a signed period, 12 fiscal year-ends.
  - **✅ THE HELPERS HOLD. No defect found** — which is a result, not a blank: the reasoning in those comments is correct at every boundary, including month-end stickiness (`Jan 31 + 2` is `Mar 31`, not `Feb 28` carried forward — a cumulative clamp would drift every lease and asset onto the 28th forever).
  - **★★★ AND THEN THE MUTATION THAT SURVIVED: switching `ymdLocal` to `toISOString().slice(0,10)` — THE EXACT MISTAKE ITS COMMENT WARNS AGAINST — left every assertion GREEN.** It fails only from a zone AHEAD of UTC, because a Date built from local components at midnight reads back as the PREVIOUS day in UTC. **CI runs UTC.** So the guard for the bug this file exists to catch was inert exactly where it matters — the "guard whose input is always empty" shape, committed inside a test written to prevent it.
  - **★★ FIXED BY FORCING THE TIMEZONE RATHER THAN HOPING FOR ONE.** `process.env.TZ` does take effect at runtime (verified via `getTimezoneOffset`, and the block asserts the change took, or it proves nothing). The mutation now dies under `TZ=UTC`.
  - **★ AND IT FOUND ONE TIMEZONE-FRAGILE TEST IN THE EXISTING SUITE.** `anomalies.test.js` pinned `new Date("2026-01-31T12:00:00Z")` — noon UTC, which is already **1am on 1 February in Auckland** — while asserting a LOCAL-month outcome. The product is right (`thisMonth` is deliberately local, to match how entry dates are stored); the fixture was pinning a UTC instant against a local expectation. **The full suite now passes from five timezones spanning UTC−9 to UTC+13.**

- **★★ C289 — THE TIE-OUT THAT GATES SIGN-OFF CANNOT FAIL ON THE COMMONEST ENTRY SHAPE (2026-08-30).** Swept `computeControlTotals` in **both directions** — no check may fire on correct books, and each must actually fire on a broken ledger, because a control that never fires is indistinguishable from clean books (`C195(7)`, and `C241` where the sales-tax check ran BACKWARDS for its whole life under a green single-fixture suite).
  - **✅ NO FALSE FIRING:** across four magnitudes, three tax rates and three ledger shapes, every check ties on correct books; an empty company ties rather than reporting a mismatch.
