  - **★★★ THE FINDING: A ONE-SIDED **TWO-LINE** ENTRY IS INVISIBLE TO `trial_balance`.** `flattenJournalEntries` collapses a 2-line entry into ONE row carrying the primary amount and **derives the offset from that same number**, so the discrepancy is discarded before the check can see it. **A 3-line entry IS caught** (verified both ways). So the check billed as *"the fundamental tie-out"* cannot fail on the commonest shape in the ledger.
  - **★ NOT CURRENTLY REACHABLE, AND THAT IS NOT THE SAME AS SAFE:** `post_journal_entry` and `buildJournalEntry` both enforce balance at write time — **but the whole point of a control total is to catch what the write path missed**, and the QuickBooks import inserts rows directly rather than through the RPC.
  - **PINNED RATHER THAN FIXED, with the reason: the repair is in `flattenJournalEntries` or in what the control is fed, both core reporting paths.** That is a deliberate change, not a rider on a sweep — and the test says so, including that it should be deleted if the limit is ever closed.

- **★★★ C288 — SWEEPING THE REPORTING LAYER FOUND A VOID THAT NEVER REACHED THE DATABASE (2026-08-30).** The same method again — take invariants checked on one fixture and vary the ledger instead. Forty generated ledgers, deterministic, each balanced BY CONSTRUCTION, through `flattenJournalEntries` into the derivations a client actually reads.
  - **★★★ THE FIND: `ReconView`'s `voidBook` CHANGED NOTHING BUT THE SCREEN.** It set `status:"voided"` via `setInvoices` in **local state only**, **wrote an audit row saying the entry was voided**, and never touched the database. So the entry returned on the next reload, the reconciliation was computed against books that did not match storage, and **the audit trail recorded a removal that never happened** — worse than a no-op. It now goes through `removeEntry`, the single funnel (`O130`) that decides soft-delete vs dated reversal by whether the month is signed, and says so when the write does not land.
  - **★★★ AND THE SECOND HALF: `flattenJournalEntries` HARDCODED `status: "booked"`.** It carried `deleted_at` (fixed in `C207`) and **never read `e.status`** — so a database-level `status = 'voided'` was invisible to net income, GL balances and the trial balance alike. **The other half of the same omission, and the shape that guard exists for: a filter is only as good as the field reaching it.**
  - **★★ THREE OF THE FOUR FIRST FAILURES WERE MY FIXTURE, AND NAMING WHICH IS THE WHOLE DISCIPLINE.** I called `trialBalance` with accounts where it takes options · I dated entries past the as-of while asserting `prior + current === all-time` · and — the good one — **my entry ids contained an underscore, which is the sentinel meaning "an expanded multi-line row"**, so `glAccountBalance` counted primary-only and every offset leg vanished. A fixture colliding with a structural convention looks exactly like a bug in the code.
  - **★★ ONE REAL DIVERGENCE IS PINNED RATHER THAN FIXED, WITH THE REASON: `computeNetIncome` and `fiscalYearSplit` disagree on an entry carrying P&L accounts on BOTH legs** (0 vs −100). **No builder in `src/lib` produces that shape** — every one pairs a P&L leg with a balance-sheet leg — so it is unreachable today. Changing a core reporting function for a case nothing can create is the wrong risk; **the test states the divergence, so if a builder ever emits that shape it fails first.**

- **★★★ C287 — THE GAAP GUARDRAIL TESTS ONE FIXTURE PER EVENT, WHICH CANNOT FIND A ROUNDING DEFECT (2026-08-30).** `gaapInvariants.test.js` asserts Dr = Cr and the accounting equation over a list of EVENTS — **one instance each, 28 distinct numbers in the whole file.** That is the right shape for *"does this builder use the correct accounts"* and it is **structurally unable to catch a defect that appears at particular VALUES**.
  - **★★★ AND C286 IS THE PROOF, DEMONSTRATED RATHER THAN ARGUED: with the ASC 842 bug restored, `gaapInvariants` PASSES and the sweep FAILS.** That entry had been unbalanced by a cent at certain payment/term/rate combinations for as long as it existed, and `buildJournalEntry` refuses an unbalanced entry — so the lease never posted, silently, under a green guardrail.
  - **`tests/gaapSweep.test.js` covers the axis the guardrail cannot vary:** every builder over 17 awkward amounts (exact thirds, half-cent boundaries, long decimals, very large and very small) and 8 rates. **Not a replacement — the other axis.**
  - **★ IT FOUND A SECOND, SMALLER ONE: `buildDepreciationSchedule` COULD RETURN A SCHEDULE WITH HOLES IN IT.** `buildDepreciationEntry` returns null when the amount rounds to nothing — a sub-dollar asset over a long life — and the null was pushed into the array. **Both consumers walk it directly** (`persistMultiLineEntry(je)` and a `.map(je => je.…)`), so it would have thrown rather than degraded. A zero-amount month is not a schedule row; the correct schedule for such an asset is one real entry in the final month, which is what it now produces.
  - **★★ TWO OF THE THREE FIRST FAILURES WERE MY TEST, NOT THE CODE, AND SAYING WHICH IS THE POINT.** I called two builders with parameter names they do not have, and I asserted a depreciation schedule sums to the RAW cost when some sweep values carry three decimals on purpose — **a third of a cent is not money**, and the schedule correctly works in cents. Comparing to the unrounded input would have been testing my fixture rather than the code.

- **★★★ C286 — `O89` THIRD SLICE FOUND A LEASE THAT SILENTLY NEVER POSTED (2026-08-30).** `handleContractFile` was 188 lines with four awaits and **no planner calls**: after the model extracts the terms, ~130 lines of ASC 842 / ASC 606 logic built the Day 1 entry and every monthly entry inline. `calcASC842` (the present-value maths) was already a tested library function; **turning its schedule into JOURNAL ENTRIES was not — and that is the half that decides what reaches the books.** Now `src/lib/contractEntries.js`; the caller is 62 lines.
  - **★★★ THE BUG, FOUND BY SWEEPING RATHER THAN BY READING: THE LEASE COMMENCEMENT ENTRY DID NOT BALANCE.** `calcASC842` rounded `rouAsset`, `currentPortion` and `nonCurrentPortion` **independently**. Current + non-current equals the liability exactly in float, but two separate roundings can land a cent away from the rounded liability — **$1,500 × 24 months @ 5% gives Dr 34,190.85 against Cr 34,190.84.**
  - **★★★ AND THE FAILURE MODE IS SILENCE, WHICH IS WHY IT SURVIVED: `buildJournalEntry` REFUSES an unbalanced entry**, so that lease's commencement simply never posted. **No wrong number on a report — a missing one.** Fixed at the source: the non-current portion is now the **plug** (`rounded liability − rounded current`), which is both the standard accounting treatment and the only version that ties by construction.
  - **★★ I PREDICTED THE WRONG BUG, AND SAYING SO IS THE POINT.** I expected the FINANCE-LEASE month to break — it debits interest AND principal, independently rounded, against one payment credit. It does not: `calcASC842` computes `principal = payment − interest` exactly, so those two rounding errors mirror. **That one is safe by coincidence rather than by construction** (an exact half-cent tie would still break it), so the sweep now guards it too. **The suspicion was wrong and the method that tested it found a real defect one line away.**
  - **★ THE SWEEP REFUSES TO PASS VACUOUSLY** — `expect(checked).toBeGreaterThan(2000)`, verified by emptying the loop with the guard left intact. *(My first attempt at that mutation changed the guard AND the loop, so it "survived" meaninglessly — a mutation that removes its own detector proves nothing.)*

- **★★★ C285 — `O89` SECOND SLICE, AND IT FOUND A DOUBLE-CLEARING PATH (2026-08-30).** Measuring the big functions by *decision logic still inside them* rather than by line count: `handleBankFile` and `bookBankTransactions` already call planners and are mostly I/O, but **`runMatchingEngine` was 126 lines with 2 awaits, 2 state writes and ZERO planner calls** — nearly pure logic living in a component. The accept/refuse rules are now `src/lib/llmMatchFilter.js`.
  - **★★ EVERY RULE IN IT EXISTS BECAUSE SOMETHING WENT WRONG ONCE, AND NONE COULD BE EXERCISED WHERE THEY LIVED.** Never re-match a line the deterministic pass took · never re-claim an open item · and **refuse a proposal whose counterpart cannot be resolved in the open universe** — O83 February rendered an empty *"MATCHING AGAINST"* panel on a 99%-confidence proposal, asking a person to confirm a match against an entity they could not see. **A proposal nobody can look at is not a proposal.**
  - **★★★ ONE DELIBERATE BEHAVIOUR CHANGE, STATED AS ONE RATHER THAN SMUGGLED IN AS A REFACTOR: THE OLD LOOP NEVER ADDED AN ACCEPTED BANK LINE TO `handledBankIds`.** It was seeded from the deterministic pass and never updated, so **two model proposals naming the SAME bank line with different open items were BOTH accepted** — and `autoCleared` feeds `planBankImport`, so that clears the same money twice. Splitting one line across proposals is not how partials are modelled here (`amount_remaining` lives INSIDE a match), so a second proposal for a taken line is a model error. **Now refused and counted**, with a test asserting the guard does not block two proposals on genuinely different lines.
  - **★ DROPS ARE RETURNED, NOT JUST LOGGED.** They were `console.warn` only, so *"the model proposed nothing usable"* and *"the model proposed nothing"* were indistinguishable — the same shape as a guard whose input is always empty.

- **★★ C284 — `O89`: THE HIGHEST-TRAFFIC DECISION IN THE PRODUCT COULD NOT BE TESTED (2026-08-30).** The census argued against a general split — median declaration 14 lines, 225 of 237 unremarkable, *"a normal component with a dozen very large functions in it"* — so the useful move is lifting the DECISION out of the biggest one and leaving the I/O. The invoice-building step inside `processUploadItem` (920 lines) is what **every invoice a client drops** runs through, and it was a closure reading `rc`, `rn`, `Date.now()` and the surrounding scope: **its rules could only be verified by reading them.** Now `src/lib/uploadedInvoice.js`, pure, `id` and `booked_at` injected.
  - **★★★ THREE LOAD-BEARING RULES, NOW PINNED AND ALL THREE MUTATION-KILLED:** a **vendor rule beats the model** and carries 99 so the confidence gate passes it · **`type` is classified from the GL CODE, never from the model's own `type` field** — the same basis `flattenJournalEntries` uses, so an odd AI answer cannot mis-slot the row out of the transactions tab the moment it books · **the fallback is Miscellaneous**, which is exactly the account `autoBookDecision` refuses to auto-book into when the vendor has a name.
  - **★★ AND THE MISCELLANEOUS PAIR IS THE INTERESTING ONE: THE FALLBACK IS CORRECT HERE.** Stopping it is the booking GATE's job, and a test now asserts both halves together — the builder produces `7100`, and `autoBookDecision` refuses it **even at confidence 100**. Separately each looks wrong; together they turn *"we couldn't tell"* into a question rather than a silent wrong bucket.
  - **★ I SUSPECTED A DEFECT HERE AND THERE WASN'T ONE — WORTH RECORDING RATHER THAN QUIETLY DROPPING.** A first search suggested the invoice path never called `autoBookDecision`; the call was simply outside my search window. **The guard is correctly placed** — after the confidence floor, before the materiality flag, and NOT gated on the score, so a learned-vendor boost to 100 cannot defeat the account check. A suspicion checked and withdrawn is a result.
  - **★ THE EXTRACTION WAS VERIFIED BY WHAT IT LEFT BEHIND:** after removing the `const confidence` declaration, a scan for bare identifier uses in the remaining loop found two — **both inside comments.** A free identifier is a runtime crash the build cannot see, and `missingImports` only covers lib exports.

- **★★★ C282 — `O102`: SHADOW MODE IS RUNNABLE AND SCORED, AND IT BOOKS NOTHING (2026-08-30).** The ladder has been built since `C201` and **invoked by nothing** — which is the same state as not existing (`C195(7)`: a mechanism nobody triggers is indistinguishable from one with nothing to do). `runShadowCalibration` runs it over a date range; `shadowReport.js` scores the result against Amendment A §5 into **PROCEED / STOP / AMBIGUOUS**.
  - **★★★ IT RUNS THE PASS TWICE OVER IDENTICAL INPUT, ON PURPOSE.** §4.1(3) makes a verdict that varies across runs an automatic fail — *KNOWN is a persistent STATE, not a recomputation* — and **with a single pass that check could never fire**: one run looks exactly like two agreeing runs. The second is a `dryRun` so the stored rows are not doubled.
  - **★★★ AND IT WILL NOT GRANT ITSELF A HUMAN'S SIGN-OFF.** §4.2's itemised disagreement review and clearing a merge candidate are CPA judgements; **absent means UNRESOLVED, never "fine"**, and unresolved items produce STOP. A report that defaulted those to done would be the vacuous pass this whole programme exists to prevent.
  - **★★ A KEY COVERING TWO NAMES IS FLAGGED, NOT ADJUDICATED.** Usually it is the resolver working (a bank string and an invoice string for one supplier); it is a MERGE only when they are different businesses, which a person decides. **Claiming to detect a merge automatically would be inventing certainty about the one failure that is invisible.**
  - **★ THIN EVIDENCE IS NEITHER A PASS NOR A FAIL — but the order matters.** Under 20 scored lines is AMBIGUOUS, checked AFTER the hard fails, so **a phantom in a thin month still STOPS rather than hiding behind "not enough data"**. Mutation-tested in that exact direction.
  - **★★ AND A SURVIVING MUTATION FOUND A REAL GAP IN MY VARIANCE CHECK.** My test only ever varied the VERDICT, so dropping `proposed_account_id` from the comparison key changed nothing. **A line can read "agree" in both runs while proposing a different account each time** — same label, different answer, and exactly the flapping §4.1(3) forbids. Now pinned.
  - **▶ WHAT REMAINS IS THE OPERATOR'S:** run it over two signed months including August and read the report. `C203` — the switch to booking authority — happens only after it says PROCEED.

- **★★ C281 — WIRING THE SUGGESTER FOUND A FALSE SUCCESS NEXT DOOR (2026-08-30).** `O106`'s suggester had no reader, which is the very defect this file spends pages on — so it now sits on the Vendors page, and confirming writes an alias through **the same `persistContact` + `validateAlias` path the vendor form already uses**: one door for a merge, always with a person behind it. A test asserts the block contains no client and exactly one write call.
  - **★★★ AND `persistContact` RETURNED NOTHING.** My confirmation checked `r.ok === false`, which could never fire — so a link that silently failed to save would have shown *"now counts as ✓"* while the two names stayed split, **and the pair would have vanished from the suggestion list**, making it unrepeatable. Caught by reading the function rather than by a test, and it is the C194 family exactly: a success sentence gated on a click instead of on a record.
  - **★ IT REPORTS NOW (`{ ok, error, row }`), and existing callers ignoring the value are unaffected** — the change only lets a caller that wants to claim success actually check. The dismissal is written **after** the verdict, so a failed link stays on the list.
  - **★ DISMISSALS ARE PER-DEVICE (`localStorage`), STATED AS A TRADE RATHER THAN LEFT AS AN OVERSIGHT:** storing them durably means a column or a schema hack, a re-shown suggestion costs one glance, and nothing financial is held there. **A CONFIRMED merge is durable, because it becomes an alias on the contact.**

- **★★ C280 — `O106`: SUGGESTING THE SAME SUPPLIER UNDER A NEW NAME, AND THE DESIGN IS ENTIRELY IN WHAT IT REFUSES (2026-08-30).** `O111` gave a person a way to say *"these two names are one supplier"*; they still had to NOTICE. This proposes the pairs.
  - **★★★ A SUGGESTER THAT PROPOSES A KNOWN-WRONG MERGE IS WORSE THAN NO SUGGESTER**, because people stop reading it and then accept a bad one out of habit — and a merge is a **one-way door** that launders one vendor's attested mapping onto another's charges, silently. `SYSCO` / `SYSCO FUEL` and `Lone Star` / `Lone Star Restaurant Supply` are pairs `vendorIdentity.test.js` already forbids merging, so **they must not be suggested either**, and both are pinned as negative cases.
  - **★★★ THE DISCRIMINATOR IS *WHAT THE EXTRA WORDS ARE*, NOT HOW SIMILAR THE STRINGS LOOK.** A legal form or a payment-rail word never changes who a business is; **one novel noun says what it sells.** `Franklin Ave Properties` vs `…LP RENT` and `SYSCO` vs `SYSCO FUEL` are about EQUALLY similar as strings — **no similarity score can separate them**, which is why this is a vocabulary question and not a scoring one. The sets are small, explicit, pinned by exact contents, and the words a business sells are asserted ABSENT.
  - **★ AND `LEGAL_FORM_WORDS` IS MOSTLY BELT-AND-BRACES, WHICH THE COMMENT NOW SAYS RATHER THAN LETTING IT LOOK LOAD-BEARING:** `normalizeName` already strips the common forms, so *"Alamo Ice LLC"* and *"Alamo Ice"* are one vendor before this module sees them. **My first test expected a suggestion there** — which would have meant asking a person to confirm something the system had already decided correctly.
  - **★★ A SURVIVING MUTATION EXPOSED A VACUOUS TEST OF MINE.** The word-order case used two names with EQUAL token counts, so an earlier guard rejected the pair and the order check was never reached — an order-blind replacement changed nothing. Fixed to a long side with genuinely more tokens, **and paired with the positive case**, so it cannot pass by rejecting everything.
  - **★ IT SUGGESTS AND NEVER DECIDES:** a test asserts the module contains no client, no write, and no path to an account; and a pair already confirmed OR declined is never asked again — re-proposing something declined is how a queue teaches people to ignore it.

- **★★★ C279 — `O113a` WROTE A FIELD WITH NO READER, AND IT WAS THE ANSWER TO `O113b` (2026-08-30).** The proxy computes the remaining budget to make its own decision, returns it on every SUCCESSFUL call (`x-ratelimit-remaining-ai` / `-upload`), and **deliberately exposes both through CORS so the browser can read them.** Nothing in `src/` ever did. **The §9 "name the reader" defect, committed by the commit that fixed the limiter** — a value written faithfully for days with nobody consulting it.
  - **★★ AND THE READER IS THE POINT OF `O113b`, NOT A NICETY.** The recorded finding is that the limit is not *"20 documents"* but **"20 documents minus whatever else you did this hour"**, which no user can compute — so the same action succeeds or fails on invisible prior spend from a different feature. **The fix for THAT is not a bigger number; it is a number you can see.** Until now the ceiling was discoverable only by hitting it.
  - **★★★ IT REPORTS DOCUMENTS, NEVER API CALLS.** A person has a stack of invoices, not a quota of HTTP requests — and since the two buckets bind at different rates, the honest figure is **the smaller of the two**, which also means `O113b`'s *"both walls sit at 20 files"* stops being something the user has to know. **The per-document cost is the CONSERVATIVE path** (3 AI calls, the invoice path, not the statement path's 2): a budget display that overstates is worse than none, because it is trusted right up to the moment it is wrong.
  - **★★ A MISSING HEADER MEANS "WE DO NOT KNOW", NEVER ZERO.** Zero is a claim that you are out, and making it because a header was absent would be `O98` on a surface whose entire job is to be steered by. An unknown bucket is skipped rather than assumed generous, and an unknown budget says nothing at all.
  - **★ A 429 IS ALSO A READING:** it names the bucket that ran out, so that one is recorded as zero — otherwise the count sits stale at its last healthy value precisely when it matters most.
  - **★ AND BEING OUT IS NOT REPORTED AS FAILURE.** *"We've read as much as we can this hour. Your remaining 7 documents are saved and will carry on automatically."* Nothing is lost — the drain picks them up — and a sentence that omitted that would recreate `O97`'s red-wall problem in words. Four mutations, four reds.

- **★★★ C297 — O107 CAUSE 2: TYPING RE-RENDERED THE ENTIRE APPLICATION (2026-08-31).** The chat text was state on the ROOT component, so **every keystroke rebuilt the 407-key context object and re-rendered every mounted view**. That is the mechanism behind *"it feels sluggish while I use it"*, as distinct from cause 1's *"it takes a while to load"*. **It was read in exactly ONE place** (`handleChatSend`) while **every view destructured it unused** — copy-paste, paid for on every character typed.
  - **★★★ FIXED BY LOCALITY, NOT MEMOISATION, AND THE DIFFERENCE IS THE WHOLE POINT.** Memoising a 407-key context needs a complete dependency list, and a missed dependency means **silently stale numbers on an accounting screen** — the worst failure this product can have. Moving the state into the only component that reads it **has no such failure mode**: nothing else can see it, so nothing else can go stale. A cheaper fix with a strictly smaller blast radius.
  - **★★ THE TWO THINGS THAT STILL HAD TO REACH THE BOX WERE KEPT, AND NEITHER COSTS A KEYSTROKE.** A Reports button pre-fills a question — now a `chatPrefill` channel that changes on a CLICK — and it is **keyed on the prefill's own timestamp**, because a plain string would be ignored the second time and read as the button being broken. A company switch clears the box by **remounting the composer** rather than reaching into it.
  - **★★ AND THE BOX ONLY CLEARS WHEN THE SEND IS ACCEPTED.** `handleChatSend` returns false when it declines (already loading, empty after trim); clearing regardless would discard what someone had typed.
  - **★ THE MISSING IMPORT WAS CAUGHT BY THE GUARD, NOT THE BUILD.** My edit added `<ChatComposer />` without its import — Vite does not resolve free identifiers, so the build passed. `noUndefinedRefs` failed immediately. Second time this session that guard has earned its place.

- **★★★ C277 — O107 CAUSE 1 FIXED: LOADING A COMPANY WAS ELEVEN ROUND TRIPS IN SERIES, AND THE SERIAL VERSION HAD A ROBUSTNESS BUG NOBODY HAD NOTICED (2026-08-30).** `loadAllData` awaited eleven independent table reads one after another — none needs another's result — so the wall-clock cost was the **SUM of eleven latencies where it can be the MAXIMUM**: roughly 1–3 seconds of pure waiting on every load and every company switch, none of it computation. **18 awaits → 3.**
  - **★★★ AND THE SPEED IS THE LESSER HALF: A SINGLE FAILING TABLE STARVED EVERY TABLE AFTER IT.** A `documents` error meant `reconciliations`, contracts, the audit log and the AI profile **never loaded at all** — silently, underneath a comment asserting that a secondary fetch failing *"degrades gracefully"*. It did not; it degraded everything downstream of it. **`Promise.all` would have preserved that flaw exactly** (first rejection discards every sibling result), which is why it is `allSettled` and why a test forbids `Promise.all(` in this function.
  - **★ A REJECTED READ YIELDS `{}`, so every consumer takes the same "no data" branch it always took** — the change is where the waiting happens, not what any mapping does.
  - **★★ AND I INTRODUCED A BUG WHILE FIXING IT, CAUGHT BY WRITING THE TEST RATHER THAN THE CODE.** The new mid-flight company-switch guard returns from INSIDE the `try` — which still runs the `finally`, marking data "loaded" for a company the user has switched away from. **That is precisely the false emptiness the CR-18 comment at the top of the same function refuses to allow**, reintroduced twenty lines below it. The `finally` is now conditional on still being on that company.
  - Three mutations, three reds: `all` for `allSettled` · the stale-company guard removed from the `finally` · a rejected read no longer degrading to no-data.

- **★★ C275 — A REAL BANK ACCOUNT LOOKED LIKE THE ONE WE SEED, SO ONBOARDING COULD NEVER FINISH (2026-08-30). NO MIGRATION NEEDED — THE FACT WAS ALREADY RECORDED.**
  - `isPlaceholderBank` decided *"this is the row we seeded, not a real account"* from its NAME plus the absence of details — so **a genuine account actually called "Primary Checking", with no bank name, no last four and no balance yet, was indistinguishable from the seed**, and the onboarding step *"add your bank account"* could never be ticked. Nothing on screen explained why. **Editing it was the user's own assertion that the account is theirs; the code had no way to hear it.**
  - **★★ AND THE ANSWER WAS ALREADY IN THE SCHEMA: `bank_accounts` CARRIES A `set_updated_at` TRIGGER.** The seeded row is inserted once and never touched, so its two timestamps are identical; the first save moves `updated_at` and it stays moved. **A migration was drafted in my head and then not needed** — worth recording, because the reflex here is to add a column (`accounts.origin`, `documents.document_date`) and the cheaper move is to check whether the fact is already being written.
  - **★ AN UNPARSEABLE OR MISSING TIMESTAMP PAIR MEANS "WE DO NOT KNOW", NOT "IT WAS EDITED".** The other direction would declare every account real on any company whose rows predate the timestamps — **ticking off a step the person never did**, which is the same false-green family as everything else in this file. The old heuristic stays in charge when we cannot tell.
  - **★ AND A SECOND'S TOLERANCE, NOT EQUALITY:** both timestamps come from the same `now()` on insert, and a test pins that insert-time jitter is not an edit while five seconds is.

- **★★★ C274 — MY OWN UNCHECKED-WRITE GUARD PASSED FOR THE WRONG REASON (2026-08-30).** `C249` made the rule mechanical: every row-targeted `.update()` must carry `.select()` or go through the helper, and **`bank_accounts` is on the list of tables that may never be excused.** It reported clean anyway.
  - **★★ THE HOLE: THE SCAN TOOK A FIXED 5-LINE WINDOW, AND `persistBankAccounts`' UNCHECKED `.update()` SITS TWO LINES ABOVE AN UNRELATED `.insert(...).select(...)`.** The window borrowed the *insert's* `.select()` and cleared the *update*. **A guard that passes for the wrong reason is worse than no guard, because it gets cited as evidence** — and it was: C249's report said the class was closed. The chain now ends at its own semicolon.
  - **★ THE FIXED SCAN IMMEDIATELY FOUND A SECOND WRITE — ON `journal_entry_lines`, the table the guard's own comment calls unexcusable — AND IT WAS A FALSE POSITIVE WORTH KEEPING.** `persistChatRetagProject` re-reads the rows and rejects unless EVERY one carries the new value: that verifies the VALUE, where rows-affected only verifies that something was touched. **A read-back is a STRONGER check than `.select()`, not a weaker one**, so the guard learned to recognise it rather than force correct money-path code into a lint shape — and it requires the read-back AND a branch on it, because **a read-back nobody acts on is just a wasted query.**
  - **★★ AND THE WRITE ITSELF WAS THE C247 CLASS: IT SAVES THE USER'S OWN TYPING.** The account name, the bank, the last four digits, the balance — silently lost, inside a `console.warn` catch, with no reason for them to suspect it and no way to notice until the value was simply gone. It is now checked, audited, and **names which account failed**, because *"something went wrong"* does not tell someone which of their edits to redo.

- **★★ C273 — RECONCILE STOPPED ASKING FOR A STATEMENT IT ALREADY HELD (2026-08-30), AND THE GUARD THAT SHOULD HAVE CAUGHT MY BUG DID NOT.**
  - **The handover path (`reconcileOffer`) knew the lines were parsed and stored; the TAB did not.** So a CPA who navigated to Reconcile rather than following the link was asked to re-upload a file the system read days ago — **and ReconView carries its own second CSV parser to read it with**, which is the two-implementations-of-one-contract shape this codebase keeps paying for. `statementToOffer` names the stored statement and its line count; the drop zone stays for a statement we genuinely do not hold.
  - **★ IT REFUSES TO OFFER A MONTH A COMPLETED RECONCILIATION ALREADY COVERS**, using `reconciliationCoversStatement` — the same predicate the auto-complete path uses, so the two cannot drift into disagreeing about what *"already done"* means. And **the count is fetched, never assumed**: `statementOfferCopy` says less rather than inventing a number, because the number is the whole reason to trust the offer.
  - **★★★ AND THE INTERESTING PART IS A MISS: I USED `monthLabel` IN ReconView WITHOUT IMPORTING IT, THE BUILD PASSED, AND `viewsRender.test.jsx` PASSED TOO.** Vite does not resolve free identifiers, so it is a runtime crash the build cannot see — **and the render sweep missed it because the guilty call sits in a branch that only renders when a statement exists, which the render fixture has none of.** That is C246's own recorded limitation (*"catches a screen that crashes, not one that silently renders empty"*) meeting reality inside a day.
  - **`tests/missingImports.test.js` closes it, scoped narrowly on purpose:** does a component call something `src/lib` exports without importing it? A general undefined-identifier check is a linter and would drown in false positives; this asks one question with almost no room for one. **Verified by removing the exact import I forgot and watching it fail.**
  - **★★ AND IT TOOK THREE TRIES BECAUSE PROSE LIVES IN MORE PLACES THAN COMMENTS.** First version flagged `rows.push(...)` — a method, not a free identifier. Second flagged `badge (only when signed off)` inside a **JSX block comment** and `"your credit card (liability 2200)"` inside a **string literal**. **That is the sixth and seventh time a source-scanning guard in this repo has tripped on prose**, and the durable rule is now explicit: **strip block comments, line comments AND string literals before scanning** — line-prefix filtering was never going to be enough.

- **★★★ O131 DECIDED & BUILT 2026-08-30 (C272; migration `085`) — A SOLO OWNER MAY SIGN WITH AN ACKNOWLEDGEMENT.** Operator's call. **It needed a MIGRATION, not a checkbox:** `053` gates `period_signoffs` on `is_company_reviewer` and the database means it — probe (1) watched an owner refused that morning — so a UI-only fix would have rebuilt the C225 team-invites failure exactly: the whole flow present and the last step impossible.
  - **★★ IT GRANTS THE ACTION, NOT THE ROLE, AND NOT THE SEAT.** `053` step 3 met this same case once by setting `role = 'admin'` on the owner of any reviewer-less company — right for a one-time backfill, wrong now: **permanent, so a company that later hires an accountant never gets the separation back, and un-undoable since `081` blocked self-role-changes.** `085` evaluates the condition AT WRITE TIME, so **the separation returns by itself the moment a real reviewer joins, with nobody changing a setting.**
  - **★★★ AND `canAttestPeriod` WAS DELIBERATELY NOT WIDENED, WHICH WAS THE ONE-LINE FIX AND WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG.** `nav.js isReviewerSeat` derives the CPA cockpit from it, so widening it drops a client into the ten-tab reviewer workbench — the exact IA the North Star exists to keep them out of. `canSelfAttest` is its own predicate and a test pins that `canAttestPeriod('owner')` and `isReviewerSeat({role:'owner'})` both stay false.
  - **★★★ THE FLAG CANNOT LIE — THE POLICY DECIDES IT, NOT THE CLIENT.** A `CASE` in `with check` accepts a row marked `self_attested` ONLY from a solo owner and an unmarked row ONLY from a reviewer. **So the column is a property of how the write was permitted, not a description written alongside it** — §9's describe-from-the-record rule enforced by the database. Without it an owner could record an accountant's review that never happened, which is the one lie this table must never be able to tell.
  - **★ THE CONTROL LIVES ON THE OWNER'S HOME, BECAUSE AN OWNER CANNOT OPEN `ReviewView` AT ALL.** Putting it on the reviewer's screen would have been the third instance this year of a flow whose last step is unreachable.
  - **★ THE ACKNOWLEDGEMENT STATES THE TWO NON-OBVIOUS FACTS: nobody else has checked, AND the month locks.** The lock clause is not boilerplate — `078` makes the database refuse changes to a signed month, and someone who signs without knowing that finds out when a correction is rejected, which is the worst moment to learn it.
  - **★★ AND THE PANEL NOW SAYS WHO SIGNED.** *"Reviewed and signed off"* and *"you signed these yourself — no accountant has reviewed them"* are **different facts**, and the whole value of a sign-off is who stood behind it. Read off the ROW rather than inferred from current membership, so a company that later hires an accountant keeps telling the truth about its self-signed history. Four mutations, four reds — one of which had to be re-run because shell escaping meant the anchor never matched.

- **★★★ THE FIRST LIVE THROUGHPUT MEASUREMENT — 20 DOCUMENTS AN HOUR, AND THE ARITHMETIC CLOSES ON EXACTLY 60 (Red River drive, 2026-09-02).** 35 documents dropped at 04:35; **15 fully processed** (13 booked, 2 correctly held for a question) and **20 refused** by our own AI limiter, each carrying its real trailing-window reset (*"resets in about 56 minutes"* — `086` working on a live backlog for the first time).
  - **★★ EVERY NUMBER FITS, WITH NOTHING LEFT OVER: 15 documents × 3 calls = 45, leaving 15 of the 60.** Those 15 are 15 of the 20 failures getting their **classify** call through and being refused on **extract**; the last 5 were refused at classify and cost nothing. **15 + 5 = 20 failures · 45 + 15 = 60, the ceiling exactly.**
  - **★★★ SO THREE CALLS PER DOCUMENT IS NOW MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED** — `AI_CALLS_PER_DOCUMENT = 3` was a conservative guess and is the real figure, which is what makes the "documents remaining" display honest rather than optimistic.
  - **★★★ AND `O113a` IS INDEPENDENTLY CORROBORATED BY PRODUCTION DATA FOR THE FIRST TIME.** Were refusals still charged, the 20 refusals would have driven the counter to ~80 and the arithmetic could not have landed on 60. **The limiter does not charge for work it declines** — proved in a rolled-back probe when it shipped, and now proved again by a real burst that never knew it was being watched.
  - **★ THE THROUGHPUT IS THE `O97` NUMBER, MEASURED: 20 documents/hour**, so a genuine onboarding at 120–240 documents is **six to twelve hours**. Not a defect — the design. **It is also why the drain is the whole ballgame: at that rate EVERY real client's first upload hits the wall**, and the only question is whether the queue quietly finishes or drops it. On this drive it dropped 20, for the single reason recorded in `O133`.
  - **★★ AND A DIAGNOSTIC TRAP WORTH KEEPING: `rate_limit_events` CANNOT ANSWER A RETROSPECTIVE QUESTION, BY DESIGN.** `086` prunes anything outside the window on every call — *"anything outside the window is not evidence any more"* — so a burst's events are gone within the hour. **Throughput must be captured DURING a run or derived from the outcome, never queried afterwards**; a query the next morning returns one row and looks like the limiter never fired.

- **★★ THE REVIEW-SCREEN PASS — SIX FINDINGS, 2026-09-03. Recorded, not fixed.**
  - **★★★ (1) FIXED 2026-09-03 (C305) — THE SALES-TAX CONTROL TOTAL COUNTED TAX WE *PAID* AS TAX WE *OWE*, SO IT COULD ONLY EVER FAIL ON A PURCHASE-ONLY MONTH.**
    - **★★★ AND "REVENUE-BEARING" WAS ALREADY IN THE FUNCTION'S OWN COMMENT, WITH THE HELPER DEFINED ONE LINE ABOVE AND CALLED BY NOTHING.** `glIsRevenueCode` existed, was never invoked, and the comment described the filter as though it were there — the `O123` shape exactly, where a comment asserts behaviour the code does not have. **The fix is the line somebody meant to write.**
    - **★ REVENUE-BEARING IS A PROPERTY OF THE ENTRY, NOT OF THE ROW.** A taxed sale is `Dr A/R / Cr Revenue / Cr Sales Tax Payable` and arrives as several expanded rows sharing one base id, so the revenue leg may not be the row carrying the tax figure. Collected in a first pass; a mutation that checks only the current row kills 7 tests.
    - **★★ AND THE NEGATIVE CASES CARRY THE WEIGHT, because "the purchase month ties" is equally satisfied by returning 0 always** — which would silently retire the `O59` control this check exists for. Pinned: a real sale is still counted, a real month counts only the sales side (8, not 22.80), and the mis-posted-to-revenue failure still fires. Three mutations, three reds.
    - *(original finding:)* **THE SALES-TAX CONTROL TOTAL COUNTS TAX WE *PAID* AS TAX WE *OWE*, SO IT CAN ONLY EVER FAIL ON A PURCHASE-ONLY MONTH. CONFIRMED IN CODE.** `taxChargedOnInvoices` sums `import_metadata.tax_amount` over **every** live entry with no filter on direction, and compares it to `Sales Tax Payable` — a liability for tax **we collect on OUR sales**. Tax paid to a supplier is part of the expense and is owed to nobody. Live: *"tax charged on invoices is $14.80, sales tax owed is $0.00 — off by $14.80"*, on books that are correct. **`severity: high`, so it degrades the trust panel and blocks a clean sign-off** — the `079`/C291 direction, a guard firing on good work, in the check `C241` repaired without ever questioning its population. **The fix is the SIDE: only outgoing (AR) invoices charge sales tax we owe.**
  - **★★ (2) FIXED 2026-09-06 (C306) — THE DUPLICATE CARD CONTRADICTED ITSELF AND FIRED TWICE ON ONE PAIR.**
    - **★★★ ONE CAUSE FOR BOTH SYMPTOMS: THE LOOP VISITS EVERY PAIR TWICE — ONCE FROM EACH ROW — AND THE DEDUPE KEY CARRIED `cents(i.amount)`, THE AMOUNT OF WHICHEVER ROW IT ARRIVED FROM.** `findDuplicate` matches within 1%, so a pair with two different amounts produced two keys and emitted two cards, headed **$632.30** and **$629.04** — which is also why the two headlines disagreed. The key is now symmetric over the pair (both amounts, sorted), and the same defect was in the off-rhythm branch two lines up.
    - **★★ AND THE SENTENCE ASSERTED THE AMOUNTS WERE EQUAL, DIRECTLY ABOVE THE TWO THAT WERE NOT.** The detector's tolerance is 1%, so unequal is the ORDINARY case — **the copy was describing a stricter rule than the one that fired.** It reads both rows now, and keeps the single-figure wording when they genuinely match (pinned, because "name both amounts" must not become "always print two numbers", which would read as a discrepancy where there is none).
    - **★★ A SURVIVING MUTATION CORRECTED MY OWN COMMENT AGAIN.** I wrote that the numeric comparator prevents an order-dependent key; **swapping to lexicographic `.sort()` left everything green, because ANY total order is symmetric.** The sort is load-bearing, the comparator is legibility. The test that claimed to cover it now says what it actually covers — a pair straddling a digit boundary is still one card. *(C255's rule a third time: a surviving mutation is a question, and sometimes the answer is that the line could not have mattered.)*
    - **★ THREE EXISTING TESTS WENT RED, ALL CORRECTLY, AND ONE WAS FRAGILE IN A WAY WORTH FIXING RATHER THAN REPOINTING.** `recurringVendor`'s anti-vacuity check identified WHICH rule fired by matching *"within a week"* in the body — a phrase that now appears only when the amounts DIFFER, which is a property of the figures and not of the rule. It discriminates on the TITLE now. The other two pinned the literal fingerprint; **the assertion beneath one of them runs `anomalySubjectPeriod` against the new key, so the date-tail parser feeding the HIGH-blocks-sign-off gate is PROVED to still place these — not assumed.**
    - **▶ ONE-TIME CHURN, EXPECTED: open `dup:` rows carry the old fingerprint**, so they auto-resolve and re-open under the new key on the next reconcile — the `C198·3b(f3)` resurrection wall, same shape, same reason.
    - *(original finding:)* **THE DUPLICATE CARD CONTRADICTS ITSELF, AND FIRES TWICE ON ONE PAIR.** Two HIGH cards for the same Hill Country pair, one headed *"Two charges … for $632.30"* and the other *"…for $629.04"* — while both list the SAME two rows, **$629.04 and $632.30**. The detector matches within 1%; the SENTENCE asserts the amounts are equal. Neither statement is true, and the pair is emitted twice. §9 world-claim plus a fingerprint that is not idempotent across near-amounts.
  - **★★ (3) THE A/P CONTROL TOTAL IS OFF BY EXACTLY THE HELD SABINE AMOUNT.** *"open bills $27,053.03, payables balance $31,678.03 — off by $4,625.00"*, and $4,625.00 is Sabine Kitchen Equipment, one of the eight documents held for a question. So a document awaiting an ANSWER appears to reach payables while being excluded from the open-bills universe. **Needs a query before it is called a defect** — but an exact match to the cent is not a coincidence worth ignoring.
  - **✅ (4) ALREADY CLOSED BY `C302` — CONFIRMED ON THE LIVE BOOKING, NOT ASSUMED.** Gusto booked `Dr 6000 Salaries & Wages / Cr 2000 A/P`, and `couldBeCapital` returns false for `salaries_wages`, so the large-charge detector can no longer ask whether payroll is equipment. *(The account gate fixed a case the payroll-source exemption never reached, which is why gating on the ACCOUNT was the right axis.)*
  - **★★★ (3) FIXED 2026-09-06 (C309) — AN OPEN BILL IS ONE WITH AN A/P LEG, NOT ONE WHOSE DEBIT IS AN EXPENSE.**
    - **`apUnpaid` READ `isExp(i) && !paid`, DERIVING OPENNESS FROM THE ENTRY'S P&L CLASS** — the §9 anti-pattern by name (*side is the A/R-or-A/P OFFSET code on the leg, never the type*). Live: Sabine posted `Dr 1500 Fixed Assets / Cr 2000 Accounts Payable`, correct in every respect, and **a fixed-asset debit is not an expense** — so the bill was invisible to open bills while its A/P credit sat in the GL balance and `ap_tie` diverged by exactly **$4,625.00**. **Buying equipment on terms is ordinary**, so this broke for any client who finances anything.
    - **★★ ADDITIVE, DELIBERATELY: the expense rule stays and still carries the overwhelming majority.** Only the non-expense purchase whose OFFSET is A/P is added, so nothing that counted before stops counting — **the change cannot subtract from a figure people already read**, and a test pins that ordinary bills are untouched.
    - **★★★ AND IT IS THREADED TO ALL FOUR CALLERS RATHER THAN THE ONE THAT WAS FAILING.** `controlTotals` had `codes.ap` in scope and fixing only it would have made the control total count Sabine while the AI snapshot and the monthly report did not — **§12's forked-definition warning, arrived at by taking the cheap option.** `aiTools` reaches it through `ctx.getAccountByRole`, the monthly report takes it as a parameter, and a test asserts every call site passes it.
    - **★ WITHOUT A CODE IT BEHAVES EXACTLY AS BEFORE — never a guess.** `2xxx` would sweep in a **loan-financed** purchase, which is a liability and NOT an open bill; the counter-case is pinned. §4's rule (users renumber) makes the company's own code the only honest input.
    - **★ EXPANDED MULTI-LINE ROWS ARE SKIPPED**, since flatten emits several rows that can share one offset and counting them all would double the bill. Stated as the scope limit it is: a multi-line capitalized purchase still diverges, and that is visible rather than silently doubled.
    - **★★ AND A SURVIVING MUTATION REMOVED A LINE OF MINE — the third time this week.** An explicit *"and this row is not the A/P leg itself"* guard could not change any outcome: a bill's A/P leg carries the EXPENSE code as its offset, so it never reaches that test. **It read as a check and was one only in appearance** (C255's shape). Removed; the property stays pinned by the test rather than by a second line. *(One mutation also broke the module outright and produced NO verdict — recorded because §12's rule applies: a module that fails to load is neither a pass nor a fail, and must be re-run properly rather than counted.)*
    - *(original diagnosis:)* **IT IS THE §9 ANTI-PATTERN BY NAME, AND IT BREAKS FOR ANY CLIENT WHO BUYS EQUIPMENT ON TERMS.** Sabine posted `Dr 1500 Fixed Assets / Cr 2000 Accounts Payable` — correct: the freezer is capitalized and the money is owed. **But `apUnpaid = isExp(i) && payment_status !== "paid"` derives openness from the entry's P&L class**, and a fixed-asset debit is not an expense — so the bill is invisible to the open-bills list while its A/P credit sits in the GL balance, and the control total diverges by exactly the capitalized amount. **§9 already states the fix: the side is the A/R-or-A/P OFFSET code on the entry's leg, never the entry's type.** ▶ NOT FIXED — `apUnpaid` feeds aging, the dashboard and the AI snapshot, so it is its own change with its own evidence.
  - **(4, original finding:) A PAYROLL PAYMENT FLAGGED AS POSSIBLE EQUIPMENT.** *"Large charge: $5,967.00 to Gusto … may need to be capitalized"*. `O85(c)` recorded the payroll exemption and `C302` gates on the ACCOUNT — which does not help an entry that was never booked to one.
  - **★★ (5) FIXED 2026-09-06 (C307) — THE STORED REFERENCE WAS NEVER DURABLE, SO THE ENTRY WAS ALWAYS GOING TO GO "MISSING".**
    - **★★★ DETECTION RUNS ON `invoicesRef.current`, AND RIGHT AFTER A BOOKING SESSION THOSE ARE IN-SESSION OBJECTS WHOSE `id` IS `Date.now() + Math.random()`** (`buildUploadedInvoice`). That float was persisted straight into `entity_refs`, so the pointer was **valid for minutes**: on the next load the ledger is keyed by database uuid, the float matches nothing, and the card reports *"1 of 1 linked entry can no longer be found"* about a `6100 Rent & Occupancy` row sitting in the books at exactly that amount.
    - **★★ THIS IS THE `O87(v)` MECHANISM AT ITS SOURCE, AND IT COST MORE THAN A CONFUSING LINE.** The same dead-refs condition made `anomalyTouchesPeriod` under-count, and **its only consumer is the HIGH-blocks-sign-off gate** — so a month could be attested over an unresolved HIGH. `C198·3c` taught both parsers to fall back to the fingerprint's own dates, which was the right patch for rows already written; **this fixes the refs rather than the reading of them.**
    - **★ AN ID THAT RESOLVES TO NOTHING IS KEPT, NEVER DROPPED.** Dropping shrinks `total`, so a genuinely removed entry would stop being reported — **trading a visible wrong count for an invisible one**, which is the trade this whole file exists to refuse. Pinned, and mutation-killed.
    - **★ AND THE GUARD ASSERTS THE INSERT ACTUALLY CALLS IT.** A pure helper nobody invokes is the `O95` shape; removing the call kills a test rather than passing quietly.
    - *(original finding:)* **THE RENT ANOMALY'S LINKED ENTRY CANNOT BE FOUND, THOUGH IT EXISTS.** *"1 of 1 linked entry can no longer be found"* against a live `6100 Rent & Occupancy` row of exactly that amount. The `O87(v)` class — detection-time `invoice_ids` not resolving after a reload — still live.
  - **★★★ (6) FIXED 2026-09-06 (C308) — THE COMPLETENESS NET HAS NEVER RUN, ON ANY COMPANY, AND THE COPY MADE IT LOOK MOMENTARY.**
    - **`fetchIntakeRows` SELECTED `created_at` FROM A TABLE WHOSE COLUMN IS `received_at`** (`047` declares no `created_at` at all). PostgREST errors on a select naming a column that does not exist, so it returned `ok:false` **every time, for everyone** — and `completenessChecked` has been false since the fetch was written.
    - **★★★ THE COPY WAS HONEST ABOUT THE QUERY AND WRONG ABOUT THE DURATION, WHICH IS WHY IT SURVIVED.** *"We couldn't check your documents JUST NOW"* is exactly right for a network blip and exactly wrong for a permanent error — **a permanent failure wearing transient clothing is one nobody investigates.** `O98` got the hard half right (never claim the books are fine) and the easy half hid the bug for months.
    - **★★★ AND THE SECOND LAYER WOULD HAVE OUTLIVED THE FIRST, SILENTLY.** `staleIntakeRows` ages each row from `r.received_at || r.created_at || now` — **the column it actually reads was not being fetched either.** So a fix that merely made the query legal would have left every row aging from `now`, the staleness window never firing, and the net **reporting all-clear while doing nothing** — strictly worse than the error, because an error is visible.
    - **★★ A FAILED CHECK NOW SAYS WHY.** The caller records ok/not-ok and **discards `error.message`**, so this had nowhere to be seen for as long as it existed. *(Third instance of the same shape this week: `O133`'s constraint violation, `C305`'s unused helper, and now a column name — all invisible because nothing printed the reason.)*
    - **★★ THE GUARD READS THE REAL DDL.** A column name is a contract with the schema and nothing was checking it: the build cannot see inside a string, and a unit test with a hand-made fixture supplies whatever the reader asks for — the `·3a` shape, both sides agreeing while neither matches the database. **Every `select` in the module is now parsed against `047`'s `create table`**, and the DDL parse refuses to pass vacuously. Three mutations, three reds — including the SILENT one, dropping `received_at` while keeping the query legal.
    - *(original finding:)* **THE COMPLETENESS NET DID NOT RUN.** *"We couldn't check your documents just now"*. The copy is honest (it says it could not check, not that everything is fine — `O98` observed), and the check still failed to run.
  - *(Also noted, possibly deliberate: Home says "2 things look unusual", Review lists 4. If that is the owner seat muting MEDIUM per `O86(d)`, it is correct and undocumented.)*

- **★★★ THE RED RIVER LEDGER REVIEW — 13 OF 15 PAIRINGS CORRECT, AND THE QUEUE WAS WRONG IN THE MIRROR IMAGE OF THE BOOKS (2026-09-03, C302/C303/C304).** The operator challenged the screen — *"Rio Grande produce as a meal? nahhh"* — and the challenge was right and productive even though the ACCOUNT was fine. Checked against the ledger rather than the screenshot.
  - **★★★ EVERY LINE ON THAT SCREEN WAS FALSE ABOUT THE WORLD, SO THE READER HAD NOTHING TO STEER BY.** Rio Grande, Hill Country and Sysco were booked CORRECTLY to Food Cost and called meals; Corner Market went to Kitchen Supplies and was called office supplies; Bluebonnet and Lone Star went to Travel & Entertainment and were called client meals. **A linen service is not a client meal under any reading — so all seven were wrong, and NOTHING distinguished the five that were fine underneath from the two that were genuinely broken.**
    - **★★ A CORRECTION TO MY OWN FIRST WRITE-UP, KEPT BECAUSE THE SLIP IS THE LESSON.** I recorded the queue as *"accurate exactly where the books were wrong"* — meaning the sentence MATCHED THE ACCOUNT it booked to. That is INTERNAL CONSISTENCY, and I wrote it as *honest*, which reads as TRUE. **A description can agree perfectly with the record and still be false about the world**, and calling that honesty is how a §9 fix gets marked complete while the sentence a person reads is still wrong. The operator caught it immediately: *"queue honest when it says a client meal for Bluebonnet? That isn't accurate at all is it?"*
    - **★★★ AND THE CORRECTED READING IS THE STRONGER FINDING: NOT "MISLEADING IN BOTH DIRECTIONS" BUT "UNIFORMLY WRONG, THEREFORE UNUSABLE AS A SIGNAL".** The operator's reaction — *"I think there are a bunch of wrong ones in here"* — was the CORRECT response to that screen, and it was right about the count and unable to be right about which. Separating the five from the two took a SQL query. **That is what a divergent description costs: not confusion about one line, but the loss of the only audit a client can perform.**
  - **★★ THAT IS THE COST OF THE §9 CLASS STATED PRECISELY, AND IT IS NOT "THE COPY IS SLOPPY": A DESCRIPTION THAT DIVERGES FROM THE RECORD DESTROYS THE READER'S ABILITY TO AUDIT BY READING.** Every wrong sentence spends attention on correct work, and every accidentally-right one buys cover for a real defect. The only way to tell the two apart was a SQL client.
  - **THE TRAP WAS BEATEN: Alamo Ice & Beverage → `5020 Beverage Cost`, 3 for 3.** CO2 tank exchanges and fountain syrup read like equipment and are drinks inventory; that specimen was planted from the `O88` Alamo Fire finding and did not reproduce.
  - **★★ AND THE ONE REMAINING MISS WAS A CHART GAP, NOT A CATEGORISER ERROR — Tier 1 #6's argument on a live document.** A Texas **mixed beverage gross receipts tax** is a tax on the restaurant's OWN sales and a real operating expense. The chart holds Payroll Tax, Income Tax and (real-estate template only) Property Taxes, **and nothing for anything else** — so it landed in Licenses & Permits, the nearest thing that existed. **The categorizer picked second because there was no first.** `6910` is now **"Taxes & Licenses"**, matching **IRS Schedule C line 23**, so a gross receipts tax, a franchise tax and a liquor license sit where a preparer looks for all three. Dedupe is by CODE, so existing companies keep their own name and gain no duplicate — new companies only, and a rename is theirs.

- **★★★ `O133` — THE DURABLE-FIRST INTAKE HAS NEVER ONCE STORED A FILE (found live, mid-drive, 2026-09-01; fixed C300).**
  - **`documents.document_type` IS `NOT NULL DEFAULT 'other'` UNDER A CHECK ALLOWING SEVEN VALUES, AND O97 STEP 1 PASSED THE LITERAL `"pending"`.** Every durable-first insert was rejected, the storage upload rolled back, nothing was written, and **`document_intake.document_id` — the exact field `planDrain` requires to consider a row drainable — stayed null.**
  - **★★★ THE DRAIN'S INPUT WAS EMPTY FOR A DIFFERENT REASON THAN THE ONE THIS FILE RECORDS.** §11 says the drain is *"forward-looking only"* and waits for documents to arrive under the new ordering. **They never arrived, because the write that creates them always failed** — so a note explaining an empty queue was describing the wrong cause, and looked like the situation was understood.
  - **★★ FOUR DEFECTS IN ONE FEATURE, AND THE SECOND IS THE ONE WITH A LESSON:** the placeholder violated the constraint · **the caller claimed durability from a truthy return** — `storeDocument` hands back an in-session float on failure, so `if (durableDocId)` was true either way and the intake row was stamped *"file stored — safe to close the tab"* · the repairing stamp wrote the classifier's RAW vocabulary (`"qbo"` is not an allowed value — **the same defect on the line meant to fix it**) · the dedup early-return could never correct a placeholder row.
  - **★★★ THE GUARD FOR (2) ALREADY EXISTED, WAS COMMENTED, AND WAS NOT ADOPTED.** Both bank-statement callers test the return with an inline uuid regex and say why — *"only a real uuid; the in-session fallback id isn't a documents FK"*. **The NEWEST caller, whose entire purpose is durability, is the one that skipped it.** One shared `isDurableDocId` now: three copies of a predicate is precisely the shape that lets the third site omit it.
  - **★★★ AND THE TEST THAT PINNED THIS PASSED THE WHOLE TIME. It asserted the writer emits the literal `"pending"` and never asked whether the COLUMN would accept it** — the `·3a` extractor-and-fixture shape, in a test that reads like coverage. **The doctrine it violated was already written one file over**, in `docLibrary.js`: *"a document we cannot label is still a document we must keep, and losing the file to protect a taxonomy would be the wrong trade."* That is exactly the trade that was happening on every upload.
  - **★ A SURVIVING MUTATION CORRECTED THE CODE'S OWN COMMENT.** The line described as the downgrade guard **cannot affect correctness** — the test below it already refuses that case. What it actually prevents is a no-op write on every unclassifiable document, and it is pinned for that instead. *(C255's lesson again: a surviving mutation is a question, and sometimes the answer is that the line could never have mattered.)*

- **★★ `O132` — THE OPENING BALANCE IS ASKED FOR ONE STEP BEFORE THE DOCUMENT THAT ANSWERS IT (2026-09-01). FOUND BY WALKING A NEW COMPANY END TO END, NOT BY READING CODE.**
  - **EVERY PART WORKS; ONLY THE SEQUENCE IS WRONG.** `deriveStatementOpening` recovers the opening from the first row's running balance less its amount, **prefers the PRINTED figure when the statement states one and flags disagreement rather than silently choosing**, and `shouldProposeOpening` raises a confirm card in Bank Import when no opening exists for that account. **The checklist runs opening balances at step 3 and the first upload at step 4**, so the statement that would derive the number arrives after the person has been asked to invent it.
  - **★★ AND TYPING FIRST IS NOT MERELY REDUNDANT, IT COSTS SOMETHING — which is what lifts this above an ordering nit.** `resolveAdoptedBalance` returns `mismatch` when a typed balance disagrees with the statement's ending and **deliberately leaves the typed value alone**. That refusal is CORRECT — silently adjusting someone's stated cash position is exactly the auto-adjust this product refuses everywhere else — **but it means the number they were asked to guess becomes a reconciliation question instead of a starting point.**
  - **★ THE STEP'S OWN HINT ALREADY SAYS *"We'll pull your starting cash from a bank statement"*.** So the copy knows the better path exists and the ordering points away from it — **the same shape as a guard whose input is always empty: the feature is present, correct, and structurally hard to reach.**
  - **▶ NOT FIXED. The repair is ORDER, not mechanism** — let step 3 be satisfied BY step 4, or say plainly that uploading a statement first fills it in. Recorded rather than fixed because changing onboarding's sequence is a product decision, and the machinery it would route to is already built and tested.

- **★★ O131 — A SOLO SIGNUP'S BOOKS CAN NEVER BE SIGNED OFF, AND THE SCREEN PROMISED OTHERWISE FOREVER (2026-08-30, C271). THE PRODUCT DECISION IS STILL THE OPERATOR'S; THE DISHONEST SENTENCE IS NOT.** An owner deliberately cannot attest their own books — the separation is the point when an accountant is involved, and the database enforces it. **But one person holds one role**, so a solo company has nobody who ever can, and the trust panel said *"Awaiting your accountant's sign-off"* about a person who does not exist.
  - **★★ THE FIX IS THE PART THAT IS TRUE UNDER EVERY OPTION ON THE TABLE, WHICH IS WHY IT COULD SHIP BEFORE THE DECISION.** Require an accountant, or let a solo owner attest with an acknowledgement — **under both, promising a review that cannot arrive is wrong.** The line now states the position and the ONE thing that changes it, and **deliberately does not imply the books are wrong: nobody has reviewed them is a different fact**, and conflating the two would frighten someone whose books are fine.
  - **★★ THE FAILURE DIRECTION IS THE WHOLE REASON THIS IS NOT ONE LINE. `hasAttester` DEFAULTS TRUE, AND A FAILED MEMBERSHIP QUERY KEEPS THE OLD SENTENCE.** An errored query returns no rows, which is **indistinguishable from a company with no accountant** — getting it backwards would tell an accountant-led client that nobody is reviewing their books. `O98`'s rule, applied to a membership read: an empty result may only be reported as an empty result when the query is known to have RUN. A test pins the caller's error check, not just the predicate.
  - **★ AN UNACCEPTED INVITE IS NOT A PERSON.** Counting one would put the same false promise back with an extra step — the screen would wait on an accountant who never clicked the link. Pinned.
  - **★ AND THE NEGATIVE CASE IS PINNED BESIDE IT: an accountant-led company still reads *"Awaiting your accountant's sign-off"*.** Without that, "the solo sentence changed" is equally satisfied by deleting the sentence for everyone. **What IS signed stays a fact even when no attester remains** — a company whose accountant has left has still genuinely had those months reviewed.
  - **★ A MUTATION'S `sed` FAILED SILENTLY AND ITS "PASS" WAS MEANINGLESS.** The grep count of 0 caught it; re-run properly, it killed 3 tests. **Confirm the file actually changed before believing a mutation survived** — a mutation that never applied looks exactly like a mutation the tests withstood.

- **★★ THE TWO RAILS' NAME RULES — INSTRUMENTED, NOT CHANGED (2026-08-30, C270).** The bank rail accepts a party when either normalised name CONTAINS the other; the invoice rail demands exact equality. The roadmap's instruction was explicit and right: *tighten toward exact, never loosen — and not as a rider on a bug fix, because that path has been live and correct for eight drives and changing it needs its own evidence.*
  - **★★ SO NOTHING CHANGED. `nameMatchKind` IS THE SAME PREDICATE, SPLIT SO THE KIND IS REPORTABLE** — a test asserts `kind !== NONE` agrees with the old inline expression on **every ordered pair of a 15-name corpus**, so the refactor cannot have quietly become a behaviour change. Every deterministic match now carries `name_match`, and a drive prints the census.
  - **★★★ AND THE RISK IS NARROWER THAN THE RULE READS, WHICH IS ITSELF THE FIRST PIECE OF EVIDENCE: THE NAME TEST ONLY EVER SEES CANDIDATES THAT ALREADY MATCHED THE AMOUNT TO THE CENT, ON THE SAME SIDE.** A wrong merge therefore needs three coincidences at once — two vendors whose names contain one another, an open item of the wrong one at the identical amount, and the same A/R-or-A/P side. **That is not "the loose rule is fine"; it is a precise statement of WHERE IT BITES, and it points at one population: flat-fee recurring vendors, whose amounts are constant and identical by construction (`O117`/`O127`).** If this rule has ever merged anything wrongly, that is where.
  - **★ THE CENSUS ANSWERS THE QUESTION NOBODY COULD ANSWER: IS THE LOOSE RULE DOING ANY WORK AT ALL?** A drive returning `substring: 0` makes tightening free and the decision trivial; a non-zero count turns each case into a thing to look at by hand. **Neither could be said before, which is why the decision had sat unscheduled.**
  - **`substringOnlyPairs` is the standing blast-radius census** — every pair of names the loose rule would merge and the strict one would not — and its test asserts it names the exact pairs `vendorIdentity.test.js` already forbids merging (SYSCO / SYSCO FUEL, Lone Star / Lone Star Restaurant Supply) **and that it manufactures no risk where none exists.** The normaliser is INJECTED rather than imported, so the answer is about the rule actually in use rather than a copy of it that has since drifted.

- **★★★ O97 CLOSED 2026-08-30 (C269) — THE QUEUE WAS CALLING SAFE WORK BROKEN, AND THE FLAG THAT SAID OTHERWISE WAS ALREADY ON THE ROW.** Steps 1 and 2 had landed: the bytes are stored BEFORE the first AI call, the upload catch reads `classifyAIFailure`, marks the intake `failed` so the drain retries it, stamps `transient: true`, and writes the honest sentence — *"We've hit our own hourly limit… Everything you sent is saved and will carry on automatically — there's nothing to re-send."*
  - **★★ AND THE TILE RENDERED THAT SENTENCE IN RED, UNDER A ⚠, INSIDE A RED BORDER, BESIDE A CHIP READING "Error", WITH "Clear ×" OFFERED OVER IT. FIVE SIGNALS SAYING BROKEN WRAPPED AROUND ONE SENTENCE SAYING FINE.** Nobody reads the sentence; they read the colour. **That is why correct copy did not save the August drive** — the operator's *"200 files in, 20 succeed, 180 turn red, and the owner concludes the product is broken"* was literally true with the honest text already on screen.
  - **★★ IT IS THE §9 DESCRIBE-FROM-THE-RECORD RULE ONE LAYER OUT: THE SENTENCE WAS RIGHT AND THE STYLING WAS THE PARALLEL DESCRIPTION THAT DIVERGED.** Every prior instance had a STRING computed from the intent instead of the outcome. Here five independent style expressions each re-decided what `status === "error"` meant, and **`item.transient` — the one field that distinguishes *waiting* from *broken* — was read by none of them.** Five readings is how they came to disagree; `queueItemTone` is now the single one.
  - **★ RED IS RESERVED FOR WHAT A PERSON CAN ACT ON.** A transient failure is our own budget and the drain clears it with no human involved, so it gets ⏳, muted text and *"Waiting its turn"*. **The chip deliberately does not say "Retrying"** — we are not retrying now, and inviting someone to watch for a change that arrives in forty minutes is its own small lie.
  - **★ THE NEGATIVE CASE IS PINNED BESIDE THE POSITIVE, because "waiting is not red" is equally satisfied by switching red off.** An unreadable file still reads ERROR, still shows ⚠, still says *"Needs a look"*; and **a failure carrying NO `transient` flag is treated as BROKEN, not as waiting** — the conservative direction, since a mislabelled waiting row sits there looking fine while nothing ever collects it. Four mutations, four reds, each confirmed to have changed the file first.
  - **★ AND AN EXISTING TEST WENT RED FOR THE RIGHT REASON: C198·2b PINNED THE CHIP'S LITERAL EXPRESSION IN THE VIEW, AND THE LOGIC MOVED.** Re-pointed at `queueItemChip` rather than deleted — the guarantee it protects (a pipeline throw reads *"Needs a look"*, never *"Done"*) is unchanged, and it now additionally asserts that a pipeline throw is ERROR and not WAITING. **A test that fails because its subject moved is a test doing its job; the failure mode to avoid is quietly dropping the assertion with it.**

- **★★ THE `078` CALLER SWEEP — COMPLETE 2026-08-30. FOUR FINDS, AND THE RULE PAID FOR ITSELF FOUR TIMES.** The rule (§6): *when adding a refusal, grep every caller of the thing it now refuses.* A new refusal does not merely add a behaviour — it **retroactively changes every path that used to be allowed**, and each of those paths was written against the old permission.
  - **C235 — the QuickBooks undo** announced success without checking, and counted rows stored at import time rather than rows actually removed. `078` turned that from harmless into dangerous: an import covering a signed month can no longer be undone, so it would have reported *"47 entries removed ✓"*, marked the import undone, and left all 47 in the books with no way to retry.
  - **C236 — `079`, the guard blocking a change that moves no money.** `078` gave ENTRIES column-level discrimination and LINES none, so retagging a project on a signed month's entry was refused. **This is the failure `078`'s own check (5) existed to catch, and it varied the PERIOD where it needed to vary the COLUMN.** For a guard, *"it blocks too much"* is the dangerous direction — it breaks ordinary work and only surfaces when someone tries to do their job.
  - **★ C237 — A BACKGROUND JOB THAT COULD RAISE AN INTERACTIVE DIALOG.** `persistMultiLineEntry` holds a signed-period entry by popping a confirmation — right for a person who just dropped a document into a closed month. **`autoPostDepreciation` is the only caller with nobody watching**, and it runs on every company load: a depreciation row in a signed month would have asked for a decision about something the user never did, got no answer, left the row `pending`, and repeated forever. `background: true` audits and returns; the caller counts the row as incomplete. **The flag is opt-in and a test pins that exactly ONE call site passes it** — defaulting it would have been the same bug pointed the other way, and quieter.
  - **★★ C238 — THE UNDO BUTTONS, AND THEY WERE HIDING INSIDE THE FUNCTION WE HAD JUST FIXED.** `softDeleteInvoices` was hardened yesterday (`O124(c)`) to check whether the delete landed. **The Undo handler ten lines below it wrote, ignored the result, and said "Restored ✓".** Contracts the same, in a loop nobody read. **A FAILED UNDO IS INVISIBLE BY CONSTRUCTION** — local state puts the rows back on screen either way, so success and failure render identically until the next reload takes them away, which is the worst order to learn it in. Both now write FIRST and paint after: **the screen follows the database, not the click.**
  - **★ AND A MUTATION ESCAPED THE FIRST VERSION OF ONE TEST, WHICH IS THE METHOD POINT.** Asserting `checkedIdsUpdate(` APPEARS survived leaving the call behind `if (false)`. **A checked write whose answer nobody reads is exactly the unchecked write it replaced, and a source scan cannot see reachability** — so the assertion has to pin the CONSUMPTION (`const r = await …` and `if (!r.ok)`), not the call. Sibling of the `O123` guard that was correct only by virtue of something upstream.
  - **★ A TEST THAT FAILED AGAINST CORRECT CODE, KEPT AS THE LOUD VERSION OF A QUIET FAILURE.** C237's first draft sliced `App.jsx` from `persistMultiLineEntry` to `const ensureAccount` — which appears EARLIER as a nested helper, so `indexOf` returned a position before the start and the slice was empty. Three tests failed against correct code. **The quiet version of that mistake is a slice that happens to be non-empty and contains none of what is asserted** — which passes forever. An end anchor must be searched from the start offset.

- **PRODUCT IDEAS (post-launch; recorded from the O85 drive — both now carried on ROADMAP §0 TIER 3).**
  - **PAYROLL AUTO-POST** *(SUPERSEDED — promoted to C198-adjacent by O86 finding (b); kept for the rationale)*. A payroll register dropped on the home zone should **auto-post its standard entry** (with an undo window) rather than parking on a confirm card. The debit/credit confirmation card is a **CPA-only** surface once the IA collapses (North Star Phase 2) — the owner should see an outcome, not a journal entry to approve. Pairs with the payroll PDF-ingestion gap (§11 O84 finding 3).
  - **★ OWNER NUDGE CHANNEL (differentiator-class; Tier 3).** Route exceptions and clarifying questions to the owner **where they already are** — Slack / SMS / email — e.g. *"What was this $155 charge at X yesterday?"* — and let the **answer teach the vendor mapping** (`learnFromCorrection`, so the reply is worth something permanently). The exception ROUTING already exists (C186's queue); this is purely a **delivery-channel** feature on top of it. Turns the trust layer from a screen the client must visit into a conversation that finds them, and it feeds the same learning loop as the teach-in/entity-profiles work.

- **★ SECURITY & DOCUMENT-HANDLING AUDIT — client peace-of-mind + tenant integrity. ✅ TIER 1 COMPLETE (2026-08-09, ROADMAP §0 TIER 1 #11); TIER 2 UNBLOCKED; the SECURITY LADDER (s1–s6) is the live work.** *(Opened 2026-08-08: the **first candidate third-party user attempted signup that day**. Until now every company in the database has been the operator's own; the tenant wall has never had to hold against a stranger, and "it has never been tested by an adversary" is not the same claim as "it holds.")* Two deliverables, strictly ordered — **the story is written from the audit's findings, never ahead of them.**

  - **★ TIER 1 — RLS / TENANT-WALL AUDIT (ROADMAP §0 TIER 1, launch-blocking). MUST COMPLETE BEFORE ANY REAL THIRD-PARTY ACCOUNT HOLDS REAL DATA.** A **read-only verification session, architect-driven via SQL** — no code changes expected, and none welcome mid-audit. Four checks:
    1. **Every table carrying `company_id` has RLS enabled AND policies that actually enforce it.** Enumerate from `pg_policies` / `pg_tables` and **join against every table with a `company_id` column** — the deliverable is the **set difference**: any company-scoped table with no policy, or with RLS off, is a finding. **This has happened before, twice**: `accounts` shipped company-scoped with no RLS at all (caught by the OWASP pass, fixed in `023` — see §3), and `048_rls_exposed_tables.sql` exists because a later sweep found more. §8's "new tenant table → migration with RLS + the four policies" is a convention, and a convention is not an enforcement mechanism.
    2. **Storage.** Buckets **private** (the `documents` bucket holds bank statements and receipts); object paths **company-scoped**; download URLs **signed and expiring**, never permanent public URLs. A private bucket fronted by a permanent URL is a public bucket with extra steps.
    3. **Key hygiene.** The anon key being public is safe **ONLY IF (1) passes** — it is not independently safe, it is safe *because* RLS is the boundary (§3). Verify **no service-role key is shipped client-side** (grep the built bundle, not just the source — `dist/` is what ships) and that `ai-proxy` remains the only holder of `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`.
    4. **Cross-tenant probe — the actual proof.** Confirm a session scoped to one company **cannot read another's rows or files**. The **five-company census** recorded above (`3a704760` Franklin Ave Pizza, `c787582f` Test Corp ONE, `24d5e576` Northwind Studio, `3a4a6b3c` Shadow Test Co, `66fa0cc2`) makes this **concretely testable today** — real distinct tenants, known row counts, no fixture to build first.
    **Any gap found = an immediate fix commit BEFORE onboarding anyone.** ⚠️ **Run the audit OUTSIDE the app.** The Security dashboard's `security_check` RPC is the application grading its own homework, and it is `SECURITY DEFINER` — a passing self-report is exactly what a missing policy would also produce. Query `pg_policies` directly. *(Same lesson as C195(7): a guard whose input is always empty is indistinguishable from a clean queue — assert the mechanism FIRES, don't accept "nothing outstanding".)* Note also the **deliberate platform-admin bypass** (§3 Option A): `is_company_member` returns true for `PLATFORM_ADMIN_EMAILS`, so the audit must probe as a **normal user of one tenant**, never as the operator — the operator's own account cannot fail this test. *(Same trap that blocked the DB-layer sign-off proof below.)*

  - **TIER 2 — THE CLIENT-FACING SECURITY STORY (ROADMAP §0 TIER 2; write AFTER Tier 1 verifies, never before).** The truthful two-sentence answer to **"where does my bank statement go?"** — transit encryption, at-rest encryption, access scoping — **plus the part that answer usually omits: the AI pipeline is a data path.** Per §5 the document's text/base64 travels client → Supabase Edge Function (`ai-proxy`) → `api.anthropic.com`, so the honest answer names **Anthropic as a sub-processor** and must state **retention terms, whether we are on a zero-retention tier, and whether client data trains models — with the vendor's answer IN WRITING**, not inferred from a docs page. Three more, each currently unanswerable:
    - **Deletion/retention honesty.** The architecture is **soft delete** (§7: `deleted_at`/`deleted_by`, deliberately, for recoverability and audit trail). So **"delete" does not mean purged** — and a retention promise must be **defined AND implemented** before it is quoted to anyone. Quoting a purge we don't perform is the same class of defect as the O86 surfaces that reported work they hadn't done, with legal consequences instead of a lost afternoon.
    - **Audit-log completeness — VIEWED, not just CHANGED.** `logAudit`/`logAI` record **mutations** (§8). **Reads are not logged at all**, so *"who has looked at my books?"* has no answer today. Sharpest edge: **Support Mode**. Platform-admin *actions* are attributed (`Platform Admin - <email>`), but platform-admin *views* are not recorded anywhere — and read access to their books by someone at the vendor is precisely what a client would most want logged. Scope a read-audit for at minimum support-mode access before making any access-transparency claim.
    - **SOC 2 — noted as a company milestone, NOT scheduled.** Recorded so it stops being re-raised as though it were a task; it is a program with an auditor and a calendar, and nothing above depends on it.

  - **★★ AUDIT COMPLETE — TIER 1 CLOSED (2026-08-08/09). THE WALLS HOLD, AND ONE OF THEM DIDN'T.** *(Supersedes the Tier 1 spec above, which stays as the record of what was checked.)*
    - **RLS coverage — VERIFIED from live `pg_policies`.** **All 33 tables carrying `company_id` have RLS enabled AND policies enforcing it.** The set difference the spec asked for came back **empty** — no company-scoped table is unprotected.
    - **Storage — VERIFIED.** Bucket **private**, object paths **folder-scoped by company**, membership-checked policies on **all four commands**.
    - **★ FINDING (real, and the reason the audit was worth running) — the journal family carried TWO OR-combined permissive policy generations.** Postgres ORs permissive policies, so **the loosest wins** and every role gate and guard in the older generation had been a **dead letter** since migration `001` landed beside it — a `viewer` had full write access to the general ledger. **Second defect in the same place:** the older `UPDATE` policies had `USING` but **no `WITH CHECK`**, so the post-update row was never re-validated and **a member could reassign a row to another tenant's `company_id`** — a hole that was *masked* by the very broad policies the dedup removes, so a naive dedup would have opened it.
    - **FIXED — `061_je_policy_dedup.sql` APPLIED and verified live (C199 `d612964`).** A single generation governs each of the six ledger tables, **the stricter one survives**, **`WITH CHECK` on all six updates**, and writes route through **`is_company_writer()` (SECURITY DEFINER)** — the role gate **OR** platform admin, preserving Support Mode rather than collaterally revoking it.
    - **★ INVARIANT ESTABLISHED — no ledger table carries two permissive policies for one command.** This is the durable rule, not the one-time cleanup: it is checkable in a single query (group `pg_policies` by `tablename, cmd` having `count(*) > 1`) and **every future policy migration must leave it true**. *(Corollary worth internalising: in RLS, adding a policy can only ever LOOSEN a table. A strict policy beside a broad one is not a restriction — it is a comment.)*
    - **Known remaining duplicates — deliberately excluded, not missed.** `audit_log` / `subscriptions` (old SELECTs are **strict subsets** of the surviving ones → dropping them is a pure no-op cleanup); `unknown_documents` (**a real behaviour change** — its older generation is role-gated, so deduping would start rejecting uploads by a `viewer` → **queued as `062`**); `users` (the own-OR-teammates pair is **intentional**, not a generation collision).
      - **DECLINED 2026-08-11 — `audit_log` / `subscriptions` duplicate SELECTs stay. No row, no work.** Reason: they are a **provable no-op** — the old policies are strict SUBSETS of the surviving ones, so they can neither widen access nor change a result; the §11 invariant they nominally offend ("no ledger table carries two permissive policies for one command") is about POLICY GENERATIONS DIVERGING, and these do not diverge. A migration to drop them would be pure churn against a live database for zero behavioural gain. **Recorded as a decision so the orphan sweep stops re-discovering them as unanchored work** — declining is an answer; silence is what makes something an orphan.
    - **★ LIVE CROSS-TENANT PROBE — PASSED (2026-08-09).** A **non-platform-admin** account (admin of one test company only) **sees only its own company**: every surface renders empty, **no cross-tenant rows anywhere**. This is the proof the spec insisted on — run as a normal user of one tenant, not as the operator, whose platform-admin bypass means their account *cannot* fail the test. **Timing matters: the probe PREDATES any real data exposure** — the first real third-party account (**Basement Ballin Studios**, owner `maxabdella6@`) now exists, and the wall was proven before it held anyone else's books.
